# BIOL 130 — Modules 3–6 Flashcards

*Dr. Vivian Dayeh · Introductory Cell Biology · Fall 2026 · University of Waterloo*

Suneung-style analytical questions in four selectable-answer formats — 보기 (bogi) combination, matching, true/false, and multiple choice — built from the Module 3 (pp. 32–51), Module 4 (pp. 52–67), Module 5 (pp. 68–84) and Module 6 (pp. 85–94) lecture notes. Questions test reasoning, not recall: most put a concept to work in an experiment, a real-world case (drugs, diseases, lab techniques), or a diagram, and the final section connects these modules with each other and with Modules 1–2. For the interactive, auto-graded version — now covering Modules 1–6, with the diagrams drawn in — use the [study page](https://claude.ai/artifact/6hd61Bm4Fyc2ZkqwWr6Ctm); Modules 1–2 have their own doc in this Project. Items marked **◇ Diagram** show a figure in the interactive and Anki versions; here, each figure is described in one line of text instead, so you can sketch it yourself.

## Module 3 — Cells and Membranes

### Membrane Structure & Fluidity

**Q1. [보기 Combination · 3점] Stripping a membrane, one treatment at a time**

Intact cells like the one in the diagram are treated in two separate experiments. Which statements in <보기> are correct?

**◇ Diagram — The fluid mosaic model: what sits where in the membrane.** Cross-section of a plasma membrane: a phospholipid bilayer; a channel protein spanning the whole bilayer; a peripheral protein sitting on the cytoplasmic face; cholesterol in both layers, its small polar end level with the phospholipid heads and its body among the tails; and sugar chains of a glycolipid and a glycoprotein on the extracellular side.

> **(가)** An enzyme that cuts sugar chains is added to the fluid outside the cells. It cannot cross the plasma membrane.
>
> **(나)** A salt wash that disrupts only noncovalent (ionic and hydrogen-bond) attachments is applied; the bilayer itself stays intact.

**<보기>**
- ㄱ. In (가), both the glycolipid's and the glycoprotein's sugar chains can be cut, because membrane carbohydrates all face the extracellular fluid.
- ㄴ. The channel protein counts as a peripheral protein of the cytoplasmic face, because part of it is exposed to the cytoplasm.
- ㄷ. In (나), the peripheral protein would be released, but the channel protein would stay in the membrane.

① ㄱ　 ② ㄷ　 ③ ㄱ, ㄴ　 ④ ㄱ, ㄷ　 ⑤ ㄴ, ㄷ

**Answer: ④ ㄱ, ㄷ**
- ㄱ — **O** — Sugars are attached only on the extracellular face — exactly where the impermeable enzyme is — so both kinds are exposed to it.
- ㄴ — **X** — A protein that spans the bilayer is integral (transmembrane) even though its ends are exposed on both faces; peripheral proteins sit on one face and are never embedded.
- ㄷ — **O** — Peripheral proteins are held to one face by noncovalent bonds; the channel is integral, embedded across the bilayer, so it stays unless the bilayer itself is dissolved.

*Concepts linked:* Membrane carbohydrates face outward · Integral vs peripheral proteins · Fluid mosaic model

---

**Q2. [보기 Combination · 3점] Membranes built for the cold**

Two related fish species keep their membranes at a workable fluidity in very different waters. Using the diagram, which statements in <보기> are correct?

**◇ Diagram — Four membrane patches: tail length, double bonds, cholesterol.** Four membrane patches drawn side by side: phospholipids with long, straight saturated tails; phospholipids with short, straight saturated tails; phospholipids whose unsaturated tails are kinked at C=C double bonds and sit further apart; and phospholipids with cholesterol molecules wedged between them.

> **(가)** Species P lives in near-freezing Arctic water.
>
> **(나)** Species Q lives in warm tropical water.

**<보기>**
- ㄱ. Q could keep its membranes from becoming too fluid in warm water by switching to shorter fatty acid tails.
- ㄴ. P's membrane phospholipids would be expected to carry a higher proportion of unsaturated fatty acid tails than Q's.
- ㄷ. Cholesterol in these fish membranes would restrain lipid movement in warmth and hinder tight packing in cold, buffering fluidity in both species.

① ㄱ　 ② ㄴ　 ③ ㄱ, ㄴ　 ④ ㄴ, ㄷ　 ⑤ ㄱ, ㄴ, ㄷ

**Answer: ④ ㄴ, ㄷ**
- ㄱ — **X** — Shorter tails touch less and make a membrane more fluid; Q would need longer (and more saturated) tails to stiffen its membranes.
- ㄴ — **O** — C=C kinks keep tails from packing tightly, which keeps a membrane fluid in the cold.
- ㄷ — **O** — Cholesterol is a fluidity buffer: its rigid rings restrain movement at high temperature, and its bulk blocks tight packing at low temperature.

*Concepts linked:* Fluidity: tail length · double bonds · cholesterol · Real-world: temperature adaptation

---

**Q3. [보기 Combination · 2점] Rafts in a fluid sea**

Read (가) and (나). Which statements in <보기> are correct?

> **(가)** Certain lipids and proteins gather in discrete patches of the plasma membrane called lipid rafts.
>
> **(나)** The fluid mosaic model describes membrane components as mobile and able to come together in transient interactions.

**<보기>**
- ㄱ. Lipid rafts contradict the fluid mosaic model, since components in a raft can no longer move.
- ㄴ. 'Mosaic' refers to the mixture of lipids, proteins, and carbohydrates that makes up the membrane.
- ㄷ. Rafts hold together because the lipids in them become covalently bonded to one another.

① ㄴ　 ② ㄷ　 ③ ㄱ, ㄴ　 ④ ㄱ, ㄷ　 ⑤ ㄱ, ㄴ, ㄷ

**Answer: ① ㄴ**
- ㄱ — **X** — Rafts are dynamic microdomains within a fluid membrane; the model explicitly allows components to come together transiently.
- ㄴ — **O** — The core is a lipid bilayer, with proteins and carbohydrates forming a mixed pattern — a mosaic.
- ㄷ — **X** — Raft components associate through weak, transient interactions in a fluid membrane — no covalent links; rafts act as platforms for cell processes.

*Concepts linked:* Lipid rafts · Fluid mosaic model · Membrane components

**Q4. [Matching · 4점] Seven jobs of a membrane**

Match each observation to the membrane function it best demonstrates.

**Observation**
A. The cell digests macromolecules inside lysosomes while building proteins in the surrounding cytosol
B. Ions such as Na+ cannot cross the lipid bilayer on their own, so gradients across it can be maintained
C. Glucose enters through a specific transporter protein
D. Enzymes of one pathway are held side by side on a membrane surface
E. A protein hormone binds the outside of a cell and triggers changes inside without entering
F. Cells in a tissue attach to and communicate with their neighbours
G. Thylakoid membranes convert light energy into chemical energy

**Membrane function**
1. Compartmentalization
2. Responding to external signals (signal transduction)
3. Selectively permeable barrier
4. Transporting solutes
5. Energy transduction
6. Intercellular interaction
7. Scaffold for biochemical activities

**Correct matches:**
- A = The cell digests macromolecules inside lysosomes while building proteins in the surrounding cytosol → Compartmentalization
- B = Ions such as Na+ cannot cross the lipid bilayer on their own, so gradients across it can be maintained → Selectively permeable barrier
- C = Glucose enters through a specific transporter protein → Transporting solutes
- D = Enzymes of one pathway are held side by side on a membrane surface → Scaffold for biochemical activities
- E = A protein hormone binds the outside of a cell and triggers changes inside without entering → Responding to external signals (signal transduction)
- F = Cells in a tissue attach to and communicate with their neighbours → Intercellular interaction
- G = Thylakoid membranes convert light energy into chemical energy → Energy transduction

All seven follow from one structure: a selectively permeable bilayer (barrier, compartments) studded with proteins (transporters, scaffolds, receptors, junctions) — plus, in membranes like the thylakoid membrane, energy-converting machinery.

*Concepts linked:* Membrane functions · Compartmentalization · Signal transduction · Energy transduction

**Q5. [True / False · 1점]**

> The detergent SDS has a single hydrocarbon tail attached to a large, charged head group, so like a phospholipid it is amphipathic.

**◇ Diagram — Phospholipid shape decides what forms in water.** Phospholipids with one tail and a large head pack into a micelle with their tails in the core; phospholipids with two tails and a smaller head form a bilayer sheet with the tails sandwiched inside, which closes into a liposome that encloses water and has no exposed edges.

**Claim:** Based on the diagram, SDS in water would assemble into a bilayer that closes into liposomes, just as phospholipids do.

**Answer: False**

Being amphipathic isn't enough — shape decides. One tail plus a large head packs into micelles, with tails filling the core and no inner water space. Bilayers and liposomes need two tails and a smaller head.

*Concepts linked:* Micelle vs bilayer · Head size and tail number

---

**Q6. [True / False · 1점]**

> During microinjection, a fine glass needle is pushed through a cell's plasma membrane and then withdrawn. Small punctures like this reseal within seconds on their own.

**Claim:** Resealing happens because phospholipids at the edge of the hole expose hydrophobic tails to water, and the lipids spontaneously rearrange to close the gap.

**Answer: True**

Water excludes nonpolar tails (the hydrophobic effect), so an open edge is unstable; lipids flow together to hide the tails again. That is why the bilayer is called self-healing — no covalent bonds or repair enzymes are needed for a small hole.

*Concepts linked:* Self-healing bilayer · Hydrophobic effect · Real-world: microinjection

---

**Q7. [True / False · 2점]**

> In healthy cells, the phospholipid phosphatidylserine sits almost entirely in the inner (cytoplasmic) layer of the plasma membrane. When a cell begins programmed cell death, phosphatidylserine appears in the outer layer, and immune cells use it as an 'eat me' signal.

**Claim:** Keeping this lipid in one layer is possible because phospholipids move freely sideways within a layer but rarely flip-flop between the two layers.

**Answer: True**

Spontaneous flip-flop is rare, which is what lets the two layers differ. Enzymes move phosphatidylserine to the inner layer, and because it rarely flips back on its own, it stays there; in dying cells, other enzymes move it outward within minutes — far faster than spontaneous flip-flop.

*Concepts linked:* Layer asymmetry · Lipid flip-flop · Real-world: signal on dying cells

---

**Q8. [True / False · 2점]**

> A cell's membrane lipids carry a fluorescent label. A laser bleaches a small spot so it goes dark; within seconds, the spot glows again as its fluorescence recovers.

**Claim:** The recovery shows that labeled lipids from the opposite layer of the bilayer flipped over into the bleached spot.

**Answer: False**

Recovery comes from unbleached lipids diffusing sideways into the spot within the same layer. Flip-flop between layers is rare — far too rare to refill a spot in seconds. The experiment demonstrates membrane fluidity.

*Concepts linked:* Lateral diffusion · Flip-flop is rare · Real-world: fluorescence recovery experiments

---

**Q9. [True / False · 1점]**

> During exocytosis, a vesicle's membrane merges with the plasma membrane and its contents end up outside the cell.

**Claim:** For an instant during fusion, the cytosol is open to the outside of the cell, because the membrane has to break before the vesicle can join it.

**Answer: False**

Membranes are continuous, unbroken sheets whose structure allows fusion without losing continuity: the vesicle's interior becomes continuous with the outside, while the cytosol stays sealed off throughout.

*Concepts linked:* Membranes are continuous sheets · Fusion · Exocytosis

---

**Q10. [True / False · 1점]**

> A phospholipid's hydrophilic head contains glycerol, a phosphate group, and a polar group such as choline; its two tails are fatty acids.

**Claim:** Replacing the phosphate and its polar group with a third fatty acid attached to the glycerol would give a molecule that forms bilayers even more readily, because it would have more hydrophobic tail.

**Answer: False**

That molecule is a triacylglycerol — hydrophobic throughout, with no polar head. Without being amphipathic it has nothing to face the water, so it clumps into oil droplets instead of forming a bilayer.

*Concepts linked:* Amphipathic phospholipid · Triacylglycerol · Bilayer formation

---

**Q11. [True / False · 1점]**

> When certain immune cells receive a signal, particular receptor proteins and lipids gather together into lipid rafts in the plasma membrane.

**Claim:** Because the membrane would stay intact and fluid, dispersing the rafts would leave this signaling unaffected.

**Answer: False**

Rafts are microdomains that act as platforms, concentrating particular lipids and proteins for cell processes. Dispersing them spreads those components apart, which can weaken signaling even though the bilayer itself stays intact and fluid.

*Concepts linked:* Lipid rafts as platforms · Receptors

**Q12. [Multiple Choice · 2점]**

A drug company wants a lipid particle that carries a water-soluble drug dissolved inside it through the bloodstream. Using the diagram, which design would work?

**◇ Diagram — Phospholipid shape decides what forms in water.** Phospholipids with one tail and a large head pack into a micelle with their tails in the core; phospholipids with two tails and a smaller head form a bilayer sheet with the tails sandwiched inside, which closes into a liposome that encloses water and has no exposed edges.

① One-tailed lipids that form micelles, holding the drug in the micelle's core　 ② Triacylglycerols, since three tails make an even more stable bilayer than two　 ③ A flat phospholipid bilayer sheet carrying the drug on one face, since the sheet never needs to close　 ④ Two-tailed phospholipids that close into liposomes, trapping the drug in the enclosed water space　 ⑤ Any lipid at all, since every lipid is hydrophobic and keeps water out equally well

**Answer: ④ Two-tailed phospholipids that close into liposomes, trapping the drug in the enclosed water space**

Only a closed bilayer (liposome) encloses an inner aqueous space for a water-soluble drug. A micelle's core is packed with hydrophobic tails; triacylglycerol has no polar head, so it isn't amphipathic and can't form a bilayer; open sheet edges would expose tails to water, so sheets close up.

*Concepts linked:* Liposomes · Micelle vs bilayer · Amphipathic lipids · Real-world: liposomal drug delivery

---

**Q13. [Multiple Choice · 2점]**

Four artificial membranes are each made from one kind of phospholipid: W (18-carbon tails, no C=C), X (18-carbon tails, two C=C each), Y (12-carbon tails, no C=C), and Z (12-carbon tails, two C=C each). No cholesterol is present. Which pair correctly names the least fluid and the most fluid membrane?

**◇ Diagram — Four membrane patches: tail length, double bonds, cholesterol.** Four membrane patches drawn side by side: phospholipids with long, straight saturated tails; phospholipids with short, straight saturated tails; phospholipids whose unsaturated tails are kinked at C=C double bonds and sit further apart; and phospholipids with cholesterol molecules wedged between them.

① Least fluid: Z; most fluid: W　 ② Least fluid: W; most fluid: X　 ③ Least fluid: Y; most fluid: X　 ④ Least fluid: X; most fluid: Y　 ⑤ Least fluid: W; most fluid: Z

**Answer: ⑤ Least fluid: W; most fluid: Z**

Longer tails and fewer double bonds both lower fluidity, so W (long, saturated) is least fluid and Z (short, kinked) is most fluid. X and Y each have one fluidizing feature, so neither can sit at an extreme.

*Concepts linked:* Tail length · C=C double bonds · Membrane fluidity

---

**Q14. [Multiple Choice · 1점]**

A kidney-cell membrane protein binds a hormone at the cell's outer surface; binding changes the shape of a part of the same protein that faces the cytoplasm. Which description fits this protein?

① A receptor that is an integral, transmembrane protein　 ② A receptor that is a peripheral protein on the outer surface　 ③ A transporter that is a peripheral protein on the inner surface　 ④ An anchor that is an integral protein linking the membrane to the cell wall　 ⑤ An enzyme that is a peripheral protein, since receptors cannot span membranes

**Answer: ① A receptor that is an integral, transmembrane protein**

Receiving a signal is the receptor function; relaying a shape change from the outer face to the cytoplasmic face requires one protein spanning the bilayer — an integral, transmembrane protein. Peripheral proteins sit on only one face, and animal cells have no cell wall.

*Concepts linked:* Membrane protein functions · Integral vs peripheral · Transmembrane proteins

---

**Q15. [Multiple Choice · 1점]**

Cholesterol inserts into the lipid bilayer of animal cell membranes. Which description of its orientation in the bilayer matches its structure?

① It lies entirely within the hydrophobic core, since a steroid has no polar part at all　 ② It is attached to sugar chains that face the extracellular fluid, like a glycolipid　 ③ Its OH group sits by the phospholipid heads; its rings and tail lie among the fatty acid tails　 ④ It sits on the cytoplasmic surface, held in place there by peripheral proteins　 ⑤ It is covalently bonded to neighbouring phospholipids, which is how it stiffens the membrane

**Answer: ③ Its OH group sits by the phospholipid heads; its rings and tail lie among the fatty acid tails**

Cholesterol is amphipathic: a hydroxyl head plus a rigid four-ring body and a hydrocarbon tail. The OH stays near the polar heads and water; the rest packs alongside the tails, where it buffers fluidity through noncovalent packing.

*Concepts linked:* Cholesterol structure · Amphipathic · Fluidity buffer

### Endomembrane System

**Q16. [보기 Combination · 3점] Following a pulse of labeled protein**

Pancreatic cells, which secrete digestive enzymes, are given a brief pulse of radioactive amino acids, and the label is tracked along the route in the diagram. Which statements in <보기> are correct?

**◇ Diagram — The endomembrane route: ER → Golgi → destination.** Flowchart of the endomembrane route: a rough ER studded with ribosomes and a ribosome-free smooth ER; a vesicle carries material from the ER to the Golgi, which ships it to three destinations: secretory vesicles, the plasma membrane, and lysosomes.

> **(가)** At 3 minutes, the label is concentrated in the rough ER.
>
> **(나)** At 20 minutes it is in the Golgi apparatus; by 2 hours it is in secretory vesicles near the plasma membrane.

**<보기>**
- ㄱ. The label appeared first in the rough ER because ribosomes on that membrane assemble amino acids into the new enzymes.
- ㄴ. The label reached the Golgi inside vesicles, since the Golgi is not physically attached to the ER.
- ㄷ. Each time a secretory vesicle releases its enzymes, its membrane is lost from the cell, so the plasma membrane gradually shrinks.

① ㄱ　 ② ㄷ　 ③ ㄱ, ㄴ　 ④ ㄱ, ㄷ　 ⑤ ㄴ, ㄷ

**Answer: ③ ㄱ, ㄴ**
- ㄱ — **O** — Ribosomes bound to the rough ER are sites of protein synthesis, so newly incorporated amino acids show up there first.
- ㄴ — **O** — The Golgi is the next stop for vesicles budding from the ER; no membrane bridge joins them.
- ㄷ — **X** — The vesicle membrane fuses into the plasma membrane and becomes part of it — fusion adds membrane rather than removing it.

*Concepts linked:* Rough ER · ER-to-Golgi vesicles · Exocytosis · Real-world: pulse-chase experiments

---

**Q17. [보기 Combination · 3점] Enzymes made right, delivered wrong**

In I-cell disease, lysosomal enzymes are made normally, but a Golgi defect means they are not sorted to lysosomes. Which statements in <보기> are correct?

> **(가)** Normal route: lysosomal enzymes are made in the rough ER → sorted in the Golgi → packaged into lysosomes.
>
> **(나)** In patients, lysosomes swell with undigested material, and the missing enzymes are found at high levels in the blood.

**<보기>**
- ㄱ. Undigested macromolecules build up inside the patients' lysosomes because the lysosomes lack the enzymes that break them down.
- ㄴ. The enzymes' amino acid sequences must be abnormal, since the enzymes end up in the wrong place.
- ㄷ. The enzymes reach the blood because, when not sorted to lysosomes, they leave the Golgi in secretory vesicles and are released by exocytosis.

① ㄷ　 ② ㄱ, ㄴ　 ③ ㄱ, ㄷ　 ④ ㄴ, ㄷ　 ⑤ ㄱ, ㄴ, ㄷ

**Answer: ③ ㄱ, ㄷ**
- ㄱ — **O** — Lysosomes degrade proteins, nucleic acids, lipids, and complex carbohydrates only if their enzymes arrive; without them, material accumulates.
- ㄴ — **X** — Synthesis in the rough ER is normal; the failure is in the Golgi's sorting step. A correct protein can still be sent to the wrong destination.
- ㄷ — **O** — The Golgi ships proteins to lysosomes, the plasma membrane, or secretory vesicles; enzymes that miss the lysosome route are shipped out of the cell.

*Concepts linked:* Lysosomal enzyme route · Golgi sorting · Real-world: I-cell disease

---

**Q18. [보기 Combination · 3점] Assembly line in the Golgi**

Read (가) and (나) about how the Golgi apparatus modifies a membrane glycoprotein. Which statements in <보기> are correct?

> **(가)** The enzyme that adds sugar Y is found only in cisternae that the protein reaches after the cisternae holding the enzyme that adds sugar X.
>
> **(나)** Sugar Y can be attached only to a sugar chain that already carries sugar X.

**<보기>**
- ㄱ. Glycosylation links the sugars to the protein by hydrogen bonds, so the chains can easily fall off later.
- ㄴ. Placing different enzymes in different regions of the Golgi lets modifications occur in a set order as the protein moves through.
- ㄷ. Once the protein reaches the plasma membrane, its sugar chains will face the extracellular fluid, because the inside of the Golgi corresponds to the outside of the cell after vesicle fusion.

① ㄱ　 ② ㄴ　 ③ ㄷ　 ④ ㄱ, ㄷ　 ⑤ ㄴ, ㄷ

**Answer: ⑤ ㄴ, ㄷ**
- ㄱ — **X** — Glycosylation joins carbohydrates covalently to lipids or to specific amino acids of proteins; the chains are stable.
- ㄴ — **O** — Each modification step happens in a different region with its own enzymes, so X is added before Y by position.
- ㄷ — **O** — A vesicle's inner face becomes the outer face of the plasma membrane when it fuses — which is why membrane carbohydrates all face outward.

*Concepts linked:* Golgi cisternae · Glycosylation · Membrane orientation · Carbohydrates face outward

---

**Q19. [보기 Combination · 2점] One system, two kinds of connection**

Which statements in <보기> about how the endomembrane system is connected are correct?

> **(가)** The outer nuclear membrane is continuous with the ER membrane.
>
> **(나)** Material travels from the ER to the Golgi, and from the Golgi to the plasma membrane, only inside vesicles.

**<보기>**
- ㄱ. (가) is a permanent membrane bridge, while (나) is a transient connection made by vesicles budding from one compartment and fusing with another.
- ㄴ. A phospholipid made in the smooth ER could reach the plasma membrane without ever leaving a membrane.
- ㄷ. The nucleus is the innermost organelle of this system, and the plasma membrane belongs to the same system.

① ㄱ　 ② ㄴ　 ③ ㄷ　 ④ ㄴ, ㄷ　 ⑤ ㄱ, ㄴ, ㄷ

**Answer: ⑤ ㄱ, ㄴ, ㄷ**
- ㄱ — **O** — Endomembrane compartments are linked either physically by membrane bridges or transiently by vesicles.
- ㄴ — **O** — It stays in the bilayer as vesicles bud and fuse, moving from the ER membrane to the Golgi membrane to the plasma membrane.
- ㄷ — **O** — The endomembrane system includes the nuclear envelope, ER, Golgi, lysosomes, vesicles, and the plasma membrane; the nucleus is its innermost organelle.

*Concepts linked:* Endomembrane system · Membrane bridges vs vesicles · Smooth ER lipids

---

**Q20. [Matching · 3점] Which compartment is doing the work?**

Match each observation to the endomembrane compartment most directly responsible.

**Observation**
A. A newly made hormone protein first appears on membranes studded with ribosomes
B. A cell that mass-produces phospholipids packs a huge area of ribosome-free tubules and sacs into its cytoplasm
C. Proteins passing through a stack of flattened sacs are modified step by step, gain carbohydrate chains, and are shipped to different destinations
D. Unneeded proteins, lipids, and nucleic acids are broken down in an acidic compartment
E. Large RNA molecules exit through protein-lined openings in a double membrane

**Compartment**
1. Rough ER
2. Nuclear envelope (nuclear pores)
3. Smooth ER
4. Golgi apparatus
5. Lysosome

**Correct matches:**
- A = A newly made hormone protein first appears on membranes studded with ribosomes → Rough ER
- B = A cell that mass-produces phospholipids packs a huge area of ribosome-free tubules and sacs into its cytoplasm → Smooth ER
- C = Proteins passing through a stack of flattened sacs are modified step by step, gain carbohydrate chains, and are shipped to different destinations → Golgi apparatus
- D = Unneeded proteins, lipids, and nucleic acids are broken down in an acidic compartment → Lysosome
- E = Large RNA molecules exit through protein-lined openings in a double membrane → Nuclear envelope (nuclear pores)

Each compartment's structure fits its job: ribosomes on the rough ER for protein synthesis, folded ribosome-free smooth ER for lipids, Golgi cisternae for step-by-step modification and sorting, acidic lysosomes for digestion, and pores in the double nuclear envelope for exchange.

*Concepts linked:* Endomembrane compartments · Structure–function

---

**Q21. [Matching · 3점] If this organelle failed…**

Match each failing structure in an animal cell to its most direct consequence.

**Failing structure**
A. Rough ER
B. Golgi apparatus
C. Lysosomes
D. Mitochondria
E. Cytoskeleton

**Most direct consequence**
1. Far less ATP is made from the energy stored in sugars
2. Proteins leave the ER but are not modified, given sugars, or sorted to their destinations
3. Worn-out proteins, lipids, and nucleic acids pile up undigested
4. The cell loses the protein scaffold that gives it its structure
5. Proteins destined for secretion or the plasma membrane are no longer made

**Correct matches:**
- A = Rough ER → Proteins destined for secretion or the plasma membrane are no longer made
- B = Golgi apparatus → Proteins leave the ER but are not modified, given sugars, or sorted to their destinations
- C = Lysosomes → Worn-out proteins, lipids, and nucleic acids pile up undigested
- D = Mitochondria → Far less ATP is made from the energy stored in sugars
- E = Cytoskeleton → The cell loses the protein scaffold that gives it its structure

Each structure has one main job in the animal cell: the ER makes proteins and lipids, the Golgi modifies and sorts them, lysosomes break down macromolecules, mitochondria harness energy, and the cytoskeleton is the cell's protein scaffold.

*Concepts linked:* Organelle roles in an animal cell

**Q22. [True / False · 1점]**

> Pancreatic cells that secrete digestive enzymes (proteins) are packed with rough ER, while adrenal cells that make lipid-based steroid hormones are packed with smooth ER.

**◇ Diagram — The endomembrane route: ER → Golgi → destination.** Flowchart of the endomembrane route: a rough ER studded with ribosomes and a ribosome-free smooth ER; a vesicle carries material from the ER to the Golgi, which ships it to three destinations: secretory vesicles, the plasma membrane, and lysosomes.

**Claim:** A cell specialized to secrete a protein hormone would be expected to be packed mainly with smooth ER, since smooth ER is what buds off vesicles.

**Answer: False**

Budding vesicles doesn't make a protein factory: proteins for secretion are built by ribosomes on the rough ER. Smooth ER, which lacks ribosomes, makes fatty acids and phospholipids and is abundant in lipid-producing cells.

*Concepts linked:* Smooth vs rough ER · Structure matches function

---

**Q23. [True / False · 2점]**

> A protein is found in the narrow space between the inner and outer membranes of the nuclear envelope.

**Claim:** Because the outer nuclear membrane is continuous with the ER, this space is continuous with the ER's interior, so the protein could reach the ER lumen without crossing a membrane.

**Answer: True**

The nuclear envelope is a double membrane whose outer membrane is physically continuous with the ER; the gap between the two membranes therefore connects directly to the inside of the ER.

*Concepts linked:* Nuclear envelope · ER continuity · Membrane bridges

---

**Q24. [True / False · 2점]**

> Chloroquine, an antimalarial drug, is a weak base that accumulates inside lysosomes and soaks up some of their H+, raising the internal pH from about 5 toward 6.

**◇ Diagram — The endomembrane route: ER → Golgi → destination.** Flowchart of the endomembrane route: a rough ER studded with ribosomes and a ribosome-free smooth ER; a vesicle carries material from the ER to the Golgi, which ships it to three destinations: secretory vesicles, the plasma membrane, and lysosomes.

**Claim:** Raising the lysosome's pH from 5 to 6 cuts its H+ concentration to one-tenth, which would be expected to slow the breakdown of macromolecules inside it.

**Answer: True**

pH is a log scale, so rising one unit means one-tenth the H+. The proton pump normally keeps the lysosome near pH 5, the acidic environment its digestive enzymes work in; making it less acidic impairs digestion.

*Concepts linked:* Lysosome proton pump · pH and enzyme activity · Real-world: chloroquine

---

**Q25. [True / False · 2점]**

> The enzymes of glycolysis are dissolved in the fluid outside the organelles, while the enzymes of the citric acid cycle are inside the mitochondrial matrix.

**Claim:** Both sets of enzymes are in the cytoplasm, but only the glycolysis enzymes are in the cytosol.

**Answer: True**

Using Module 3's definitions, the cytoplasm is everything in the cell except the nucleus, mitochondria included, while the cytosol is only the fluid outside the organelles. So matrix enzymes are cytoplasmic but not cytosolic.

*Concepts linked:* Cytoplasm vs cytosol · Mitochondrial matrix

---

**Q26. [True / False · 1점]**

> Plasma cells, the immune cells that secrete thousands of antibody (protein) molecules per second, have cytoplasm filled with stacked sheets of rough ER.

**Claim:** Because antibodies are secreted, they are most likely made by free ribosomes in the cytosol, while all that rough ER mainly makes membrane lipids.

**Answer: False**

Proteins for secretion are built by ribosomes on the rough ER and then travel ER → Golgi → secretory vesicles → exocytosis; the huge rough-ER area is where the antibodies are made. Lipid synthesis is the smooth ER's job.

*Concepts linked:* Rough vs smooth ER · Secretory route · Real-world: antibody-secreting cells

---

**Q27. [True / False · 1점]**

> Ribosomes are found both bound to the rough ER and free in the cytosol.

**Claim:** Because ribosomes can bind the ER membrane, they are membrane-enclosed organelles that belong to the endomembrane system.

**Answer: False**

Ribosomes have no membrane of their own; they are non-endomembrane structures that attach to the rough ER's cytosolic face or float free in the cytosol.

*Concepts linked:* Ribosomes · EMS vs non-EMS

---

**Q28. [True / False · 1점]**

> Nuclear pores perforate the nuclear envelope, letting molecules move between the nucleus and the cytoplasm.

**Claim:** Away from the pores, a single lipid bilayer separates the nucleus from the cytosol, just as the plasma membrane separates the cell from its surroundings.

**Answer: False**

The nuclear envelope is a double membrane — an inner and an outer membrane, the outer one continuous with the ER — so two bilayers separate the nucleus from the cytosol everywhere except at the pores.

*Concepts linked:* Nuclear envelope · Double membrane

---

**Q29. [True / False · 2점]**

> A drug blocks all vesicle traffic from the ER to the Golgi apparatus.

**Claim:** Newly made lysosomal enzymes would still reach the lysosomes, since lysosomes collect their enzymes directly from the rough ER.

**Answer: False**

Lysosomal enzymes are made in the rough ER, sorted in the Golgi, and only then packaged into lysosomes. The Golgi isn't physically attached to the ER, so blocking ER-to-Golgi vesicles keeps the enzymes from ever reaching lysosomes.

*Concepts linked:* Lysosomal enzyme route · ER-to-Golgi vesicles

---

**Q30. [Multiple Choice · 2점]**

Three molecules must cross the nuclear envelope: a Na+ ion entering the nucleus, a newly made mRNA leaving it, and a large transcription-factor protein (made in the cytoplasm) entering it. Which statement is correct?

① All three diffuse passively, because nuclear pores are simply open holes in the envelope　 ② The ion diffuses passively through the pores; the mRNA and the large protein need active transport through them　 ③ All three cross the double membrane directly, without needing to use the nuclear pores　 ④ The mRNA diffuses out passively because it is made inside the nucleus; only the protein needs active transport　 ⑤ The ion needs active transport because it is charged, while the large protein diffuses through passively

**Answer: ② The ion diffuses passively through the pores; the mRNA and the large protein need active transport through them**

Nuclear pores let small molecules and ions pass by passive diffusion, but large proteins and RNA require active transport. Where a molecule is made doesn't change that — its size does.

*Concepts linked:* Nuclear pores · Nuclear envelope · mRNA export

---

**Q31. [Multiple Choice · 1점]**

A researcher breaks open liver cells and spins out only the nuclei, keeping everything else (sample 1). From part of sample 1, she then removes all membrane-bound organelles, keeping only the fluid (sample 2). How should the samples be named?

① Sample 1 is the cytosol; sample 2 is the cytoplasm　 ② Both samples are cytosol, since neither contains the nucleus　 ③ Sample 1 is the cytoplasm; sample 2 is the cytosol　 ④ Sample 1 is the endomembrane system; sample 2 is the cytoplasm　 ⑤ Sample 1 is the cytoplasm; sample 2 is the nucleoplasm

**Answer: ③ Sample 1 is the cytoplasm; sample 2 is the cytosol**

Using Module 3's definitions: cytoplasm = all cell contents except the nucleus, organelles included; cytosol = the fluid region outside the organelles but inside the plasma membrane.

*Concepts linked:* Cytoplasm vs cytosol · Real-world: cell fractionation

---

**Q32. [Multiple Choice · 1점]**

A wilted houseplant is watered, and its leaves become firm again within hours. Which structure is most directly responsible for restoring firmness?

① The chloroplasts, which make extra sugars that stiffen the leaf's cell walls　 ② The cell wall, which actively contracts to pull the leaf back into shape　 ③ The smooth ER, which makes new phospholipids so each cell can quickly grow larger again　 ④ The central vacuole, which fills with water and presses the cell against its wall　 ⑤ The lysosomes, which break down and clear away the damaged, wilted tissue

**Answer: ④ The central vacuole, which fills with water and presses the cell against its wall**

Water entering by osmosis swells the vacuole, pressing the membrane against the wall; that turgor pressure — not the wall on its own — keeps soft plant tissue firm.

*Concepts linked:* Vacuole · Turgor pressure · Cell wall

---

**Q33. [Multiple Choice · 1점]**

A glycoprotein receptor ends up in the plasma membrane with its sugar chains facing outside the cell. Using the diagram, which route did it take?

**◇ Diagram — The endomembrane route: ER → Golgi → destination.** Flowchart of the endomembrane route: a rough ER studded with ribosomes and a ribosome-free smooth ER; a vesicle carries material from the ER to the Golgi, which ships it to three destinations: secretory vesicles, the plasma membrane, and lysosomes.

① Rough ER → vesicle → Golgi → vesicle → plasma membrane　 ② Free ribosome in the cytosol → Golgi → plasma membrane　 ③ Smooth ER → lysosome → plasma membrane　 ④ Rough ER → Golgi through a permanent membrane bridge → plasma membrane　 ⑤ Golgi → rough ER → vesicle → plasma membrane

**Answer: ① Rough ER → vesicle → Golgi → vesicle → plasma membrane**

Membrane proteins are made on rough-ER ribosomes, carried by vesicle to the Golgi (not physically attached to the ER), where sugars are added, then shipped by vesicle to the plasma membrane.

*Concepts linked:* Secretory pathway · Golgi sorting · Glycosylation

---

**Q34. [Multiple Choice · 2점]**

Lysosomes keep their interior near pH 5, while the cytosol stays near pH 7. Which result would best support the course's explanation of why lysosomal contents must be kept separate from the rest of the cell?

① Lysosomal enzymes released into the cytosol digest the cell's ribosomes　 ② Cytosolic proteins unfold and stop working at pH 5 but not at pH 7　 ③ Lysosomal enzymes are made on the rough ER and then sorted in the Golgi　 ④ Cytosolic proteins keep their shape and activity at every pH from 3 to 11　 ⑤ Lysosomes stay acidic only while their proton pumps are supplied with ATP

**Answer: ② Cytosolic proteins unfold and stop working at pH 5 but not at pH 7**

The course's reason is that the cytosol's proteins and organelles suit pH 7 and would be damaged at pH 5, so the matching test exposes cytosolic proteins to pH 5, with pH 7 as the control. Enzyme digestion is a different reason; the other results are unrelated or contradict the claim.

*Concepts linked:* Why lysosomes are kept separate · Experimental logic

---

**Q35. [Multiple Choice · 1점]**

Two cells of equal volume are compared. In cell X, the ER is a highly folded network of tubules and flattened sacs; in cell Y, the ER is a few round, unfolded sacs enclosing the same total volume. Which prediction follows?

① Cell Y has more ER membrane area, because round sacs have the most surface for their volume　 ② Both cells have the same ER membrane area, because their ER encloses the same volume　 ③ Folding reduces the ER's membrane area but greatly increases the space enclosed inside it　 ④ Folding matters only for mitochondria; the shape of the ER doesn't affect its output　 ⑤ Cell X has far more ER membrane area, so more surface on which to make lipids and proteins

**Answer: ⑤ Cell X has far more ER membrane area, so more surface on which to make lipids and proteins**

For a given enclosed volume, a round shape has the least surface; folding into tubules and flattened sacs packs much more membrane into the same space. That large membrane area is where the ER makes lipids and proteins.

*Concepts linked:* ER structure · Membrane surface area

---

**Q36. [Multiple Choice · 2점]**

Away from any pores or channels, how many lipid bilayers separate each of these spaces from the cytosol: the inside of the nucleus, the mitochondrial matrix, and the inside of a lysosome?

① 1, 2, 1　 ② 2, 1, 1　 ③ 2, 2, 2　 ④ 2, 2, 1　 ⑤ 1, 1, 1

**Answer: ④ 2, 2, 1**

The nuclear envelope is a double membrane, and a mitochondrion has an outer and an inner membrane; a lysosome is a specialized vesicle bounded by a single membrane.

*Concepts linked:* Nuclear envelope · Mitochondrial membranes · Lysosome membrane

---

**Q37. [Multiple Choice · 1점]**

Electron micrographs show ribosomes attached to the outer membrane of the nuclear envelope, just as they are attached to the rough ER. Which fact best explains this?

① The nuclear envelope is part of the Golgi apparatus　 ② The outer nuclear membrane is physically continuous with the ER membrane　 ③ Ribosomes are assembled in the nucleolus and stick to the envelope as they leave　 ④ Nuclear pores are lined with ribosomes that carry out export　 ⑤ The inner nuclear membrane is where the cell's lipids are made

**Answer: ② The outer nuclear membrane is physically continuous with the ER membrane**

The outer nuclear membrane and the ER form one continuous membrane, so the outer nuclear membrane can behave like rough ER, with ribosomes bound to its cytosolic face.

*Concepts linked:* Nuclear envelope–ER continuity

### Mitochondria & Chloroplasts

**Q38. [보기 Combination · 3점] Counting membranes to the destination**

Two molecules start in the cytosol. Using the diagram, which statements in <보기> are correct?

**◇ Diagram — Mitochondrion vs. chloroplast: membranes and compartments.** Side-by-side mitochondrion and chloroplast with labelled compartments. Mitochondrion: 1 outer membrane, 2 intermembrane space, 3 inner membrane folded inward, 4 matrix. Chloroplast: A outer membrane, B inner membrane, C thylakoid membrane, D lumen inside a thylakoid, E granum (a stack of thylakoids), F stroma around the thylakoids.

> **(가)** Molecule X must reach the lumen of a thylakoid.
>
> **(나)** Molecule Y, a charged molecule such as ADP, must reach the mitochondrial matrix.

**<보기>**
- ㄱ. X and Y must each cross the same number of membranes: two.
- ㄴ. Once Y passes through the porins of the outer membrane, it will reach the matrix without needing any other transport protein.
- ㄷ. The chloroplast stroma occupies the position that corresponds to the mitochondrial matrix: the space enclosed by the inner membrane.

① ㄱ　 ② ㄴ　 ③ ㄷ　 ④ ㄱ, ㄴ　 ⑤ ㄱ, ㄴ, ㄷ

**Answer: ③ ㄷ**
- ㄱ — **X** — X crosses three — the chloroplast's outer (A), inner (B), and thylakoid (C) membranes — while Y crosses two, the mitochondrial outer (1) and inner (3) membranes.
- ㄴ — **X** — Porins make only the outer membrane nonselective; the inner membrane is a separate, selective barrier that charged molecules cross only through transport proteins.
- ㄷ — **O** — Both are the innermost space bounded by the inner membrane; the chloroplast simply adds thylakoids inside it.

*Concepts linked:* Double membranes · Thylakoid as a third membrane · Porins

---

**Q39. [보기 Combination · 3점] Bacteria inside our cells**

Using the diagram, which statements in <보기> about the origin of mitochondria and chloroplasts are correct?

**◇ Diagram — Two hypotheses for how the eukaryotic cell arose.** Two proposed routes to the eukaryotic cell, each drawn as four stages. Hypothesis 1: an archaeon; infolding forms a nucleus and endomembrane system; the cell engulfs a proteobacterium; a eukaryote with mitochondria. Hypothesis 2: an archaeon with a proteobacterium; the symbiont lives inside the host; infolding forms a nucleus; a eukaryote with mitochondria.

> **(가)** Mitochondria and chloroplasts have their own circular genomes and ribosomes, and they grow and divide semi-autonomously.
>
> **(나)** Mitochondria resemble proteobacteria, and chloroplasts resemble cyanobacteria, in organization and biochemistry.

**<보기>**
- ㄱ. Their circular genomes support an endosymbiotic origin, since bacteria typically have a single circular chromosome.
- ㄴ. Under both hypotheses, mitochondria descend from a proteobacterium; the hypotheses differ in whether the nucleus formed before or after that partnership.
- ㄷ. Under Hypothesis 1, the proteobacterium was engulfed by a host cell that had not yet formed a nucleus.

① ㄱ　 ② ㄷ　 ③ ㄱ, ㄴ　 ④ ㄴ, ㄷ　 ⑤ ㄱ, ㄴ, ㄷ

**Answer: ③ ㄱ, ㄴ**
- ㄱ — **O** — Circular DNA, their own ribosomes, and independent division are bacterial traits kept from free-living ancestors.
- ㄴ — **O** — Hypothesis 1: infolding makes the nucleus and EMS first, then a proteobacterium is engulfed. Hypothesis 2: the partnership comes first and infolding makes the nucleus later.
- ㄷ — **X** — That describes Hypothesis 2. In Hypothesis 1, the archaeon forms its nucleus and endomembrane system by infolding before engulfing the proteobacterium.

*Concepts linked:* Endosymbiosis · Two hypotheses for eukaryote origin · Semi-autonomous organelles

---

**Q40. [Matching · 4점] Locating events in the two energy organelles**

Using the diagram's labels, match each compartment to the scenario that happens there or depends on it.

**◇ Diagram — Mitochondrion vs. chloroplast: membranes and compartments.** Side-by-side mitochondrion and chloroplast with labelled compartments. Mitochondrion: 1 outer membrane, 2 intermembrane space, 3 inner membrane folded inward, 4 matrix. Chloroplast: A outer membrane, B inner membrane, C thylakoid membrane, D lumen inside a thylakoid, E granum (a stack of thylakoids), F stroma around the thylakoids.

**Compartment**
A. 1 — mitochondrial outer membrane
B. 2 — intermembrane space
C. 3 — mitochondrial inner membrane
D. 4 — matrix
E. C — thylakoid membrane
F. F — stroma

**Scenario**
1. Where mitochondrial genes are transcribed and translated
2. A toxin that blocks ATP synthase acts here
3. A small dye molecule crosses easily through large, nonselective channels
4. Reached after crossing the chloroplast's outer and inner membranes, without entering any thylakoid
5. Where chlorophyll absorbs light energy
6. Where that dye stops if it cannot cross the next, more selective membrane

**Correct matches:**
- A = 1 — mitochondrial outer membrane → A small dye molecule crosses easily through large, nonselective channels
- B = 2 — intermembrane space → Where that dye stops if it cannot cross the next, more selective membrane
- C = 3 — mitochondrial inner membrane → A toxin that blocks ATP synthase acts here
- D = 4 — matrix → Where mitochondrial genes are transcribed and translated
- E = C — thylakoid membrane → Where chlorophyll absorbs light energy
- F = F — stroma → Reached after crossing the chloroplast's outer and inner membranes, without entering any thylakoid

Porins make the outer membrane nonselective, so the inner membrane is the real barrier. The ETC and ATP synthase sit in the inner membrane; DNA and ribosomes are in the matrix; pigments are in thylakoid membranes; the stroma is the fluid around the thylakoids.

*Concepts linked:* Mitochondrial compartments · Chloroplast compartments

---

**Q41. [True / False · 1점]**

> A student argues that mitochondria belong to the endomembrane system because they are surrounded by membranes and sit in the cytoplasm next to the ER and Golgi.

**Claim:** The student is right, because what qualifies an organelle for the endomembrane system is being enclosed by a membrane.

**Answer: False**

Membership depends on connection — by membrane bridges or vesicle traffic — not on having a membrane. Mitochondria and chloroplasts are membranous but are not part of the endomembrane system.

*Concepts linked:* EMS membership · Mitochondria and chloroplasts are non-EMS

---

**Q42. [True / False · 1점]**

> Heart muscle cells use ATP nonstop. Their mitochondria have much more densely folded inner membranes than the mitochondria of less active cells.

**◇ Diagram — Mitochondrion vs. chloroplast: membranes and compartments.** Side-by-side mitochondrion and chloroplast with labelled compartments. Mitochondrion: 1 outer membrane, 2 intermembrane space, 3 inner membrane folded inward, 4 matrix. Chloroplast: A outer membrane, B inner membrane, C thylakoid membrane, D lumen inside a thylakoid, E granum (a stack of thylakoids), F stroma around the thylakoids.

**Claim:** The extra folding would be expected to support faster ATP production, because it increases the membrane area available for the electron transport chain and ATP synthase.

**Answer: True**

The ETC and ATP synthase are located in the inner membrane; folding packs more of that membrane — and more ATP-making machinery — into each mitochondrion.

*Concepts linked:* Inner membrane folding · ATP synthase · Real-world: heart muscle

---

**Q43. [True / False · 2점]**

> Hypothesis 2 in the diagram begins with an archaeon host living in partnership with a proteobacterium symbiont inside it.

**◇ Diagram — Two hypotheses for how the eukaryotic cell arose.** Two proposed routes to the eukaryotic cell, each drawn as four stages. Hypothesis 1: an archaeon; infolding forms a nucleus and endomembrane system; the cell engulfs a proteobacterium; a eukaryote with mitochondria. Hypothesis 2: an archaeon with a proteobacterium; the symbiont lives inside the host; infolding forms a nucleus; a eukaryote with mitochondria.

**Claim:** Hypothesis 2 implies that, for a time, a cell contained the ancestor of mitochondria but did not yet have a nucleus.

**Answer: True**

In Hypothesis 2 the symbiosis comes first and membrane infolding produces the nucleus later — so there was a stage with a symbiont inside a still nucleus-free archaeal host. Hypothesis 1 predicts the opposite order.

*Concepts linked:* Hypothesis 1 vs Hypothesis 2 · Prokaryotes lack a nucleus

---

**Q44. [True / False · 1점]**

> Mitochondria and chloroplasts both contain their own circular genomes and ribosomes, and both grow and divide independently within the cell.

**◇ Diagram — Two hypotheses for how the eukaryotic cell arose.** Two proposed routes to the eukaryotic cell, each drawn as four stages. Hypothesis 1: an archaeon; infolding forms a nucleus and endomembrane system; the cell engulfs a proteobacterium; a eukaryote with mitochondria. Hypothesis 2: an archaeon with a proteobacterium; the symbiont lives inside the host; infolding forms a nucleus; a eukaryote with mitochondria.

**Claim:** Mitochondria and chloroplasts descend from the same engulfed bacterium, which later specialized into two kinds of organelle.

**Answer: False**

They come from two different endosymbiotic events: mitochondria from a proteobacterium, and chloroplasts from a cyanobacterium engulfed later by a eukaryotic cell that already had mitochondria.

*Concepts linked:* Endosymbiosis: two separate partners

---

**Q45. [True / False · 1점]**

> A leaf cell contains both chloroplasts and mitochondria.

**Claim:** Because chloroplasts already make sugars, a leaf cell would be expected to rely on its chloroplasts, rather than mitochondria, to harness the energy in those sugars as ATP.

**Answer: False**

Chloroplasts capture light energy to make sugars; mitochondria then harness the energy in sugars to make ATP. Plant cells need both — mitochondria are present in almost all eukaryotic cells.

*Concepts linked:* Chloroplasts make sugars · Mitochondria make ATP

---

**Q46. [True / False · 2점]**

> Mature human red blood cells have no mitochondria (and no nucleus), yet they still need ATP — for example, to run their Na+/K+ pumps.

**Claim:** Red blood cells must make their ATP without the citric acid cycle and the electron transport chain.

**Answer: True**

The citric acid cycle runs in the mitochondrial matrix, and the ETC and ATP synthase sit in the inner membrane; with no mitochondria, red cells rely on ATP-making reactions in the cytosol (glycolysis).

*Concepts linked:* Mitochondrial compartments · Real-world: red blood cells

---

**Q47. [True / False · 2점]**

> Both the ER and the inner mitochondrial membrane are highly folded.

**Claim:** In both cases, folding is valuable mainly because it increases the volume of the compartment the membrane encloses.

**Answer: False**

Folding increases membrane area, not enclosed volume. More ER membrane means more room for making lipids and proteins; more inner mitochondrial membrane means more room for the electron transport chain and ATP synthase.

*Concepts linked:* Membrane folding and surface area · ER · Inner mitochondrial membrane

---

**Q48. [Multiple Choice · 2점]**

Chloramphenicol, an antibiotic that blocks bacterial ribosomes, can cause serious side effects (such as bone-marrow suppression) by also blocking protein synthesis inside one human organelle. Which organelle is it, and why is it vulnerable?

① Mitochondria — they descend from bacteria and keep bacteria-like ribosomes　 ② Rough ER — its ribosomes are attached to a membrane, like some bacterial ones　 ③ Nucleus — it holds the cell's DNA, which the drug stops cells from reading　 ④ Golgi apparatus — it modifies newly made proteins, so it depends on ribosomes　 ⑤ Lysosomes — their digestive enzymes are proteins that need constant replacement

**Answer: ① Mitochondria — they descend from bacteria and keep bacteria-like ribosomes**

Mitochondria originated as endosymbiotic proteobacteria and keep their own DNA and ribosomes in the matrix, so a drug aimed at bacterial ribosomes can hit them too; cytosolic and rough-ER ribosomes are the eukaryotic type.

*Concepts linked:* Mitochondrial ribosomes · Endosymbiosis · Real-world: antibiotic side effects

---

**Q49. [Multiple Choice · 1점]**

In autumn, the green colour drains from many leaves as their chlorophyll is broken down. Where in the chloroplast is this pigment being lost, and which process does the loss directly weaken?

① The stroma — capturing light energy to make sugars　 ② The outer membrane — the barrier controlling what enters the chloroplast　 ③ The thylakoid membranes — converting sugars into ATP　 ④ The lumen — storing the sugars made in summer　 ⑤ The thylakoid membranes — capturing light energy to make sugars

**Answer: ⑤ The thylakoid membranes — capturing light energy to make sugars**

Light-collecting pigments, mostly chlorophyll, sit in the thylakoid membrane, so losing them weakens light capture for photosynthesis. Converting sugars into ATP is the mitochondrion's job.

*Concepts linked:* Thylakoid pigments · Photosynthesis · Real-world: autumn leaves

---

**Q50. [Multiple Choice · 2점]**

A small dye added to the cytosol can pass through porins but cannot cross a lipid bilayer on its own or use any other mitochondrial protein. Where in the mitochondrion will it be found?

① In the matrix　 ② In both the matrix and the intermembrane space　 ③ In the intermembrane space　 ④ Nowhere — porins are in the inner membrane　 ⑤ In the thylakoid lumen

**Answer: ③ In the intermembrane space**

Porins sit in the outer membrane, so the dye passes into the intermembrane space; with no way across the inner membrane, it never reaches the matrix. Thylakoids belong to chloroplasts, not mitochondria.

*Concepts linked:* Porins · Intermembrane space · Inner-membrane barrier

---

**Q51. [Multiple Choice · 2점]**

A single-celled green alga contains both mitochondria and chloroplasts. Which sequence of events best explains how it came to have both?

① A single engulfed proteobacterium later divided into both mitochondria and chloroplasts　 ② A cyanobacterium engulfed a proteobacterium, and together the pair became the first alga　 ③ A cell that already had mitochondria later engulfed a cyanobacterium that became the chloroplast　 ④ Chloroplasts formed first by infolding of the cell membrane, and mitochondria were engulfed later　 ⑤ Mitochondria came from an engulfed cyanobacterium, and chloroplasts from a proteobacterium

**Answer: ③ A cell that already had mitochondria later engulfed a cyanobacterium that became the chloroplast**

Two separate endosymbioses: mitochondria came from a proteobacterium; later, in the lineage leading to green algae and plants, a cell that already had mitochondria engulfed a cyanobacterium. That is why nearly all eukaryotes have mitochondria but only some have chloroplasts.

*Concepts linked:* Endosymbiosis: mitochondria and chloroplasts

### Passive Transport & Osmosis

**Q52. [보기 Combination · 3점] Two hormones, two strategies**

Using the diagram, which statements in <보기> are correct?

**◇ Diagram — What can cross a bare lipid bilayer?.** A bare lipid bilayer lets small gases (O2, CO2), nonpolar molecules such as lipids, and small uncharged polar water cross by simple diffusion, but blocks charged ions such as Na+, K+ and H+, which need channels or pumps, and large macromolecules such as proteins and carbohydrates, which need bulk transport.

> **(가)** Testosterone, a steroid hormone (a lipid), binds receptor proteins inside its target cells.
>
> **(나)** Insulin, a protein hormone, binds receptor proteins on the outer surface of its target cells.

**<보기>**
- ㄱ. Testosterone can reach receptors inside the cell because, being nonpolar, it crosses the bilayer by simple diffusion.
- ㄴ. Insulin stays outside because it is nonpolar and gets trapped in the bilayer's hydrophobic core.
- ㄷ. A small fragment of insulin that kept many charged side chains could cross the bilayer as freely as water.

① ㄱ　 ② ㄴ　 ③ ㄷ　 ④ ㄱ, ㄷ　 ⑤ ㄴ, ㄷ

**Answer: ① ㄱ**
- ㄱ — **O** — Nonpolar molecules such as lipids pass directly through the hydrophobic core of the bilayer.
- ㄴ — **X** — Insulin is a large, polar macromolecule — too large to cross the bilayer at all — so it signals through a surface receptor instead.
- ㄷ — **X** — Shrinking helps only if the molecule is also uncharged; charged groups are still blocked by the hydrophobic core.

*Concepts linked:* Selective permeability · Simple diffusion · Receptors · Real-world: steroid vs protein hormones

---

**Q53. [보기 Combination · 3점] Reading uptake curves**

Uptake of two molecules into cells is measured as their outside concentration is raised. Using the diagram, which statements in <보기> are correct?

**◇ Diagram — Four ways across a membrane.** Four ways across a membrane, each with solute dots crowded on one side: a solute crossing straight through the bilayer, a solute passing through a channel protein&#x27;s aqueous pore, and a solute carried by a protein that binds it and changes shape each move from the crowded outside to the sparse inside; a pump that converts ATP to ADP + Pi moves its solute from the sparse inside to the crowded outside.

> **(가)** Uptake of X rises at first but levels off at high outside concentrations; a drug that locks X's transport protein in one shape blocks uptake. X always moves from high to low concentration.
>
> **(나)** Uptake of Y rises in direct proportion to its outside concentration, never levels off, and needs no protein.

**<보기>**
- ㄱ. X crosses by facilitated diffusion through a carrier: each molecule must bind and trigger a shape change, so uptake stops rising once all the carriers are busy.
- ㄴ. Because X's uptake levels off, X must be moving against its concentration gradient using energy.
- ㄷ. Y is most likely a small uncharged or nonpolar molecule crossing by simple diffusion.

① ㄱ　 ② ㄴ　 ③ ㄱ, ㄷ　 ④ ㄴ, ㄷ　 ⑤ ㄱ, ㄴ, ㄷ

**Answer: ③ ㄱ, ㄷ**
- ㄱ — **O** — Carriers bind specific molecules and change conformation to release them on the other side; a limited number of carriers caps the rate, and locking the shape blocks transport.
- ㄴ — **X** — Leveling off reflects a limited number of carriers, not the direction of movement; X moves down its gradient, so this is passive.
- ㄷ — **O** — Simple diffusion needs no protein, so nothing saturates — the rate just tracks the concentration gradient.

*Concepts linked:* Carrier proteins · Simple vs facilitated diffusion · Passive transport

---

**Q54. [보기 Combination · 3점] The wrong IV bag**

Using the diagram, which statements in <보기> are correct?

**◇ Diagram — Same solution, different outcomes: animal vs. plant cells.** Grid of an animal cell (no wall) and a plant cell (with a wall) in three solutions: more solute outside, equal solute, and less solute outside. Arrows show net water movement out of the cells, balanced in both directions, and into the cells respectively, and each cell is drawn with the shape it takes in that solution.

> **(가)** Normal saline (0.9% NaCl) is isotonic to red blood cells and is safe to infuse into a vein.
>
> **(나)** By mistake, a large volume of pure water is infused into a patient's vein.

**<보기>**
- ㄱ. In normal saline, water molecules stop crossing the red cell membrane.
- ㄴ. Red blood cells exposed to the pure water would swell and could burst (hemolysis), because water moves into the cells, where the solute concentration is higher.
- ㄷ. A plant cell placed in pure water would swell but not burst, because its wall resists the pressure; it becomes turgid.

① ㄱ　 ② ㄴ　 ③ ㄷ　 ④ ㄱ, ㄴ　 ⑤ ㄴ, ㄷ

**Answer: ⑤ ㄴ, ㄷ**
- ㄱ — **X** — Isotonic means no net flow; water still crosses in both directions at equal rates.
- ㄴ — **O** — Pure water is hypotonic to the cells: water enters by osmosis, the cells swell, and animal cells, having no wall, can lyse.
- ㄷ — **O** — The wall pushes back against the swelling vacuole and membrane, producing turgor instead of lysis.

*Concepts linked:* Tonicity · Hemolysis · Turgor · Real-world: IV fluids

---

**Q55. [Matching · 4점] Name that transport mode**

Match each example to the transport mode it uses.

**Example**
A. O2 entering a red blood cell in the lungs
B. K+ leaving a nerve cell through a pore that opens only when triggered
C. Glucose moving down its gradient into a muscle cell by binding a carrier that changes shape
D. Na+ pushed out of a cell against its gradient by a pump that hydrolyzes ATP
E. Glucose pulled into an intestinal cell against its gradient as Na+ flows in
F. A white blood cell engulfing a whole bacterium

**Transport mode**
1. Simple diffusion
2. Secondary active transport
3. Phagocytosis (bulk transport)
4. Facilitated diffusion through a gated channel
5. Facilitated diffusion through a carrier
6. Primary active transport

**Correct matches:**
- A = O2 entering a red blood cell in the lungs → Simple diffusion
- B = K+ leaving a nerve cell through a pore that opens only when triggered → Facilitated diffusion through a gated channel
- C = Glucose moving down its gradient into a muscle cell by binding a carrier that changes shape → Facilitated diffusion through a carrier
- D = Na+ pushed out of a cell against its gradient by a pump that hydrolyzes ATP → Primary active transport
- E = Glucose pulled into an intestinal cell against its gradient as Na+ flows in → Secondary active transport
- F = A white blood cell engulfing a whole bacterium → Phagocytosis (bulk transport)

Ask two questions: is a protein needed, and is the solute moving with or against its gradient? Down the gradient = passive (no protein = simple; protein = facilitated). Against it = active (ATP directly = primary; another ion's gradient = secondary). Whole particles need bulk transport.

*Concepts linked:* Passive vs active · Channels vs carriers · Primary vs secondary · Bulk transport

---

**Q56. [Matching · 3점] Same physics, different cells**

Match each real situation to what happens to the cells.

**Situation**
A. Red blood cells dropped into distilled water
B. Red blood cells placed in concentrated salt water
C. Spinach leaves soaked in strong brine
D. Cells of a wilted plant after the soil is watered
E. Red blood cells suspended in normal (0.9%) saline

**What happens to the cells**
1. Their membranes pull away from the cell walls (plasmolysis)
2. They swell against their walls and become turgid
3. They shrivel (crenation)
4. They swell and burst (lysis)
5. No net water movement; they keep their normal shape

**Correct matches:**
- A = Red blood cells dropped into distilled water → They swell and burst (lysis)
- B = Red blood cells placed in concentrated salt water → They shrivel (crenation)
- C = Spinach leaves soaked in strong brine → Their membranes pull away from the cell walls (plasmolysis)
- D = Cells of a wilted plant after the soil is watered → They swell against their walls and become turgid
- E = Red blood cells suspended in normal (0.9%) saline → No net water movement; they keep their normal shape

First decide whether the outside is hypertonic, hypotonic, or isotonic to the cells; water then moves toward the higher solute concentration. The cell wall is the only reason plant and animal outcomes differ.

*Concepts linked:* Tonicity · Crenation · Plasmolysis · Turgor

---

**Q57. [True / False · 1점]**

> Cells produce CO2 continuously as a waste product of metabolism.

**◇ Diagram — What can cross a bare lipid bilayer?.** A bare lipid bilayer lets small gases (O2, CO2), nonpolar molecules such as lipids, and small uncharged polar water cross by simple diffusion, but blocks charged ions such as Na+, K+ and H+, which need channels or pumps, and large macromolecules such as proteins and carbohydrates, which need bulk transport.

**Claim:** CO2 leaves the cell by simple diffusion, without a transport protein, and its net movement stays outward as long as its concentration inside the cell is higher than outside.

**Answer: True**

Small gases like CO2 cross the bilayer directly. Net diffusion runs from high to low concentration and continues while the gradient exists — and producing CO2 inside keeps that gradient going.

*Concepts linked:* Simple diffusion · Concentration gradient

---

**Q58. [True / False · 1점]**

> A membrane permeable to urea separates two compartments. After an hour, the urea concentration is the same on both sides.

**Claim:** At this point, urea molecules have stopped crossing the membrane.

**Answer: False**

Only the net movement stops. Random motion continues in both directions, so influx and efflux still happen — they are simply equal.

*Concepts linked:* Diffusion equilibrium · Influx and efflux

---

**Q59. [True / False · 1점]**

> Aquaporins are channel proteins that let water cross membranes much faster than through the bilayer alone. When the body needs to conserve water, kidney cells insert more aquaporins into their membranes.

**◇ Diagram — Four ways across a membrane.** Four ways across a membrane, each with solute dots crowded on one side: a solute crossing straight through the bilayer, a solute passing through a channel protein&#x27;s aqueous pore, and a solute carried by a protein that binds it and changes shape each move from the crowded outside to the sparse inside; a pump that converts ATP to ADP + Pi moves its solute from the sparse inside to the crowded outside.

**Claim:** With enough aquaporins, kidney cells can move water against its concentration gradient back into the body.

**Answer: False**

A channel only provides an aqueous pore — facilitated diffusion. Water still moves by osmosis, down its own concentration gradient (toward the side with more solute); aquaporins change the rate, not the direction.

*Concepts linked:* Channel proteins · Osmosis · Real-world: kidney water conservation

---

**Q60. [True / False · 2점]**

> In cystic fibrosis, a chloride-ion channel in airway cells fails to work, so fewer Cl- ions move out of the cells into the thin fluid layer that lines the airways. Patients' airway mucus becomes thick and sticky.

**Claim:** The thick mucus is explained by osmosis: with less solute moving into the airway fluid, less water follows it out of the cells.

**Answer: True**

Water moves toward the side with more solute (lower water concentration). When Cl- secretion fails, the airway fluid gains less solute, so less water leaves the cells and the mucus dehydrates.

*Concepts linked:* Ion channels · Osmosis · Real-world: cystic fibrosis

---

**Q61. [True / False · 1점]**

> Paramecium, a single-celled protist with no cell wall, lives in pond water that is hypotonic to its cytoplasm.

**◇ Diagram — Same solution, different outcomes: animal vs. plant cells.** Grid of an animal cell (no wall) and a plant cell (with a wall) in three solutions: more solute outside, equal solute, and less solute outside. Arrows show net water movement out of the cells, balanced in both directions, and into the cells respectively, and each cell is drawn with the shape it takes in that solution.

**Claim:** Without a way to pump out the water that constantly enters, Paramecium would swell and burst, like a red blood cell in pure water.

**Answer: True**

In a hypotonic solution water keeps entering by osmosis; with no wall to resist the pressure, the cell would lyse — so wall-less freshwater protists must actively expel water (with contractile vacuoles).

*Concepts linked:* Hypotonic environment · Lysis · Real-world: freshwater protists

---

**Q62. [True / False · 1점]**

> Glucose enters most body cells through GLUT carrier proteins, always moving from higher to lower glucose concentration.

**◇ Diagram — Four ways across a membrane.** Four ways across a membrane, each with solute dots crowded on one side: a solute crossing straight through the bilayer, a solute passing through a channel protein&#x27;s aqueous pore, and a solute carried by a protein that binds it and changes shape each move from the crowded outside to the sparse inside; a pump that converts ATP to ADP + Pi moves its solute from the sparse inside to the crowded outside.

**Claim:** Because a protein is required, this glucose uptake is powered by ATP.

**Answer: False**

Needing a protein doesn't settle it — facilitated-diffusion carriers and ATP-driven pumps are both proteins. Direction does: GLUT moves glucose down its gradient, so this is facilitated diffusion, which is passive and uses no ATP.

*Concepts linked:* Facilitated diffusion · Passive vs active

---

**Q63. [True / False · 2점]**

> Solution A is hypertonic to red blood cells, and red blood cells are hypertonic to solution B.

**Claim:** If A and B were separated by a membrane permeable only to water, net water flow would be from B into A.

**Answer: True**

Solute concentrations rank A > cells > B. Water moves from the lower-solute (higher-water) side toward the higher-solute side, so from B into A. Tonicity is always relative to something.

*Concepts linked:* Tonicity is relative · Osmosis

---

**Q64. [Multiple Choice · 1점]**

A radioactive tracer shows that 500 molecules of solute X enter a cell each second while 200 leave each second. Which description is correct?

① Influx is 500/s, efflux is 200/s, and the net flux is 700/s into the cell　 ② Influx is 500/s, efflux is 200/s, and the net flux is 300/s into the cell　 ③ Influx is 200/s, efflux is 500/s, and the net flux is 300/s out of the cell　 ④ There is no net flux, because molecules are moving in both directions　 ⑤ The net flux is 300/s out of the cell, since efflux always opposes influx

**Answer: ② Influx is 500/s, efflux is 200/s, and the net flux is 300/s into the cell**

Influx = movement in, efflux = movement out, and net flux = the difference when one exceeds the other: 500 − 200 = 300/s inward.

*Concepts linked:* Influx · Efflux · Net flux

---

**Q65. [Multiple Choice · 1점]**

Lettuce left in a salty dressing turns limp. What happened in its cells?

① The dressing is hypotonic, so water rushed into the cells until their walls burst　 ② Salt entered the cells by simple diffusion, making the cells swell and then collapse　 ③ The dressing is isotonic, so the limpness must come from the vinegar dissolving the walls　 ④ Water left the cells (hypertonic dressing), so their membranes pulled off the walls: plasmolysis　 ⑤ The dressing is hypertonic, so the cells shrivelled by crenation, just as red blood cells do

**Answer: ④ Water left the cells (hypertonic dressing), so their membranes pulled off the walls: plasmolysis**

Higher solute outside (hypertonic) pulls water out by osmosis. In plant cells the membrane pulls away from the wall (plasmolysis) and turgor is lost; 'crenation' is the term for animal cells, which have no wall.

*Concepts linked:* Hypertonic solution · Plasmolysis vs crenation · Real-world: wilting salad

---

**Q66. [Multiple Choice · 2점]**

A cell whose internal solute concentration is 0.3 M is placed in a 0.5 M solution of a solute that cannot cross its membrane. Which statement is correct?

① The solution is hypertonic, and water leaves the cell because the water concentration is higher inside the cell than outside　 ② The solution is hypertonic, and water leaves the cell because water moves from high solute concentration to low solute concentration　 ③ The solution is hypotonic, and water enters the cell because the cell's solute concentration is lower　 ④ The solute diffuses in until both sides reach 0.4 M, so no water moves　 ⑤ No water moves until the cell uses ATP to pump the solute out

**Answer: ① The solution is hypertonic, and water leaves the cell because the water concentration is higher inside the cell than outside**

More solute outside means less free water outside; water diffuses from higher [H2O] (inside) to lower [H2O] (outside) — that is, toward the higher solute concentration. The solute can't cross, so only water moves.

*Concepts linked:* Osmosis as water diffusion · Tonicity
