### Active Transport

**Q67. [보기 Combination · 3점] A heart drug that slows a pump**

Digoxin, used to treat some heart conditions, partially blocks the Na+/K+ pump in heart muscle cells. Using the diagram, which statements in <보기> are correct?

**◇ Diagram — The Na⁺/K⁺ pump cycle: 3 Na⁺ out, 2 K⁺ in per ATP.** Four-step cycle of the Na+/K+ pump; the outside stays high in Na+ and the inside high in K+. Step 1: three Na+ bind from the cytoplasm while the pump is open inward. Step 2: ATP phosphorylates the pump (ATP to ADP), which opens outward and releases the Na+ outside. Step 3: two K+ bind from outside while the pump is still phosphorylated. Step 4: the phosphate is released, the pump resets to face inward, and the K+ enter the cytoplasm.

> **(가)** The Na+/K+ pump moves 3 Na+ out and 2 K+ in per ATP, keeping Na+ low inside the cell.
>
> **(나)** Heart cells also have a Na+/Ca2+ antiporter: as Na+ flows in down its gradient, it pushes Ca2+ out of the cell.

**<보기>**
- ㄱ. With fewer working pumps, Na+ would slowly build up inside the heart cells.
- ㄴ. Digoxin would therefore let Ca2+ accumulate inside the heart cells, because the weakened Na+ gradient powers less Ca2+ removal.
- ㄷ. Since the antiporter uses no ATP directly, it would keep removing Ca2+ at its normal rate however much the pump is blocked.

① ㄴ　 ② ㄱ, ㄴ　 ③ ㄱ, ㄷ　 ④ ㄴ, ㄷ　 ⑤ ㄱ, ㄴ, ㄷ

**Answer: ② ㄱ, ㄴ**
- ㄱ — **O** — The pump is what keeps intracellular Na+ low; slowing it lets the Na+ that leaks in accumulate.
- ㄴ — **O** — The antiporter is secondary active transport driven by the Na+ gradient; a smaller gradient pushes out less Ca2+, and the extra Ca2+ strengthens contraction.
- ㄷ — **X** — Secondary active transport runs on the gradient that primary active transport builds; weaken the pump and everything that depends on its gradient weakens too.

*Concepts linked:* Na+/K+ pump · Secondary active transport · Antiporter · Real-world: digoxin

---

**Q68. [보기 Combination · 3점] Two forces on every ion**

The diagram shows the two forces on Na+ and on K+ in a resting animal cell, whose inside is negative relative to the outside. Apply the same reasoning to other ions and conditions. Which statements in <보기> are correct?

**◇ Diagram — Two forces on an ion: concentration and charge.** A membrane whose outside (ECF) is the positive side and rich in Na+, and whose inside (ICF) is the negative side and rich in K+. For Na+, a solid arrow (concentration) and a dashed arrow (charge) both point into the cell. For K+, the solid concentration arrow points out of the cell and the dashed charge arrow points in.

> **(가)** Ca2+ is far more concentrated outside the cell than inside, and so is Cl-.
>
> **(나)** For a brief moment, the inside of the cell becomes positive relative to the outside.

**<보기>**
- ㄱ. When Ca2+ channels open in a resting cell, Ca2+ rushes in, because both its concentration gradient and the charge difference push it inward.
- ㄴ. For Cl- in a resting cell, the charge difference also pulls it inward, adding to its concentration gradient.
- ㄷ. During (나), the charge force on Na+ would point outward, so the total inward push on Na+ would be weaker than at rest.

① ㄴ　 ② ㄷ　 ③ ㄱ, ㄴ　 ④ ㄱ, ㄷ　 ⑤ ㄴ, ㄷ

**Answer: ④ ㄱ, ㄷ**
- ㄱ — **O** — Like Na+, Ca2+ is concentrated outside (chemical force inward) and positively charged, so the negative interior attracts it (electrical force inward); the forces add.
- ㄴ — **X** — Like charges repel: a negative interior pushes the anion outward, opposing its inward concentration gradient.
- ㄷ — **O** — With the inside positive, the electrical force now repels Na+; only its concentration gradient still pushes it in, so the total inward drive shrinks.

*Concepts linked:* Electrochemical gradient · Chemical vs electrical force · Charge of the ion

---

**Q69. [보기 Combination · 3점] Why oral rehydration solution contains sugar**

Cholera causes severe diarrhea, but the intestinal Na+/glucose symporter keeps working. Using the diagram, which statements in <보기> are correct?

**◇ Diagram — Glucose absorption across an intestinal cell.** Intestinal cell absorbing glucose. The gut lumen has high Na+ and low glucose; inside the cell Na+ is kept low and glucose builds up; the blood has less glucose than the cell. At the apical membrane a Na+/glucose symporter (secondary active) carries Na+ and glucose into the cell together. Glucose diffuses across the cell and leaves across the basal membrane through a glucose uniporter (facilitated diffusion). On the basal side a Na+/K+ pump converts ATP to ADP + Pi while moving 3 Na+ out and 2 K+ in.

> **(가)** Oral rehydration solution (ORS) — water with both Na+ and glucose — has saved millions of cholera patients.
>
> **(나)** Plain water, or salt water without sugar, rehydrates cholera patients much less effectively.

**<보기>**
- ㄱ. The symporter hydrolyzes ATP to move glucose against its gradient.
- ㄴ. ORS works because Na+ and glucose absorbed together through the symporter raise the solute concentration inside intestinal cells, and water follows by osmosis.
- ㄷ. If the basal Na+/K+ pumps stopped, glucose uptake from the gut would eventually fail even though the symporter itself uses no ATP.

① ㄱ　 ② ㄷ　 ③ ㄱ, ㄴ　 ④ ㄴ, ㄷ　 ⑤ ㄱ, ㄴ, ㄷ

**Answer: ④ ㄴ, ㄷ**
- ㄱ — **X** — It is secondary active transport: Na+ flowing down its gradient drives glucose uphill. The ATP is spent by the basal Na+/K+ pump.
- ㄴ — **O** — Moving solute into the cells (and on into the blood) creates the osmotic pull that draws water out of the gut.
- ㄷ — **O** — The pump keeps Na+ low inside the cell; without it the Na+ gradient runs down and the symporter loses its driving force.

*Concepts linked:* Na+/glucose symport · Primary vs secondary active · Osmosis · Real-world: oral rehydration therapy

---

**Q70. [Matching · 3점] Who moves what, and on whose energy?**

Match each transport protein to its correct classification.

**Transport protein**
A. Na+/glucose transporter (apical side, intestine)
B. Na+/H+ exchanger (animal-cell plasma membrane)
C. Glucose transporter (basal side, intestine)
D. Na+/K+-ATPase
E. Lysosomal proton pump

**Classification**
1. Primary active pump for a single solute — ATP moves H+ into an organelle against its gradient
2. Secondary active antiporter — solutes move opposite ways; the Na+ gradient powers it
3. Primary active antiporter — ATP hydrolysis moves two ions in opposite directions
4. Uniporter — one solute moving down its gradient (facilitated diffusion)
5. Secondary active symporter — both solutes move the same way; the Na+ gradient powers it

**Correct matches:**
- A = Na+/glucose transporter (apical side, intestine) → Secondary active symporter — both solutes move the same way; the Na+ gradient powers it
- B = Na+/H+ exchanger (animal-cell plasma membrane) → Secondary active antiporter — solutes move opposite ways; the Na+ gradient powers it
- C = Glucose transporter (basal side, intestine) → Uniporter — one solute moving down its gradient (facilitated diffusion)
- D = Na+/K+-ATPase → Primary active antiporter — ATP hydrolysis moves two ions in opposite directions
- E = Lysosomal proton pump → Primary active pump for a single solute — ATP moves H+ into an organelle against its gradient

Every transporter gets an energy word (primary, secondary, passive) for what powers it; direction words say whether it moves one solute (uniport) or two in the same (symport) or opposite (antiport) directions.

*Concepts linked:* Symport · Antiport · Uniport · Primary vs secondary

---

**Q71. [True / False · 2점]**

> A mutant Na+/K+ pump can be phosphorylated by ATP (step 2 in the diagram) but can never release its phosphate (step 4).

**◇ Diagram — The Na⁺/K⁺ pump cycle: 3 Na⁺ out, 2 K⁺ in per ATP.** Four-step cycle of the Na+/K+ pump; the outside stays high in Na+ and the inside high in K+. Step 1: three Na+ bind from the cytoplasm while the pump is open inward. Step 2: ATP phosphorylates the pump (ATP to ADP), which opens outward and releases the Na+ outside. Step 3: two K+ bind from outside while the pump is still phosphorylated. Step 4: the phosphate is released, the pump resets to face inward, and the K+ enter the cytoplasm.

**Claim:** The mutant pump will release Na+ outside and bind K+, but it can never carry K+ into the cell or begin another cycle.

**Answer: True**

Phosphorylation opens the pump outward (Na+ out, K+ binds). Releasing the phosphate is what snaps the pump back to face inward and releases K+ inside; without it, the cycle stops after step 3.

*Concepts linked:* Na+/K+ pump cycle · Conformational change

---

**Q72. [True / False · 1점]**

> Plant cells, like animal cells, maintain ion gradients across their plasma membranes.

**Claim:** Plant cells build these gradients with proton (H+) pumps rather than a Na+/K+ pump, which is found only in animal cells.

**Answer: True**

The Na+/K+ pump occurs only in animal cells. Plant cells use ATP-driven proton pumps, and the resulting H+ gradient powers their secondary active transport.

*Concepts linked:* Na+/K+ pump · Proton pumps

---

**Q73. [True / False · 1점]**

> A plant cell uses ATP to pump H+ out across its plasma membrane.

**◇ Diagram — Two forces on an ion: concentration and charge.** A membrane whose outside (ECF) is the positive side and rich in Na+, and whose inside (ICF) is the negative side and rich in K+. For Na+, a solid arrow (concentration) and a dashed arrow (charge) both point into the cell. For K+, the solid concentration arrow points out of the cell and the dashed charge arrow points in.

**Claim:** The H+ gradient it builds stores energy in its concentration difference but not in any charge difference across the membrane.

**Answer: False**

H+ carries a positive charge, so pumping it builds a charge difference as well as a concentration difference — an electrochemical gradient, which can then drive secondary active transport.

*Concepts linked:* Proton pump · Electrochemical gradient

---

**Q74. [True / False · 1점]**

> A carrier protein for facilitated diffusion and an active transporter both bind their solute and change shape.

**Claim:** Because both work by changing shape, both would be expected to move their solute from higher to lower concentration.

**Answer: False**

Only the carrier follows the gradient. An active transporter couples its shape change to an energy source, so it moves its solute in one direction — against the gradient.

*Concepts linked:* Carrier vs active transporter

---

**Q75. [True / False · 2점]**

> Cyanide stops mitochondria from making ATP. In a poisoned cell, the Na+/K+ pumps slow, and the Na+ and K+ gradients across the plasma membrane gradually run down.

**Claim:** Secondary active transport in this cell would also decline, even though secondary transporters never use ATP themselves.

**Answer: True**

Secondary transporters run on ion gradients that primary (ATP-driven) pumps build. When ATP runs out, the gradients fade — and so does everything they drive.

*Concepts linked:* Primary vs secondary active transport · Real-world: cyanide poisoning

---

**Q76. [True / False · 2점]**

> The Na+ and K+ gradients across an animal cell's membrane always favour Na+ moving in and K+ moving out, yet the Na+/K+ pump steadily moves Na+ out and K+ in.

**Claim:** What keeps the pump's cycle running in this direction is its coupling to ATP: phosphorylation and phosphate release force its shape changes to occur in one fixed order.

**Answer: True**

An active transporter changes conformation when it binds its solutes; here each change is tied to ATP — phosphorylation after Na+ binds inside, phosphate release after K+ binds outside — so the cycle runs one way, uphill for both ions. ATP hydrolysis supplies more energy than the gradients store.

*Concepts linked:* Active transporters move solutes one way · Na+/K+ pump cycle

---

**Q77. [Multiple Choice · 2점]**

Based on the diagram, what is the net movement of positive charge across the plasma membrane for each ATP the Na+/K+ pump uses?

**◇ Diagram — The Na⁺/K⁺ pump cycle: 3 Na⁺ out, 2 K⁺ in per ATP.** Four-step cycle of the Na+/K+ pump; the outside stays high in Na+ and the inside high in K+. Step 1: three Na+ bind from the cytoplasm while the pump is open inward. Step 2: ATP phosphorylates the pump (ATP to ADP), which opens outward and releases the Na+ outside. Step 3: two K+ bind from outside while the pump is still phosphorylated. Step 4: the phosphate is released, the pump resets to face inward, and the K+ enter the cytoplasm.

① Five positive charges move out of the cell　 ② One positive charge moves into the cell　 ③ No net charge moves, since Na+ and K+ both carry +1　 ④ Two positive charges move out of the cell　 ⑤ One positive charge moves out of the cell

**Answer: ⑤ One positive charge moves out of the cell**

Each cycle moves 3 Na+ out and 2 K+ in: 3 − 2 = one net positive charge out, which helps make the inside negative relative to the outside.

*Concepts linked:* Pump stoichiometry · Electrochemical gradient

---

**Q78. [Multiple Choice · 2점]**

In the diagram, glucose enters the intestinal cell through a symporter coupled to Na+ but leaves across the basal membrane through a uniporter. Why can the exit step be passive?

**◇ Diagram — Glucose absorption across an intestinal cell.** Intestinal cell absorbing glucose. The gut lumen has high Na+ and low glucose; inside the cell Na+ is kept low and glucose builds up; the blood has less glucose than the cell. At the apical membrane a Na+/glucose symporter (secondary active) carries Na+ and glucose into the cell together. Glucose diffuses across the cell and leaves across the basal membrane through a glucose uniporter (facilitated diffusion). On the basal side a Na+/K+ pump converts ATP to ADP + Pi while moving 3 Na+ out and 2 K+ in.

① The basal uniporter hydrolyzes ATP itself, so the exit step is really a form of active transport　 ② Cell glucose exceeds blood glucose, so exit is downhill; lumen glucose is lower, so entry is uphill　 ③ Glucose is nonpolar, so it diffuses freely through the basal membrane without any protein　 ④ Na+ in the blood drives glucose out of the cell through the basal uniporter by symport　 ⑤ The apical membrane has no transport proteins, so only the basal exit step needs one

**Answer: ② Cell glucose exceeds blood glucose, so exit is downhill; lumen glucose is lower, so entry is uphill**

Direction relative to the gradient decides the mechanism: lumen (low) → cell (high) needs secondary active transport, while cell (high) → blood (lower) is facilitated diffusion through a uniporter.

*Concepts linked:* Symporter vs uniporter · Facilitated diffusion · Glucose absorption

---

**Q79. [Multiple Choice · 2점]**

A bacterium uses ATP to pump H+ out of the cell. A drug-efflux antiporter in the same membrane lets H+ flow back in while pushing an antibiotic out — a common cause of antibiotic resistance. Which change would most directly stop the antiporter from expelling the antibiotic?

① Adding more glucose to the medium so the bacterium can make more ATP　 ② Raising the antibiotic concentration outside the bacterial cell　 ③ Blocking the bacterium's ribosomes for a few seconds with another drug　 ④ Making the membrane leaky to H+, so the H+ gradient collapses　 ⑤ Adding a second antibiotic that targets the cell wall

**Answer: ④ Making the membrane leaky to H+, so the H+ gradient collapses**

The antiporter is secondary active transport: its energy comes from H+ moving down its electrochemical gradient. Remove the gradient, and the drug can no longer be pushed out against its own gradient.

*Concepts linked:* Proton gradient · Antiporter · Real-world: antibiotic efflux pumps

---

**Q80. [Multiple Choice · 2점]**

Each cycle of the Na+/K+ pump removes one more ion than it brings in, offsetting the Na+ that constantly leaks into an animal cell. What would happen to the volume of a cell whose pumps were completely blocked by the poison ouabain?

① It would shrink, because K+ stays trapped inside and draws water out of the cell　 ② It would stay the same size, because Na+ and K+ carry the same positive charge　 ③ It would swell: Na+ that leaks in accumulates, so water follows it in by osmosis　 ④ It would shrink, because a blocked pump can no longer let water into the cell　 ⑤ It would burst at once, because the pump is also the cell's main route for water

**Answer: ③ It would swell: Na+ that leaks in accumulates, so water follows it in by osmosis**

Na+ (with Cl- following) keeps leaking in but is no longer pumped out, so internal solute rises; water follows by osmosis and the cell swells. The pump is part of how wall-less animal cells control their volume.

*Concepts linked:* Na+/K+ pump · Osmosis · Cell volume

### Bulk Transport

**Q81. [보기 Combination · 3점] When LDL can't get in**

LDL particles carry cholesterol in the blood and are taken into cells by receptor-mediated endocytosis. In familial hypercholesterolemia, the LDL receptor is defective, and blood cholesterol stays very high. Using the diagram, which statements in <보기> are correct?

**◇ Diagram — Bulk transport: three ways in, one way out.** Four bulk transport modes drawn side by side. Phagocytosis: the membrane wraps around a large particle, forming a vesicle next to a smaller one. Pinocytosis: the membrane dips in around a little extracellular fluid, forming a small vesicle. Receptor-mediated endocytosis: receptors holding cargo sit in a pit with a dashed coat on its cytoplasmic side, forming a coated vesicle. Exocytosis: a vesicle moves to the membrane and its contents appear outside the cell.

> **(가)** Normal cells gather LDL receptors in coated pits and take LDL in efficiently, even at low blood levels.
>
> **(나)** Patients' cells can still take up small amounts of LDL.

**<보기>**
- ㄱ. The small uptake in (나) could occur by pinocytosis, which brings in whatever molecules are dissolved in the engulfed fluid without recognizing them.
- ㄴ. In normal cells, LDL taken in through coated pits is delivered first to early endosomes near the cell periphery, then to late endosomes deeper inside.
- ㄷ. Clathrin on the outer face of the coated pit is what recognizes and binds LDL.

① ㄴ　 ② ㄷ　 ③ ㄱ, ㄴ　 ④ ㄴ, ㄷ　 ⑤ ㄱ, ㄴ, ㄷ

**Answer: ③ ㄱ, ㄴ**
- ㄱ — **O** — Pinocytosis is nonspecific 'cell drinking', so some LDL enters with the fluid — far less efficiently than through concentrated receptors.
- ㄴ — **O** — Receptor-mediated cargo goes into a network of endosomes: early (peripheral), then late (interior), which also receive material from the Golgi.
- ㄷ — **X** — Receptors on the extracellular side bind LDL; clathrin coats the cytoplasmic side and helps bring the vesicle in.

*Concepts linked:* Receptor-mediated endocytosis · Clathrin · Endosomes · Real-world: familial hypercholesterolemia

---

**Q82. [보기 Combination · 2점] Releasing messages by the vesicle-load**

Which statements in <보기> about exocytosis are correct?

> **(가)** Nerve cells release chemical messengers (neurotransmitters) stored in vesicles by exocytosis.
>
> **(나)** During intense activity, a single nerve terminal can release hundreds of vesicles per second.

**<보기>**
- ㄱ. The messengers reach the outside of the cell without the cytosol ever being exposed to the extracellular fluid.
- ㄴ. A nerve cell releasing many vesicles would steadily gain plasma-membrane area unless it took membrane back in by endocytosis.
- ㄷ. Vesicles that carry secreted proteins to the cell surface bud from the Golgi, part of the endomembrane system.

① ㄴ　 ② ㄱ, ㄴ　 ③ ㄱ, ㄷ　 ④ ㄴ, ㄷ　 ⑤ ㄱ, ㄴ, ㄷ

**Answer: ⑤ ㄱ, ㄴ, ㄷ**
- ㄱ — **O** — Fusion keeps membranes continuous; only the vesicle's interior opens to the outside.
- ㄴ — **O** — Each fusion adds membrane; cells balance this by retrieving membrane through endocytosis.
- ㄷ — **O** — Exocytosis delivers the contents of vesicles that bud off the endomembrane system into the extracellular space.

*Concepts linked:* Exocytosis · Membrane continuity · Real-world: neurotransmitter release

---

**Q83. [Matching · 3점] Disarming a phagocyte**

Each neutrophil below has one defect. Match each defect to its most direct consequence.

**Defect**
A. No surface receptors for a particular bacterium
B. Phagosomes that cannot fuse with lysosomes
C. Lysosomes lacking lysozyme
D. Phagosomes lacking oxidases on their inner surface
E. Lysosomes whose proton pump has failed

**Most direct consequence**
1. The bacterium is never recognized, so it isn't engulfed
2. No H2O2 is made to kill bacteria
3. Bacterial cell walls are not broken down
4. Microorganisms normally killed by acidic pH survive
5. Bacteria are enclosed but never exposed to hydrolytic enzymes

**Correct matches:**
- A = No surface receptors for a particular bacterium → The bacterium is never recognized, so it isn't engulfed
- B = Phagosomes that cannot fuse with lysosomes → Bacteria are enclosed but never exposed to hydrolytic enzymes
- C = Lysosomes lacking lysozyme → Bacterial cell walls are not broken down
- D = Phagosomes lacking oxidases on their inner surface → No H2O2 is made to kill bacteria
- E = Lysosomes whose proton pump has failed → Microorganisms normally killed by acidic pH survive

Phagocytic defense is a chain — recognition, engulfment into a phagosome, fusion with a lysosome, then killing by lysozyme, acidic pH, and H2O2. Breaking any one link leaves a specific gap; in single-celled organisms, the same pathway provides nutrition.

*Concepts linked:* Phagocytosis · Lysosomes · Immune defense

---

**Q84. [Matching · 2점] Which way in or out?**

Match each scenario to the bulk-transport process it uses.

**Scenario**
A. A macrophage engulfs a worn-out red blood cell and digests it
B. A cell samples the surrounding fluid, taking in whatever solutes it contains
C. A liver cell captures cholesterol-carrying LDL particles through clathrin-coated pits
D. A pancreatic cell releases insulin stored in vesicles

**Process**
1. Exocytosis
2. Pinocytosis
3. Phagocytosis
4. Receptor-mediated endocytosis

**Correct matches:**
- A = A macrophage engulfs a worn-out red blood cell and digests it → Phagocytosis
- B = A cell samples the surrounding fluid, taking in whatever solutes it contains → Pinocytosis
- C = A liver cell captures cholesterol-carrying LDL particles through clathrin-coated pits → Receptor-mediated endocytosis
- D = A pancreatic cell releases insulin stored in vesicles → Exocytosis

Ask what is moving and whether it is recognized: large recognized particles → phagocytosis; fluid with no recognition → pinocytosis; specific cargo through receptors and clathrin → receptor-mediated endocytosis; outward delivery by vesicle fusion → exocytosis.

*Concepts linked:* Bulk transport modes

---

**Q85. [True / False · 2점]**

> Neutrophils engulf bacteria by phagocytosis, and the phagosome then fuses with a lysosome whose contents include lysozyme. The antibiotic penicillin kills bacteria by blocking the construction of their cell walls.

**◇ Diagram — Bulk transport: three ways in, one way out.** Four bulk transport modes drawn side by side. Phagocytosis: the membrane wraps around a large particle, forming a vesicle next to a smaller one. Pinocytosis: the membrane dips in around a little extracellular fluid, forming a small vesicle. Receptor-mediated endocytosis: receptors holding cargo sit in a pit with a dashed coat on its cytoplasmic side, forming a coated vesicle. Exocytosis: a vesicle moves to the membrane and its contents appear outside the cell.

**Claim:** Lysozyme attacks the same bacterial structure that penicillin targets.

**Answer: True**

Lysozyme degrades bacterial cell walls, while penicillin blocks wall construction. Human cells have no wall, so both strategies hit bacteria without harming our own cells.

*Concepts linked:* Phagocytosis · Lysozyme · Bacterial cell wall

---

**Q86. [True / False · 1점]**

> A cell takes up a fluorescent dye by endocytosis. Moments later, the dye appears inside the cell as small bright dots.

**Claim:** At that moment, the dye is still separated from the cytosol by a membrane that came from the plasma membrane.

**Answer: True**

Endocytosis never breaks membrane continuity: the infolded membrane becomes the vesicle wall, so the cargo stays enclosed — still 'outside' the cytosol.

*Concepts linked:* Endocytosis · Membrane continuity

---

**Q87. [True / False · 1점]**

> Pinocytic vesicles are smaller than phagosomes and carry dissolved molecules rather than whole cells.

**Claim:** Like phagocytosis, pinocytosis begins when the plasma membrane recognizes a specific target.

**Answer: False**

Pinocytosis takes up extracellular fluid without recognition — whatever is dissolved comes in. Recognition through receptors is a feature of phagocytosis and receptor-mediated endocytosis.

*Concepts linked:* Pinocytosis vs phagocytosis

---

**Q88. [True / False · 2점]**

> A newly made lysosomal enzyme leaves the Golgi apparatus. Minutes earlier, LDL particles were taken into the same cell by receptor-mediated endocytosis.

**Claim:** The enzyme and the LDL could meet in late endosomes, which receive material both from early endosomes and from the Golgi apparatus.

**Answer: True**

Endocytosed cargo passes from early endosomes (near the periphery) to late endosomes (deeper inside), which also receive material from the Golgi — a meeting point of the inward and outward routes.

*Concepts linked:* Early and late endosomes · Golgi delivery

---

**Q89. [True / False · 1점]**

> A macrophage takes in material by endocytosis many times a minute.

**Claim:** Each round of endocytosis removes a little membrane from the cell surface — the reverse of what exocytosis does.

**Answer: True**

The plasma membrane folds inward and pinches off to form each vesicle, so endocytosis takes surface membrane into the cell, while exocytosis adds vesicle membrane back; busy cells balance the two.

*Concepts linked:* Endocytosis · Exocytosis · Membrane balance

---

**Q90. [Multiple Choice · 1점]**

A cell takes in a dissolved dye for which it has no receptor. Which process brings the dye in, and must the resulting vesicle fuse with a lysosome?

① Phagocytosis; yes — every engulfed particle is digested　 ② Receptor-mediated endocytosis; yes — cargo always goes to lysosomes first　 ③ Pinocytosis; yes — fluid can only be released after digestion　 ④ Exocytosis; no — the dye enters by vesicle fusion　 ⑤ Pinocytosis; no — the small vesicle need not merge with a lysosome

**Answer: ⑤ Pinocytosis; no — the small vesicle need not merge with a lysosome**

Without a receptor, uptake is nonspecific pinocytosis ('cell drinking'), which forms small vesicles that don't need to merge with a lysosome; phagocytosis is for large, recognized particles.

*Concepts linked:* Pinocytosis · Phagocytosis · Endocytosis

---

**Q91. [Multiple Choice · 2점]**

Vitamin B12 travels in the blood bound to a carrier protein at very low concentration, yet cells take it in efficiently and selectively. Which feature best explains this?

① Receptors that bind the carrier gather in coated pits, capturing it even from dilute fluid　 ② Pinocytosis takes in extracellular fluid, so it collects the vitamin in proportion to its concentration　 ③ The vitamin–carrier complex is small enough to diffuse straight across the lipid bilayer　 ④ Phagocytosis engulfs the vitamin–carrier complexes as large, recognized particles　 ⑤ Exocytosis pulls the complex into the cell each time a vesicle fuses with the membrane

**Answer: ① Receptors that bind the carrier gather in coated pits, capturing it even from dilute fluid**

Receptor-mediated endocytosis concentrates specific cargo: receptors gather in clathrin-coated pits, so the cell takes up far more of the target than nonspecific pinocytosis would at that concentration.

*Concepts linked:* Receptor-mediated endocytosis · Coated pits · Real-world: vitamin uptake

## Module 4 — Nucleic Acids and Information Flow

### DNA: Evidence & Structure

**Q92. [보기 Combination · 3점] What the mice and the enzymes proved**

Using the diagram, which statements in <보기> are correct?

**◇ Diagram — Finding the genetic material: Griffith (1928) and Avery (1944).** Griffith&#x27;s experiment: mice injected with live virulent Streptococcus pneumoniae die; with live nonvirulent bacteria they live; with heat-killed virulent bacteria they live; with heat-killed virulent plus live nonvirulent bacteria they die and live virulent cells are recovered, so something from the dead cells transformed the live ones. Avery, MacLeod and McCarty treated an extract of heat-killed virulent cells with nothing, RNase (destroys RNA), protease (destroys protein) or DNase (destroys DNA) before mixing it with live nonvirulent cells: it still transformed them after RNase or protease, but not after DNase, so DNA carries the genetic information.

> **(가)** Griffith, 1928: heat-killed virulent bacteria alone do not kill a mouse, but mixed with live nonvirulent bacteria they do, and live virulent bacteria are recovered from the mouse.
>
> **(나)** Avery, MacLeod and McCarty, 1944: an extract of heat-killed virulent cells still transforms nonvirulent cells after RNase or protease treatment, but not after DNase treatment.

**<보기>**
- ㄱ. In (가), the virulent cells recovered from the mouse must have come from live nonvirulent cells that picked up something from the dead virulent cells.
- ㄴ. Griffith's experiment by itself identified DNA as the transforming molecule.
- ㄷ. If the DNase-treated extract in (나) had still transformed the cells, DNA could not have been the molecule required for transformation.

① ㄷ　 ② ㄱ, ㄴ　 ③ ㄱ, ㄷ　 ④ ㄴ, ㄷ　 ⑤ ㄱ, ㄴ, ㄷ

**Answer: ③ ㄱ, ㄷ**
- ㄱ — **O** — Dead cells can't revive, and nonvirulent cells alone are harmless; the only source of live virulent cells is nonvirulent cells transformed by a molecule in the debris.
- ㄴ — **X** — Griffith showed that some molecule in the debris carried the genetic information for virulence, but it wasn't identified until Avery's enzyme experiments. (The notes' opening line credits Griffith with showing that DNA is the genetic material; strictly, his experiment showed only that something transforms, and Avery's team identified it as DNA.)
- ㄷ — **O** — The logic is elimination: destroying the true genetic material must block transformation, and only destroying DNA did.

*Concepts linked:* Griffith's transformation · Avery's enzyme experiment · Experimental logic

---

**Q93. [보기 Combination · 3점] Chargaff by the numbers**

Two double-stranded DNA samples of equal length are analyzed. Using the diagram, which statements in <보기> are correct?

**◇ Diagram — The DNA double helix, unrolled into a ladder.** DNA drawn as a ladder of four base pairs between two sugar-phosphate backbones. The left strand runs 5′ to 3′ from top to bottom and the right strand runs 5′ to 3′ from bottom to top, so the strands are antiparallel. Rungs from top: A with T, G with C, T with A, C with G. Dashed hydrogen bonds: two in each A-T pair and three in each G-C pair. Every rung pairs a two-ring purine with a one-ring pyrimidine. The helix is right-handed and 2 nm wide, with 3.4 nm and 10 base pairs per turn, and is stabilized by hydrogen bonds plus base stacking.

> **(가)** Sample 1 is 30% adenine.
>
> **(나)** Sample 2 is 60% G + C.

**<보기>**
- ㄱ. Chargaff's rule %A = %T would hold just as well for the genome of a virus made of single-stranded DNA.
- ㄴ. Sample 1 is 20% guanine.
- ㄷ. Sample 2 would need more heat than sample 1 to separate its strands.

① ㄴ　 ② ㄱ, ㄴ　 ③ ㄱ, ㄷ　 ④ ㄴ, ㄷ　 ⑤ ㄱ, ㄴ, ㄷ

**Answer: ④ ㄴ, ㄷ**
- ㄱ — **X** — The rule comes from complementary pairing between two strands; a single strand has no partner forcing A to equal T.
- ㄴ — **O** — %A = %T = 30%, so A + T = 60% and G + C = 40%; since %G = %C, guanine is 20%.
- ㄷ — **O** — Sample 1 is only 40% G + C. Each G–C pair has three hydrogen bonds versus two for A–T, so G–C-rich DNA holds together more strongly.

*Concepts linked:* Chargaff's rules · Hydrogen bonds per base pair · Complementary pairing

---

**Q94. [보기 Combination · 3점] Reading the ladder**

Using the diagram, which statements in <보기> are correct?

**◇ Diagram — The DNA double helix, unrolled into a ladder.** DNA drawn as a ladder of four base pairs between two sugar-phosphate backbones. The left strand runs 5′ to 3′ from top to bottom and the right strand runs 5′ to 3′ from bottom to top, so the strands are antiparallel. Rungs from top: A with T, G with C, T with A, C with G. Dashed hydrogen bonds: two in each A-T pair and three in each G-C pair. Every rung pairs a two-ring purine with a one-ring pyrimidine. The helix is right-handed and 2 nm wide, with 3.4 nm and 10 base pairs per turn, and is stabilized by hydrogen bonds plus base stacking.

> **(가)** Every rung joins a two-ring base to a one-ring base.
>
> **(나)** The sugar-phosphate backbones run along the outside of the ladder, and the bases point inward.

**<보기>**
- ㄱ. Pairing a purine with a pyrimidine on every rung keeps the helix a constant 2 nm wide.
- ㄴ. With the backbones outside, the negatively charged phosphates face the surrounding water while the bases are tucked inside.
- ㄷ. Because the strands are antiparallel, the left strand's 5' end sits across from the right strand's 5' end.

① ㄱ　 ② ㄴ　 ③ ㄱ, ㄴ　 ④ ㄱ, ㄷ　 ⑤ ㄱ, ㄴ, ㄷ

**Answer: ③ ㄱ, ㄴ**
- ㄱ — **O** — Two rings (A, G) plus one ring (C, T) always gives the same rung length; two purines would bulge the helix and two pyrimidines would pinch it.
- ㄴ — **O** — The ionized phosphates are hydrophilic and sit on the outside; the bases stack in the interior.
- ㄷ — **X** — Antiparallel means opposite directions: the left strand's 5' end faces the right strand's 3' end, as the diagram shows.

*Concepts linked:* Purine–pyrimidine pairing · Antiparallel strands · Backbone outside

---

**Q95. [보기 Combination · 3점] Carbon numbers matter**

Which statements in <보기> about nucleotide structure are correct?

> **(가)** In a DNA nucleotide, the base attaches to the 1' carbon of deoxyribose and the phosphate to the 5' carbon.
>
> **(나)** Deoxyribose has no oxygen at its 2' carbon.

**<보기>**
- ㄱ. The next nucleotide attaches at the 2' carbon, which is why losing the 2' oxygen defines DNA.
- ㄴ. A nucleoside (base + sugar only) can be joined directly into a growing chain, since the base alone decides pairing.
- ㄷ. When two nucleotides join, the phosphate on the incoming nucleotide's 5' carbon bonds to the 3'-OH of the previous nucleotide, forming a phosphodiester bond.

① ㄴ　 ② ㄷ　 ③ ㄱ, ㄷ　 ④ ㄴ, ㄷ　 ⑤ ㄱ, ㄴ, ㄷ

**Answer: ② ㄷ**
- ㄱ — **X** — Attachment is always at the 3'-OH. The missing 2' oxygen is simply what makes the sugar deoxyribose.
- ㄴ — **X** — Joining needs phosphates: incoming units are nucleoside triphosphates, whose phosphate forms the phosphodiester link and supplies the energy. A nucleoside has none.
- ㄷ — **O** — That 5'-phosphate-to-3'-OH link is the phosphodiester bond, which is why each strand has a 5' end and a 3' end.

*Concepts linked:* Nucleotide carbons · Phosphodiester bond · Nucleotide vs nucleoside

---

**Q96. [Matching · 3점] Structure explains function**

Match each feature of DNA structure to the property it explains.

**Structural feature**
A. Complementary base pairing
B. Weak hydrogen bonds between bases
C. A purine always paired with a pyrimidine
D. Sugar-phosphate backbones on the outside
E. Uneven major and minor grooves

**Property explained**
1. Each strand can serve as a template to rebuild its partner
2. Proteins can read base sequences without unwinding the helix
3. The charged, hydrophilic part of DNA faces the surrounding water
4. The helix keeps a constant 2 nm width
5. The strands can be 'unzipped' without breaking either backbone

**Correct matches:**
- A = Complementary base pairing → Each strand can serve as a template to rebuild its partner
- B = Weak hydrogen bonds between bases → The strands can be 'unzipped' without breaking either backbone
- C = A purine always paired with a pyrimidine → The helix keeps a constant 2 nm width
- D = Sugar-phosphate backbones on the outside → The charged, hydrophilic part of DNA faces the surrounding water
- E = Uneven major and minor grooves → Proteins can read base sequences without unwinding the helix

DNA's two jobs — storing information and copying it — follow from its structure: complementary, weakly paired strands make copying possible, while the regular, stable helix protects the stored sequence.

*Concepts linked:* DNA structure and function

---

**Q97. [True / False · 1점]**

> The virulent bacteria recovered from Griffith's fourth mouse were grown in culture, and their descendants were virulent too.

**◇ Diagram — Finding the genetic material: Griffith (1928) and Avery (1944).** Griffith&#x27;s experiment: mice injected with live virulent Streptococcus pneumoniae die; with live nonvirulent bacteria they live; with heat-killed virulent bacteria they live; with heat-killed virulent plus live nonvirulent bacteria they die and live virulent cells are recovered, so something from the dead cells transformed the live ones. Avery, MacLeod and McCarty treated an extract of heat-killed virulent cells with nothing, RNase (destroys RNA), protease (destroys protein) or DNase (destroys DNA) before mixing it with live nonvirulent cells: it still transformed them after RNase or protease, but not after DNase, so DNA carries the genetic information.

**Claim:** This shows that transformation changed the bacteria's heritable genetic information, rather than just coating them temporarily with material from the dead cells.

**Answer: True**

A temporary coating would be diluted away as cells divide; virulence passed on for generations means the information was stored and copied — the defining jobs of genetic material.

*Concepts linked:* Transformation · Genetic material stores and copies information

---

**Q98. [True / False · 2점]**

> One strand of a 10-base-pair DNA segment reads 5'-GGCATGCCAT-3'.

**◇ Diagram — The DNA double helix, unrolled into a ladder.** DNA drawn as a ladder of four base pairs between two sugar-phosphate backbones. The left strand runs 5′ to 3′ from top to bottom and the right strand runs 5′ to 3′ from bottom to top, so the strands are antiparallel. Rungs from top: A with T, G with C, T with A, C with G. Dashed hydrogen bonds: two in each A-T pair and three in each G-C pair. Every rung pairs a two-ring purine with a one-ring pyrimidine. The helix is right-handed and 2 nm wide, with 3.4 nm and 10 base pairs per turn, and is stabilized by hydrogen bonds plus base stacking.

**Claim:** The two strands of this segment are held together by 25 hydrogen bonds.

**Answer: False**

Count the pairs: 6 G–C pairs (G, G, C, G, C, C) × 3 = 18, plus 4 A–T pairs × 2 = 8, for 26 hydrogen bonds.

*Concepts linked:* Hydrogen bonds: G–C = 3, A–T = 2

---

**Q99. [True / False · 1점]**

> A DNA sequence is written simply as GATTC, with neither end labeled.

**Claim:** By convention, the C at the right-hand end carries the strand's free 5' phosphate.

**Answer: False**

An unlabeled sequence is written 5'→3', so the left end (G) is the 5' end; the C on the right carries the free 3'-OH.

*Concepts linked:* Sequence convention · Strand polarity

---

**Q100. [True / False · 2점]**

> A gene is 3,000 base pairs long. Use the helix dimensions given in the diagram.

**◇ Diagram — The DNA double helix, unrolled into a ladder.** DNA drawn as a ladder of four base pairs between two sugar-phosphate backbones. The left strand runs 5′ to 3′ from top to bottom and the right strand runs 5′ to 3′ from bottom to top, so the strands are antiparallel. Rungs from top: A with T, G with C, T with A, C with G. Dashed hydrogen bonds: two in each A-T pair and three in each G-C pair. Every rung pairs a two-ring purine with a one-ring pyrimidine. The helix is right-handed and 2 nm wide, with 3.4 nm and 10 base pairs per turn, and is stabilized by hydrogen bonds plus base stacking.

**Claim:** Stretched out as a double helix, this gene makes about 300 complete turns and is about 1 µm (1,000 nm) long.

**Answer: True**

3,000 bp ÷ 10 bp per turn = 300 turns; 300 × 3.4 nm = 1,020 nm ≈ 1 µm.

*Concepts linked:* Helix dimensions: 3.4 nm and 10 bp per turn

---

**Q101. [True / False · 1점]**

> In gel electrophoresis, DNA fragments are loaded into wells in a gel held at about pH 8, and an electric field is applied across it.

**Claim:** The DNA fragments will migrate toward the negative electrode.

**Answer: False**

Negatively charged DNA is attracted to the positive electrode. The ionized phosphate groups of the backbone give DNA its uniform negative charge.

*Concepts linked:* Negatively charged backbone · Real-world: gel electrophoresis

---

**Q102. [True / False · 1점]**

> Many DNA-binding proteins, including transcription factors, recognize specific base sequences without unzipping the double helix.

**Claim:** This is possible because the helix's outer surface forms major and minor grooves in which the edges of the paired bases are exposed.

**Answer: True**

The uneven grooves leave part of each base pair accessible from outside, so proteins can 'read' a sequence such as a promoter while the DNA stays double-stranded.

*Concepts linked:* Major and minor grooves · DNA-binding proteins

---

**Q103. [True / False · 1점]**

> In PCR, DNA is heated to about 95 °C so its two strands separate; on cooling, short complementary DNA pieces (primers) pair with the separated strands.

**Claim:** Heating separates the strands by breaking the phosphodiester bonds that link the two strands, and these bonds re-form when the sample cools.

**Answer: False**

Phosphodiester bonds link nucleotides within each strand and stay intact. Heat breaks the weak hydrogen bonds between paired bases (base stacking along each strand adds stability), which is what lets DNA 'unzip' down the middle.

*Concepts linked:* Unzipping DNA · Real-world: PCR

---

**Q104. [True / False · 2점]**

> The two strands of a DNA double helix are complementary.

**Claim:** So the two strands have identical sequences when each is read 5'→3'.

**Answer: False**

Complementary means paired, not identical: the partner of 5'-ATGCCT-3' reads 5'-AGGCAT-3'. Only special palindromic sequences, such as 5'-GAATTC-3', read the same on both strands.

*Concepts linked:* Complementary vs identical · Antiparallel strands

---

**Q105. [True / False · 1점]**

> Erwin Chargaff found that %A = %T and %G = %C in DNA from many species; Rosalind Franklin and Maurice Wilkins obtained X-ray crystallography images of DNA.

**Claim:** Chargaff's ratios alone were enough for Watson and Crick to conclude that DNA is a helix of two strands.

**Answer: False**

The ratios suggested which bases pair, but the helical shape and its dimensions came from the X-ray data. Watson and Crick needed both lines of evidence.

*Concepts linked:* Evidence behind the double helix

---

**Q106. [True / False · 1점]**

> A single DNA strand is 12 nucleotides long, and each nucleotide was built with a phosphate on its 5' carbon.

**Claim:** The strand therefore has two 5'-phosphate ends, one at each end.

**Answer: False**

Phosphodiester bonds link each nucleotide's 5' phosphate to the previous one's 3'-OH, so only one end keeps a free 5' phosphate; the other end has a free 3'-OH. That difference gives every strand its polarity.

*Concepts linked:* Strand polarity · Phosphodiester bonds

---

**Q107. [True / False · 1점]**

> A student builds a DNA model whose two strands twist around each other to the left, with the sugar-phosphate backbones running down the middle and the bases pointing outward.

**Claim:** Apart from the backbones and bases being swapped, the model is correct, including its left-handed twist.

**Answer: False**

The twist is wrong too: DNA's two strands form a right-handed helix. So the model has two errors — a left-handed twist, and backbones inside instead of outside with the bases pointing inward.

*Concepts linked:* Right-handed helix · Backbones outside, bases inside

---

**Q108. [Multiple Choice · 2점]**

Suppose Avery's team had found the opposite pattern: the protease-treated extract failed to transform the nonvirulent cells, while the DNase- and RNase-treated extracts still transformed them. What would they have concluded?

**◇ Diagram — Finding the genetic material: Griffith (1928) and Avery (1944).** Griffith&#x27;s experiment: mice injected with live virulent Streptococcus pneumoniae die; with live nonvirulent bacteria they live; with heat-killed virulent bacteria they live; with heat-killed virulent plus live nonvirulent bacteria they die and live virulent cells are recovered, so something from the dead cells transformed the live ones. Avery, MacLeod and McCarty treated an extract of heat-killed virulent cells with nothing, RNase (destroys RNA), protease (destroys protein) or DNase (destroys DNA) before mixing it with live nonvirulent cells: it still transformed them after RNase or protease, but not after DNase, so DNA carries the genetic information.

① DNA is the genetic material, since DNase didn't stop transformation　 ② RNA is the genetic material, since RNase didn't stop transformation　 ③ Protein, not DNA, is the transforming (genetic) material　 ④ Both DNA and protein are required, since some treatments worked　 ⑤ Nothing can be concluded, since heat-killed cells were used

**Answer: ③ Protein, not DNA, is the transforming (genetic) material**

Each enzyme destroys one candidate; the treatment that blocks transformation identifies the molecule that is required — in this hypothetical, protein. The real result (only DNase blocked it) pointed to DNA.

*Concepts linked:* Avery, MacLeod & McCarty · Logic of elimination

---

**Q109. [Multiple Choice · 2점]**

What is the complementary strand of 5'-ATGCCT-3', written in the standard 5'→3' direction?

① 5'-TACGGA-3'　 ② 5'-TCCGTA-3'　 ③ 3'-AGGCAT-5'　 ④ 5'-UACGGA-3'　 ⑤ 5'-AGGCAT-3'

**Answer: ⑤ 5'-AGGCAT-3'**

Pair each base (A–T, G–C) to get 3'-TACGGA-5', then rewrite it from its 5' end, because the strands are antiparallel: 5'-AGGCAT-3'.

*Concepts linked:* Antiparallel strands · Complementary pairing · Writing sequences 5'→3'

---

**Q110. [Multiple Choice · 1점]**

About 2 m of DNA must fit inside a human nucleus only a few micrometres across. Which is the first level of packing in the condensation series?

① The DNA double helix folds directly into a 1,400 nm condensed chromatid　 ② DNA wraps around histone proteins, forming the 10 nm nucleosome fiber　 ③ The DNA coils into a 30 nm fiber before any proteins bind to it　 ④ RNA molecules wrap around the DNA to shorten it before it folds　 ⑤ Phospholipids coat the DNA so that it can be folded more tightly

**Answer: ② DNA wraps around histone proteins, forming the 10 nm nucleosome fiber**

Packing proceeds stepwise: 2 nm DNA → 10 nm nucleosome fiber (DNA wound around histones H2A, H2B, H3, and H4) → 30 nm chromatin fiber → 300 nm → 700 nm → 1,400 nm condensed chromatid.

*Concepts linked:* Chromosome condensation · Nucleosomes · Histones

---

**Q111. [Multiple Choice · 1점]**

Which statement best describes what holds the two strands of the double helix together and stabilizes it?

① Covalent bonds that form between the paired bases on the two strands　 ② Ionic bonds between the negatively charged phosphate groups of the two strands　 ③ Phosphodiester bonds that cross the middle from one strand to the other　 ④ Many weak H-bonds between paired bases, plus stacking between neighbouring bases　 ⑤ Hydrogen bonds alone, since neighbouring bases on a strand don't interact

**Answer: ④ Many weak H-bonds between paired bases, plus stacking between neighbouring bases**

Base pairs are joined by hydrogen bonds — weak one by one but strong in number — and stacking interactions between adjacent bases on each strand add stability. Phosphodiester bonds link nucleotides only within a strand.

*Concepts linked:* Hydrogen bonds · Base stacking · Phosphodiester bonds

---

**Q112. [Multiple Choice · 1점]**

Watson and Crick built their double-helix model from other researchers' results. Which pairing of evidence and inference is correct?

① Franklin and Wilkins' X-ray data revealed the helix's shape and size; Chargaff's ratios pointed to A–T, G–C pairing　 ② Chargaff's ratios revealed the helical shape and dimensions; Franklin and Wilkins' X-ray data showed which bases pair　 ③ Franklin and Wilkins' X-ray data showed that A pairs with G; Chargaff's ratios showed that the strands run parallel　 ④ Chargaff's ratios showed that the helix is left-handed; Franklin and Wilkins' X-ray data placed the bases on the outside　 ⑤ Griffith's and Avery's experiments revealed the helical shape; Chargaff's ratios showed that the backbone lies inside

**Answer: ① Franklin and Wilkins' X-ray data revealed the helix's shape and size; Chargaff's ratios pointed to A–T, G–C pairing**

X-ray crystallography (Franklin and Wilkins) gave the helical structure; Chargaff's equal ratios implied complementary pairing. The resulting model: a right-handed, antiparallel double helix with backbones outside and bases inside.

*Concepts linked:* Watson & Crick · Franklin & Wilkins · Chargaff

---

### Gene Expression & RNA

**Q113. [보기 Combination · 3점] Small differences, big consequences**

Which statements in <보기> about how RNA differs from DNA are correct?

> **(가)** Ribose, RNA's sugar, carries an -OH on its 2' carbon, where deoxyribose has only -H.
>
> **(나)** The 5' end of a newly made RNA typically carries three phosphates, while the 5' end of DNA typically carries one.

**<보기>**
- ㄱ. The 2'-OH makes RNA less stable than DNA, which suits RNA as a short-lived working copy and DNA as long-term storage.
- ㄴ. Uracil has an extra methyl group compared with thymine, which is why it pairs with A.
- ㄷ. RNA's 5' triphosphate is left over from the first nucleotide, which was never joined to a previous nucleotide and so never lost its outer phosphates.

① ㄷ　 ② ㄱ, ㄴ　 ③ ㄱ, ㄷ　 ④ ㄴ, ㄷ　 ⑤ ㄱ, ㄴ, ㄷ

**Answer: ③ ㄱ, ㄷ**
- ㄱ — **O** — The extra hydroxyl makes RNA more reactive and less stable — fine for a message that should be temporary.
- ㄴ — **X** — Uracil has an -H where thymine has a methyl (-CH3); both pair with A.
- ㄷ — **O** — Each later nucleotide loses two phosphates (as pyrophosphate) when it is joined to the chain; the first one keeps its full triphosphate at the 5' end.

*Concepts linked:* Ribose vs deoxyribose · RNA 5' triphosphate · Uracil vs thymine

---

**Q114. [Matching · 2점] Templates and products**

Match each process to its template and product.

**Process**
A. Transcription
B. Translation
C. Reverse transcription
D. DNA replication

**Template → product**
1. RNA template → DNA
2. DNA template → DNA
3. DNA template → RNA
4. mRNA code → polypeptide

**Correct matches:**
- A = Transcription → DNA template → RNA
- B = Translation → mRNA code → polypeptide
- C = Reverse transcription → RNA template → DNA
- D = DNA replication → DNA template → DNA

Transcription, reverse transcription, and replication all copy nucleic-acid information by complementary base pairing (by RNA polymerase, reverse transcriptase, and DNA polymerase), always adding nucleotides at the 3' end; only translation, on ribosomes, changes the 'language'.

*Concepts linked:* Central dogma · Reverse transcription · Replication

---

**Q115. [True / False · 1점]**

> A liver cell and a nerve cell from the same person make very different sets of proteins.

**Claim:** As it developed, the nerve cell must have lost the genes for liver proteins from its genome.

**Answer: False**

Both cells carry the same genome. Gene expression is regulated: each cell type turns on a different subset of genes, so the liver genes are present but switched off in the nerve cell.

*Concepts linked:* Genome · Regulated gene expression

---

**Q116. [True / False · 1점]**

> HIV carries its genome as RNA. After infecting a cell, it makes a DNA copy of that RNA, which is inserted into the host's DNA.

**Claim:** The host cell's RNA polymerase carries out this step by running in reverse.

**Answer: False**

RNA polymerase makes RNA from a DNA template. Making DNA from an RNA template — reverse transcription, an exception to the central dogma — requires the virus's own RNA-dependent DNA polymerase, reverse transcriptase.

*Concepts linked:* Reverse transcription · Central dogma exception

---

**Q117. [True / False · 1점]**

> A nucleic acid isolated from a cell is 120 nucleotides long, contains ribose, and folds into a compact three-dimensional shape in which several short stretches are base-paired with one another.

**Claim:** Because parts of it are base-paired, the molecule must be double-stranded, like DNA.

**Answer: False**

Ribose identifies RNA, which is usually single-stranded; it folds into complex shapes when stretches of the same strand pair with each other. Base pairing alone doesn't make a molecule double-stranded.

*Concepts linked:* RNA folding · DNA vs RNA

---

**Q118. [True / False · 1점]**

> Like DNA, each RNA strand has a 5'-phosphate end and a 3'-hydroxyl end.

**Claim:** RNA polymerase adds each new nucleotide to the free 3'-OH end, so the 5' end of an RNA is the first part made.

**Answer: True**

All nucleic acids grow by addition at the 3' end, so the 5' end is where synthesis started — and in RNA it still carries its triphosphate.

*Concepts linked:* RNA polarity · Growth at the 3' end

---

**Q119. [True / False · 2점]**

> The notes define a gene as 'the DNA sequence that corresponds to a specific protein'. Muscle cells and liver cells both transcribe gene X, but their mature X mRNAs differ by one exon.

**Claim:** The two cells make different but related proteins from gene X, so 'one gene, one specific protein' is an oversimplification.

**Answer: True**

The same primary transcript is spliced differently in the two cells (alternative splicing), so one gene's DNA sequence corresponds to more than one related protein.

*Concepts linked:* Gene definition · Alternative splicing

---

**Q120. [True / False · 1점]**

> A mutation changes one base in the protein-coding part of a gene.

**Claim:** For this change to alter the protein's amino-acid sequence, it must first be copied into mRNA by transcription, because ribosomes read mRNA, not DNA.

**Answer: True**

In the central dogma, protein synthesis depends on the code carried by mRNA; a DNA change reaches the protein only through its RNA copy.

*Concepts linked:* Central dogma · mRNA carries the code

---

**Q121. [True / False · 2점]**

> AZT, one of the first HIV drugs, is a thymidine nucleoside whose sugar carries an azide group in place of the 3'-OH. Cells add phosphates to it, and HIV's reverse transcriptase then incorporates it into viral DNA.

**Claim:** Once AZT is added to a growing viral DNA strand, no further nucleotide can be attached, so viral DNA synthesis stops at that point.

**Answer: True**

Nucleotides can only be added to a free 3'-OH. AZT has none, so it ends the chain — which is how it blocks HIV from copying its RNA into DNA.

*Concepts linked:* 3'-OH requirement · Nucleoside vs nucleotide · Real-world: AZT

---

**Q122. [True / False · 1점]**

> A student writes: 'A gene is a protein that determines a trait.'

**Claim:** The student's definition is acceptable, because the protein made from a gene is what actually determines the trait.

**Answer: False**

The protein is the gene's product. A gene is the DNA sequence — the unit of heredity — that corresponds to that protein; making the protein is gene expression.

*Concepts linked:* Gene vs gene product

---

**Q123. [True / False · 1점]**

> Adenosine is adenine joined to ribose. ATP is adenosine carrying three phosphate groups.

**Claim:** Adenosine and ATP are both nucleotides, since each contains a base and a sugar.

**Answer: False**

Base + sugar alone is a nucleoside (adenosine); adding phosphate makes a nucleotide, and ATP is one. Only nucleoside triphosphates like ATP can be joined into RNA by RNA polymerase.

*Concepts linked:* Nucleoside vs nucleotide · ATP

---

**Q124. [Multiple Choice · 1점]**

Why is RNA synthesis called 'transcription' while protein synthesis is called 'translation'?

① Transcription happens in the nucleus and translation in the cytoplasm, so the two names simply record where each step occurs　 ② Transcription copies DNA into new DNA before division, while translation converts that DNA into RNA for export　 ③ Transcription is carried out by ribosomes reading DNA, while translation is carried out by RNA polymerase reading mRNA　 ④ Transcription copies within one nucleic-acid 'language'; translation converts it into another: amino acids　 ⑤ Transcription keeps the two DNA strands together, while translation separates them so each can be read into protein

**Answer: ④ Transcription copies within one nucleic-acid 'language'; translation converts it into another: amino acids**

DNA and RNA are both nucleic acids, so making RNA from DNA is copying in the same language; making protein from mRNA's code changes the language to amino acids. Location differs only in eukaryotes and isn't the reason for the names.

*Concepts linked:* Central dogma · Transcription vs translation

---

**Q125. [Multiple Choice · 2점]**

A purified nucleic acid, 2,000 nucleotides long, has this base composition: 20% A, 30% U, 25% G, 25% C. What is the best conclusion?

① It is double-stranded RNA, since its G and C percentages are equal　 ② It is single-stranded DNA, since 2,000 nucleotides is too long for RNA　 ③ It is double-stranded DNA, since its G and C percentages are equal　 ④ It is double-stranded RNA, since in RNA adenine always pairs with uracil　 ⑤ It is single-stranded RNA: it contains uracil, and A does not equal U

**Answer: ⑤ It is single-stranded RNA: it contains uracil, and A does not equal U**

Uracil means RNA. In a double strand every A would pair with a U, forcing %A = %U; 20% ≠ 30%, so it is single-stranded (G = C here is a coincidence).

*Concepts linked:* RNA vs DNA · Base-pairing ratios

---

**Q126. [Multiple Choice · 2점]**

RNA polymerase builds a 100-nucleotide RNA from ribonucleoside triphosphates. How many phosphate groups does the finished RNA contain?

① 102　 ② 300　 ③ 200　 ④ 100　 ⑤ 99

**Answer: ① 102**

Each nucleotide after the first loses two phosphates (as pyrophosphate) when joined and keeps one in the backbone: 99. The first nucleotide keeps its whole triphosphate at the 5' end: 3. Total = 102.

*Concepts linked:* RNA 5' triphosphate · Pyrophosphate release
