### Transcription Mechanism

**Q127. [보기 Combination · 3점] Reading a transcription bubble**

Using the diagram, which statements in <보기> are correct?

**◇ Diagram — Reading a transcription bubble.** Transcription bubble inside an RNA polymerase moving to the right. Top, the nontemplate strand reads 5′ C A T G A C C G T A 3′; bottom, the template strand reads 3′ G T A C T G G C A T 5′. The RNA made so far, 5′ triphosphate C A U G A C C, is paired to the template, and the slot for the next nucleotide, across from the template&#x27;s next base, is empty; the RNA grows 5′ to 3′.

> **(가)** Top strand: 5'-CATGACCGTA-3'. Bottom strand: 3'-GTACTGGCAT-5'.
>
> **(나)** The RNA made so far, 5'-CAUGACC, is paired with the bottom strand, and the polymerase moves to the right.

**<보기>**
- ㄱ. The polymerase reads the bottom (template) strand in the 5'→3' direction as it moves right.
- ㄴ. The RNA has the same sequence as the top (nontemplate) strand, with U in place of T.
- ㄷ. When all 10 positions shown have been transcribed, the RNA will read 5'-CAUGACCGUA-3'.

① ㄱ　 ② ㄷ　 ③ ㄱ, ㄷ　 ④ ㄴ, ㄷ　 ⑤ ㄱ, ㄴ, ㄷ

**Answer: ④ ㄴ, ㄷ**
- ㄱ — **X** — The template runs 3'→5' from left to right, so a polymerase moving right reads it 3'→5' while the RNA grows 5'→3'.
- ㄴ — **O** — RNA is complementary to the template, so it matches the nontemplate strand except that U replaces T.
- ㄷ — **O** — Copy the nontemplate strand and swap T for U: CATGACCGTA → CAUGACCGUA.

*Concepts linked:* Template vs nontemplate · Read 3'→5', grow 5'→3'

---

**Q128. [보기 Combination · 3점] Starting a eukaryotic gene**

Using the diagram, which statements in <보기> are correct?

**◇ Diagram — Starting transcription: bacteria vs. eukaryotes.** Transcription initiation. Bacteria: a single kind of RNA polymerase uses a sigma factor to recognize the promoter and starts at +1 just downstream. Eukaryotes: general transcription factors (at least six) bind a promoter whose TATA box, 5′ TATAAA 3′, lies about 25 base pairs upstream of the +1 start site; RNA polymerase II, one of three RNA polymerases and the one that makes mRNA, joins them; an activator bound to a distant enhancer reaches the complex through the mediator as the DNA loops.

> **(가)** A gene's promoter contains 5'-TATAAA-3', and transcription starts about 25 base pairs away from it.
>
> **(나)** An activator protein bound to an enhancer several thousand base pairs upstream strongly increases transcription of the gene.

**<보기>**
- ㄱ. General transcription factors bind the TATA box region of the promoter and help RNA polymerase II assemble there.
- ㄴ. The distant activator can affect transcription because the DNA loops, letting the activator contact the mediator, the general transcription factors, and Pol II at the promoter.
- ㄷ. Deleting the TATA box would leave the start site unchanged, because Pol II recognizes the +1 nucleotide directly.

① ㄱ　 ② ㄴ　 ③ ㄷ　 ④ ㄱ, ㄴ　 ⑤ ㄱ, ㄴ, ㄷ

**Answer: ④ ㄱ, ㄴ**
- ㄱ — **O** — At least six general transcription factors bind the promoter; together with Pol II they form the initiation complex.
- ㄴ — **O** — Looping brings enhancer-bound activators into contact with promoter-bound proteins through the mediator complex.
- ㄷ — **X** — The +1 site is set by the promoter complex about 25 bp from the TATA box; Pol II does not find it on its own.

*Concepts linked:* TATA box · General transcription factors · Enhancers and DNA looping

---

**Q129. [보기 Combination · 3점] Building RNA in a test tube**

Three test tubes each contain a DNA template with a promoter, RNA polymerase, and any factors it needs. Which statements in <보기> are correct?

> **(가)** Tube 1 also contains ATP, GTP, CTP, and UTP.
>
> **(나)** Tube 2 contains dATP, dGTP, dCTP, and dTTP instead. Tube 3 is like tube 1 but lacks UTP.

**<보기>**
- ㄱ. Tube 1 makes RNA; its nucleoside triphosphates supply both the building blocks and the energy for polymerization.
- ㄴ. Tube 2 also makes RNA, because only base pairing matters to the polymerase.
- ㄷ. In tube 3, RNA synthesis stalls at the first template position holding A.

① ㄱ　 ② ㄷ　 ③ ㄱ, ㄴ　 ④ ㄱ, ㄷ　 ⑤ ㄱ, ㄴ, ㄷ

**Answer: ④ ㄱ, ㄷ**
- ㄱ — **O** — Transcription needs a DNA template, RNA polymerase, and ribonucleoside triphosphates, which provide the monomers and the energy.
- ㄴ — **X** — RNA polymerase builds RNA from ribonucleotides; deoxyribonucleotides are DNA building blocks.
- ㄷ — **O** — An A in the template must be paired with U; without UTP, the polymerase can't add the next nucleotide there.

*Concepts linked:* Transcription requirements · NTPs vs dNTPs · Template pairing

---

**Q130. [보기 Combination · 3점] One nucleotide, three checkpoints**

Using the diagram, which statements in <보기> are correct?

**◇ Diagram — How each ribonucleotide is added.** Adding one ribonucleotide: the template DNA runs 3′ to 5′ down the left; the RNA, running 5′ to 3′, is paired to it and ends in a 3′ OH. An incoming ribonucleoside triphosphate sits opposite the next template base; the 3′ OH attacks the innermost of its three phosphates, and the outer two phosphates leave as pyrophosphate (P-P), so the RNA grows 5′ to 3′.

> **(가)** Read from top to bottom, the template strand shown is 3'-TGAA-5'.
>
> **(나)** The incoming nucleotide in the diagram is UTP.

**<보기>**
- ㄱ. The incoming UTP would be joined even if the next template base were G, since the polymerase checks only that it is a ribonucleotide.
- ㄴ. The RNA shown so far reads 5'-ACU-3', and adding the incoming U will make it 5'-ACUU-3'.
- ㄷ. Releasing pyrophosphate makes this a hydrolysis reaction, since a molecule splits off.

① ㄱ　 ② ㄴ　 ③ ㄱ, ㄷ　 ④ ㄴ, ㄷ　 ⑤ ㄱ, ㄴ, ㄷ

**Answer: ② ㄴ**
- ㄱ — **X** — An incoming NTP is kept only if it base-pairs correctly with the template base; opposite G, only C fits.
- ㄴ — **O** — Template T, G, and A pair with RNA A, C, and U; the incoming UTP pairs with the next template A.
- ㄷ — **X** — Hydrolysis uses water to split a bond. Here the chain's 3'-OH — not water — attacks the innermost phosphate and a new phosphodiester bond forms, a condensation-type joining. (Module 2 draws this joining as releasing water; in cells the leaving group is pyrophosphate.)

*Concepts linked:* Base-pair checking · Phosphodiester bond formation · Condensation vs hydrolysis

---

**Q131. [보기 Combination · 3점] Two poisons, two polymerases**

Which statements in <보기> are correct?

> **(가)** Rifampin, a tuberculosis drug, blocks bacterial RNA polymerase.
>
> **(나)** α-Amanitin, the toxin of the death-cap mushroom, blocks eukaryotic RNA polymerase II.

**<보기>**
- ㄱ. Rifampin stops bacteria from making new mRNA, so production of new bacterial proteins soon stops too.
- ㄴ. α-Amanitin halts mRNA production in a victim's liver cells, while RNAs made by the other two RNA polymerases are less directly affected.
- ㄷ. Rifampin can be given to patients because the single bacterial RNA polymerase differs from the RNA polymerases in human nuclei.

① ㄱ　 ② ㄷ　 ③ ㄱ, ㄴ　 ④ ㄱ, ㄷ　 ⑤ ㄱ, ㄴ, ㄷ

**Answer: ⑤ ㄱ, ㄴ, ㄷ**
- ㄱ — **O** — Bacteria use a single type of RNA polymerase for all transcription; block it and no new mRNA is available for translation.
- ㄴ — **O** — Eukaryotes have three distinct RNA polymerases, and Pol II is the one that makes mRNA.
- ㄷ — **O** — Selective toxicity comes from structural differences between bacterial and eukaryotic polymerases.

*Concepts linked:* Bacterial vs eukaryotic RNA polymerases · Real-world: rifampin and death-cap poisoning

---

**Q132. [Matching · 4점] Knock it out, see what breaks**

Match each transcription element to the result of damaging or moving it.

**Element**
A. Promoter
B. TATA box
C. Enhancer
D. Mediator complex
E. Sigma factor
F. Terminator

**Result**
1. Activator signals no longer reach the general transcription factors and Pol II
2. Nothing can bind to start the gene, so it is not transcribed at all
3. Transcription can still occur but no longer starts at a fixed site about 25 bp downstream
4. Moved thousands of bp away, it still boosts transcription as long as the DNA can loop
5. Transcripts run on past the end of the gene
6. A bacterium's RNA polymerase can no longer recognize its promoters

**Correct matches:**
- A = Promoter → Nothing can bind to start the gene, so it is not transcribed at all
- B = TATA box → Transcription can still occur but no longer starts at a fixed site about 25 bp downstream
- C = Enhancer → Moved thousands of bp away, it still boosts transcription as long as the DNA can loop
- D = Mediator complex → Activator signals no longer reach the general transcription factors and Pol II
- E = Sigma factor → A bacterium's RNA polymerase can no longer recognize its promoters
- F = Terminator → Transcripts run on past the end of the gene

A promoter decides whether a gene can be transcribed at all; the TATA box fixes where transcription starts; enhancers and the mediator adjust how much; sigma factors are bacteria's promoter-recognition tool; terminators set where it stops.

*Concepts linked:* Promoter · TATA box · Enhancer · Mediator · Sigma factor · Terminator

---

**Q133. [True / False · 1점]**

> The transcription bubble is about 14 base pairs long, but the RNA–DNA duplex inside it is only about 8 base pairs.

**Claim:** So, as the polymerase advances, older RNA must peel away from the template and exit, while the two DNA strands re-pair behind the polymerase.

**Answer: True**

Only the newest ~8 nucleotides stay paired with the template. The polymerase has channels for the RNA to exit and for the DNA to enter and leave, and the unwound DNA rejoins after transcription.

*Concepts linked:* Transcription bubble · Polymerase channels

---

**Q134. [True / False · 2점]**

> Bacteria have a single type of RNA polymerase; a sigma factor associated with it recognizes promoter sequences.

**◇ Diagram — Starting transcription: bacteria vs. eukaryotes.** Transcription initiation. Bacteria: a single kind of RNA polymerase uses a sigma factor to recognize the promoter and starts at +1 just downstream. Eukaryotes: general transcription factors (at least six) bind a promoter whose TATA box, 5′ TATAAA 3′, lies about 25 base pairs upstream of the +1 start site; RNA polymerase II, one of three RNA polymerases and the one that makes mRNA, joins them; an activator bound to a distant enhancer reaches the complex through the mediator as the DNA loops.

**Claim:** A bacterium could switch on a different set of genes by making a different sigma factor that recognizes a different promoter sequence.

**Answer: True**

Since promoter recognition is the sigma factor's job, swapping sigma factors redirects the same polymerase to other promoters — bacteria do exactly this, for example, when responding to heat stress.

*Concepts linked:* Sigma factor · Promoter recognition · Regulated gene expression

---

**Q135. [True / False · 1점]**

> Genes X and Y lie next to each other on the same chromosome.

**Claim:** Because both genes are on the same DNA molecule, they must use the same strand as their template.

**Answer: False**

Which strand is transcribed can differ from one gene to the next, depending on each gene's promoter orientation — neighbouring genes can be transcribed in opposite directions.

*Concepts linked:* Template-strand choice · Promoter orientation

---

**Q136. [True / False · 1점]**

> The 3'-OH at the end of a growing RNA attacks the innermost phosphate of the incoming nucleotide, and the outer two phosphates leave as pyrophosphate.

**Claim:** Because this chemistry needs a free 3'-OH on the growing chain, RNA — like DNA — can only be extended in the 5'→3' direction.

**Answer: True**

The incoming nucleotide brings the triphosphate (on its 5' carbon) and the chain supplies the 3'-OH, so every nucleic acid grows at its 3' end.

*Concepts linked:* Polymerization chemistry · 5'→3' growth

---

**Q137. [True / False · 1점]**

> Each phosphodiester bond in RNA forms as an incoming ribonucleotide releases its outer two phosphates as pyrophosphate.

**Claim:** The energy for each new bond comes from a separate ATP molecule hydrolyzed by the polymerase, not from the incoming nucleotide.

**Answer: False**

The incoming nucleoside triphosphate supplies its own energy: the 3'-OH attacks its innermost phosphate, and splitting off pyrophosphate drives bond formation — which is why the building blocks must be triphosphates.

*Concepts linked:* NTPs carry their own energy · Polymerization

---

**Q138. [True / False · 1점]**

> Transcription of a gene begins at its promoter and continues until RNA polymerase reaches a terminator sequence.

**Claim:** A mutation that deletes a gene's terminator would be expected to produce abnormally long RNA transcripts that continue into the DNA beyond the gene.

**Answer: True**

Termination is the third stage, after initiation and elongation, and it happens when the polymerase reaches the terminator; without one, elongation runs on past the gene's normal end.

*Concepts linked:* Initiation · Elongation · Termination

---

**Q139. [True / False · 2점]**

> RNA polymerase moves along the template strand as the RNA grows 5'→3'.

**Claim:** The polymerase therefore travels toward the 3' end of the template strand.

**Answer: False**

The template is read 3'→5', so the polymerase travels toward the template's 5' end. Because the strands are antiparallel, that keeps the new RNA growing 5'→3'.

*Concepts linked:* Template read 3'→5' · RNA grows 5'→3'

---

**Q140. [Multiple Choice · 1점]**

In the diagram, which nucleotide will RNA polymerase add next at the '?' slot?

**◇ Diagram — Reading a transcription bubble.** Transcription bubble inside an RNA polymerase moving to the right. Top, the nontemplate strand reads 5′ C A T G A C C G T A 3′; bottom, the template strand reads 3′ G T A C T G G C A T 5′. The RNA made so far, 5′ triphosphate C A U G A C C, is paired to the template, and the slot for the next nucleotide, across from the template&#x27;s next base, is empty; the RNA grows 5′ to 3′.

① CTP　 ② dGTP　 ③ GTP　 ④ UTP　 ⑤ ATP

**Answer: ③ GTP**

The template base opposite the slot is C, so the RNA needs G. RNA polymerase builds with ribonucleoside triphosphates, so the building block is GTP, not the DNA precursor dGTP.

*Concepts linked:* Template reading · Ribonucleotide triphosphates

---

**Q141. [Multiple Choice · 1점]**

Which statement correctly contrasts transcription initiation in bacteria and in eukaryotes?

① Bacteria: RNA polymerase II with general transcription factors. Eukaryotes: one polymerase guided by a sigma factor　 ② Bacteria: one RNA polymerase guided by a sigma factor. Eukaryotes: ≥6 general transcription factors plus Pol II for mRNA　 ③ Both use sigma factors to find promoters, but eukaryotes also have three different RNA polymerases　 ④ Eukaryotic Pol II binds the TATA box with no other proteins, while bacteria need activators bound to enhancers　 ⑤ Bacteria have three RNA polymerases for different genes, while eukaryotes have one, guided by a sigma factor

**Answer: ② Bacteria: one RNA polymerase guided by a sigma factor. Eukaryotes: ≥6 general transcription factors plus Pol II for mRNA**

In bacteria, a sigma factor lets the single RNA polymerase recognize promoters. In eukaryotes, general transcription factors bind the promoter, Pol II makes mRNA, and activators on enhancers act through the mediator.

*Concepts linked:* Initiation: bacteria vs eukaryotes

---

**Q142. [Multiple Choice · 2점]**

Cordycepin triphosphate looks like ATP, but its sugar lacks the 3'-OH (3'-deoxyadenosine triphosphate). Using the diagram, what happens if RNA polymerase uses it opposite a template T?

**◇ Diagram — How each ribonucleotide is added.** Adding one ribonucleotide: the template DNA runs 3′ to 5′ down the left; the RNA, running 5′ to 3′, is paired to it and ends in a 3′ OH. An incoming ribonucleoside triphosphate sits opposite the next template base; the 3′ OH attacks the innermost of its three phosphates, and the outer two phosphates leave as pyrophosphate (P-P), so the RNA grows 5′ to 3′.

① It cannot be added at all, because the incoming nucleotide's own 3'-OH performs the attack　 ② It can be added, but then nothing more can be attached, so the RNA ends there　 ③ It is added, and elongation then continues normally from the cordycepin's end　 ④ It is released whole as pyrophosphate without ever joining the chain　 ⑤ It makes the RNA grow in the 3'→5' direction from that point on

**Answer: ② It can be added, but then nothing more can be attached, so the RNA ends there**

Joining uses the chain's 3'-OH and the incoming nucleotide's innermost phosphate, so cordycepin can be added; but it provides no 3'-OH for the next attack, so it terminates the chain.

*Concepts linked:* 3'-OH requirement · Chain termination · Real-world: cordycepin

---

**Q143. [Multiple Choice · 1점]**

A mutation narrows the channel through which ribonucleoside triphosphates enter RNA polymerase. Which stage of transcription is most directly slowed?

① Initiation, because the promoter can no longer be recognized by the polymerase　 ② Termination, because the terminator sequence can no longer be read　 ③ Splicing, because introns can no longer be removed from the RNA　 ④ Elongation, because each added nucleotide must enter through that channel　 ⑤ Capping, because the 5' cap can no longer be added to the RNA

**Answer: ④ Elongation, because each added nucleotide must enter through that channel**

During elongation, RNA polymerase unwinds the DNA and adds nucleotides that enter through a dedicated channel; other channels let DNA in and out and let the RNA exit. Promoter recognition and RNA processing don't depend on NTP entry.

*Concepts linked:* Elongation · RNA polymerase channels

---

**Q144. [Multiple Choice · 2점]**

Genes A and B lie on the same chromosome, but gene A is transcribed rightward and gene B leftward. Which statement is correct?

① They use opposite DNA strands as templates, and each RNA still grows 5'→3'　 ② They use the same template strand, but gene B's RNA grows 3'→5'　 ③ Gene B must lie on a different chromosome, since one chromosome is read in only one direction　 ④ RNA polymerase reads gene A's template 3'→5' and gene B's template 5'→3'　 ⑤ Both RNAs have the same sequence, since they come from the same DNA molecule

**Answer: ① They use opposite DNA strands as templates, and each RNA still grows 5'→3'**

Which strand is the template can differ from gene to gene, set by each promoter's orientation. Because the strands are antiparallel, reading each template 3'→5' sends the polymerases in opposite directions — but every RNA grows 5'→3'.

*Concepts linked:* Template-strand choice · Antiparallel strands · 5'→3' growth

---

**Q145. [Multiple Choice · 2점]**

RNA polymerase has made a 30-nucleotide RNA and is still transcribing the gene. About how many of those 30 nucleotides have already separated from the DNA template?

① About 8　 ② About 16　 ③ None　 ④ All 30　 ⑤ About 22

**Answer: ⑤ About 22**

Inside the transcription bubble (about 14 bp), the RNA–DNA hybrid is only about 8 bp, so just the newest ~8 nucleotides stay paired; the other ~22 have peeled off and left through the exit channel. (16 comes from wrongly subtracting the whole bubble.)

*Concepts linked:* Transcription bubble · RNA–DNA hybrid

---

**Q146. [Multiple Choice · 1점]**

A drug lets RNA polymerase bind promoters and begin transcribing normally, but the polymerase stalls partway through each gene, long before it reaches the terminator. Which stage of transcription does the drug block?

① Initiation　 ② Termination　 ③ Elongation　 ④ Splicing　 ⑤ Polyadenylation

**Answer: ③ Elongation**

Initiation (binding at the promoter and starting) works; the failure happens while nucleotides are being added along the gene, which is elongation. Termination would be the problem only if the polymerase reached the terminator but failed to stop there.

*Concepts linked:* Initiation · Elongation · Termination

---

### RNA Processing: Prok vs Euk

**Q147. [보기 Combination · 3점] Two cells, two timelines**

Which statements in <보기> are correct?

> **(가)** In E. coli, ribosomes attach to an mRNA and start translating while RNA polymerase is still transcribing it.
>
> **(나)** In a human cell, the primary transcript is processed in the nucleus before it is exported.

**<보기>**
- ㄱ. In (나), the primary transcript itself is the mRNA that ribosomes translate.
- ㄴ. The overlap in (가) is possible only because no nuclear envelope separates the DNA from the ribosomes.
- ㄷ. A single E. coli mRNA can carry the information for several genes.

① ㄱ　 ② ㄷ　 ③ ㄱ, ㄴ　 ④ ㄱ, ㄷ　 ⑤ ㄴ, ㄷ

**Answer: ⑤ ㄴ, ㄷ**
- ㄱ — **X** — That is true of prokaryotes. A eukaryotic primary transcript must first be processed (cap, splicing, poly(A) tail) into export-ready mRNA.
- ㄴ — **O** — Prokaryotes lack a nucleus, so transcription and translation share the cytoplasm and can be coupled.
- ㄷ — **O** — Prokaryotic primary transcripts can be polycistronic, coding for more than one protein.

*Concepts linked:* Coupled transcription–translation · Primary transcript · Polycistronic mRNA

---

**Q148. [보기 Combination · 3점] Caps and tails**

Using the diagram, which statements in <보기> are correct?

**◇ Diagram — From primary transcript to mRNA, and alternative splicing.** A primary transcript with four exons (E1 to E4) separated by three introns carries a 5′ cap of 7-methylguanosine joined 5′ to 5′ and a poly-A tail of about 250 adenines. Splicing removes the introns and joins exons; alternative splicing of the same transcript makes mRNA 1 (E1-E2-E4, protein A) or mRNA 2 (E1-E2-E3, protein B), so one gene yields different, related proteins in different cells.

> **(가)** A 5' cap (7-methylguanosine) is added to the 5' end of the primary transcript.
>
> **(나)** About 250 adenines are added to the 3' end (polyadenylation).

**<보기>**
- ㄱ. The poly(A) tail helps export the mRNA to the cytoplasm and protects its 3' end from exonucleases.
- ㄴ. Because it is added to the 5' end, the cap protects the mRNA from exonucleases that attack its 3' end.
- ㄷ. An mRNA lacking its cap would still be translated normally; it would just break down sooner.

① ㄱ　 ② ㄴ　 ③ ㄷ　 ④ ㄱ, ㄷ　 ⑤ ㄴ, ㄷ

**Answer: ① ㄱ**
- ㄱ — **O** — Per the notes, the poly(A) tail aids export to the cytoplasm and protects the 3' end from exonucleases; it also plays a role in transcription termination.
- ㄴ — **X** — The cap guards the 5' end (its unique 5'-to-5' linkage leaves no ordinary free 5' end); the 3' end is protected by the poly(A) tail.
- ㄷ — **X** — Without the cap, the ribosome would not recognize the mRNA, so translation could not occur.

*Concepts linked:* 5' cap · Poly(A) tail · mRNA stability and translation

---

**Q149. [보기 Combination · 3점] Designing a vaccine mRNA**

mRNA vaccines deliver a lab-made mRNA encoding a viral protein into the cytoplasm of human cells, where it is translated. Which statements in <보기> are correct?

> **(가)** Manufacturers give the synthetic mRNA a 5' cap and a poly(A) tail.
>
> **(나)** The synthetic mRNA contains no introns.

**<보기>**
- ㄱ. The cap is needed so the cell's ribosomes recognize the mRNA and translate it.
- ㄴ. Leaving out introns makes sense because the mRNA goes straight to the cytoplasm, while splicing happens in the nucleus.
- ㄷ. The vaccine mRNA must be converted into DNA and enter the nucleus before any protein can be made.

① ㄴ　 ② ㄱ, ㄴ　 ③ ㄱ, ㄷ　 ④ ㄴ, ㄷ　 ⑤ ㄱ, ㄴ, ㄷ

**Answer: ② ㄱ, ㄴ**
- ㄱ — **O** — Without a cap, ribosomes would not recognize the mRNA; the cap also protects it from exonucleases.
- ㄴ — **O** — Processing, including intron removal, happens in the nucleus before export, so an mRNA delivered to the cytoplasm must already be mature.
- ㄷ — **X** — Ribosomes translate mRNA directly in the cytoplasm; nothing needs to be copied into DNA.

*Concepts linked:* 5' cap · Splicing happens in the nucleus · Real-world: mRNA vaccines

---

**Q150. [Matching · 3점] What goes wrong without it?**

Match each feature of gene expression to the consequence of losing it.

**Feature**
A. 5' cap
B. Poly(A) tail
C. RNA splicing
D. Alternative splicing
E. Nuclear pores

**Consequence of losing it**
1. Each gene can make only one version of its protein
2. Export to the cytoplasm suffers, and the 3' end is exposed to exonucleases
3. Ribosomes fail to recognize the mRNA, and its 5' end is exposed to exonucleases
4. Intron sequences stay in the mRNA and are read as if they were coding sequence
5. Processed mRNA cannot leave the nucleus to reach the ribosomes

**Correct matches:**
- A = 5' cap → Ribosomes fail to recognize the mRNA, and its 5' end is exposed to exonucleases
- B = Poly(A) tail → Export to the cytoplasm suffers, and the 3' end is exposed to exonucleases
- C = RNA splicing → Intron sequences stay in the mRNA and are read as if they were coding sequence
- D = Alternative splicing → Each gene can make only one version of its protein
- E = Nuclear pores → Processed mRNA cannot leave the nucleus to reach the ribosomes

Each processing step solves a problem created by the nucleus — protecting the message, getting it out, and removing non-coding introns — while alternative splicing adds variety.

*Concepts linked:* RNA processing · mRNA export

---

**Q151. [True / False · 2점]**

> A company copies a human gene directly from genomic DNA — introns included — places it behind a bacterial promoter, and puts it into E. coli to produce a human protein.

**◇ Diagram — Where transcription and translation happen.** Gene expression in two cells. Prokaryote (no nucleus): RNA polymerase transcribes the DNA while ribosomes already translate the growing mRNA, so transcription and translation are coupled, and one mRNA can carry several genes (polycistronic). Eukaryote: in the nucleus the gene is transcribed into a primary transcript that is processed (5′ cap, splicing of introns, poly-A tail), exported through a nuclear pore, and only then translated by ribosomes in the cytoplasm.

**Claim:** E. coli would likely make a faulty protein, because bacteria don't splice out introns, so its mRNA would still contain the intron sequences.

**Answer: True**

In prokaryotes the primary transcript is the mRNA — there is no splicing step — so intron sequences would be translated as if they were coding sequence.

*Concepts linked:* Introns and splicing · Prokaryotic mRNA · Real-world: recombinant proteins

---

**Q152. [True / False · 1점]**

> In the diagram, mRNA 1 (E1-E2-E4) and mRNA 2 (E1-E2-E3) come from the same primary transcript.

**◇ Diagram — From primary transcript to mRNA, and alternative splicing.** A primary transcript with four exons (E1 to E4) separated by three introns carries a 5′ cap of 7-methylguanosine joined 5′ to 5′ and a poly-A tail of about 250 adenines. Splicing removes the introns and joins exons; alternative splicing of the same transcript makes mRNA 1 (E1-E2-E4, protein A) or mRNA 2 (E1-E2-E3, protein B), so one gene yields different, related proteins in different cells.

**Claim:** Proteins A and B would share the amino-acid sequence encoded by E1 and E2 but differ in the part encoded by E3 versus E4.

**Answer: True**

Shared exons give shared protein segments, and the swapped exon changes one region — which is why alternative splicing yields different but related proteins.

*Concepts linked:* Alternative splicing · Related proteins

---

**Q153. [True / False · 2점]**

> In some forms of β-thalassemia, a single base change at an intron–exon boundary of the β-globin gene stops that intron from being removed correctly.

**Claim:** Because the mutation lies partly in an intron, which is cut out of the mRNA anyway, it cannot affect the β-globin protein.

**Answer: False**

Boundary sequences guide splicing. If an intron is kept or the wrong piece is cut, the mRNA's coding sequence changes, and the protein is abnormal or missing.

*Concepts linked:* RNA splicing · Real-world: β-thalassemia

---

**Q154. [True / False · 1점]**

> A polycistronic mRNA carries the information for several genes on one molecule.

**Claim:** Polycistronic mRNAs are typical of human cells, where alternative splicing produces them.

**Answer: False**

Polycistronic mRNAs are a prokaryotic feature. Alternative splicing is different: it makes several mRNAs from one gene, not one mRNA from several genes.

*Concepts linked:* Polycistronic mRNA vs alternative splicing

---

**Q155. [Multiple Choice · 2점]**

To make a human protein in E. coli, biotechnologists want a DNA version of the gene with no introns. Which approach produces it?

① Use reverse transcriptase to copy the mature, spliced mRNA into DNA　 ② Use RNA polymerase to copy the primary transcript back into DNA　 ③ Add a 5' cap to the genomic gene so bacteria will process it　 ④ Add a poly(A) tail to the gene so that the bacteria will splice it out　 ⑤ Use a sigma factor to cut the introns out of the genomic DNA

**Answer: ① Use reverse transcriptase to copy the mature, spliced mRNA into DNA**

Mature mRNA has already had its introns spliced out; reverse transcriptase (an RNA-dependent DNA polymerase) turns that RNA into intron-free DNA (cDNA). Placed behind a bacterial promoter, it can be expressed correctly in E. coli.

*Concepts linked:* Reverse transcriptase · Spliced mRNA · Real-world: cDNA cloning

---

**Q156. [Multiple Choice · 2점]**

Using the diagram's gene (exons E1–E4), suppose instead — unlike mRNA 2 in the diagram — that E1 and E4 are always kept, while E2 and E3 can each be included or skipped independently (exon order never changes). How many different mRNAs can be made?

**◇ Diagram — From primary transcript to mRNA, and alternative splicing.** A primary transcript with four exons (E1 to E4) separated by three introns carries a 5′ cap of 7-methylguanosine joined 5′ to 5′ and a poly-A tail of about 250 adenines. Splicing removes the introns and joins exons; alternative splicing of the same transcript makes mRNA 1 (E1-E2-E4, protein A) or mRNA 2 (E1-E2-E3, protein B), so one gene yields different, related proteins in different cells.

① 2　 ② 4　 ③ 3　 ④ 6　 ⑤ 8

**Answer: ② 4**

Two independent include/skip choices give 2 × 2 = 4 mRNAs: E1-E4, E1-E2-E4, E1-E3-E4, and E1-E2-E3-E4 — four different, related proteins from one gene.

*Concepts linked:* Alternative splicing · Exon combinations

---

**Q157. [Multiple Choice · 1점]**

Which best explains why eukaryotic primary transcripts must be processed while prokaryotic transcripts generally are not?

① Prokaryotes have no RNA polymerase, so their ribosomes read the DNA directly and no transcript needs processing　 ② Eukaryotic ribosomes can read only DNA, so each RNA must be converted back into DNA before it is exported　 ③ Prokaryotic transcripts are double-stranded, which already protects them from the enzymes that degrade RNA　 ④ Eukaryotes transcribe their genes in the cytoplasm, where unprocessed RNA would be translated too early　 ⑤ A nuclear envelope separates transcription from translation, so the transcript must be modified before export

**Answer: ⑤ A nuclear envelope separates transcription from translation, so the transcript must be modified before export**

The nuclear membrane adds a step between transcription and translation: the message must be processed so it can move from the nucleus to the cytoplasm and be recognized there. Prokaryotic primary transcripts are already mRNA.

*Concepts linked:* Why RNA processing exists

## Module 5 — Protein Structure, Function and Synthesis

### Amino Acids & Peptide Bonds

**Q158. [보기 Combination · 3점] Reading eight side chains**

Using the diagram, which statements in <보기> are correct?

**◇ Diagram — Eight side chains on the same backbone.** Eight panels lettered A to H, each drawing the same backbone, H₃N⁺, a central C and COO⁻ bonded in a row from left to right, with an H bonded above the central C and a different side chain bonded below it. Side chains, from the central C downward: A, CH₃; B, CH₂ then OH; C, CH₂ then COO⁻; D, four CH₂ groups in a row then NH₃⁺; E, CH₂ then SH; F, a second H; G, CH₂ then a benzene ring; H, three CH₂ groups that bond back to the backbone nitrogen (written H₂N⁺ in this panel), closing a five-membered ring of that N, the central C and the three CH₂ groups.

> **(가)** All eight amino acids are drawn as they exist at physiological pH (about 7.4).
>
> **(나)** Panels A–H share the same backbone; only the side chain below the α carbon differs.

**<보기>**
- ㄱ. Side chains C and D carry opposite full charges, so they can form an ionic bond if folding brings them together.
- ㄴ. B can hydrogen-bond with water through its –OH, even though it carries no full charge.
- ㄷ. In a folded water-soluble protein, G's side chain would most likely be found buried in the interior.

① ㄱ　 ② ㄷ　 ③ ㄱ, ㄴ　 ④ ㄱ, ㄷ　 ⑤ ㄱ, ㄴ, ㄷ

**Answer: ⑤ ㄱ, ㄴ, ㄷ**
- ㄱ — **O** — C's –COO- is negative (acidic) and D's –NH3+ is positive (basic); opposite charges attract.
- ㄴ — **O** — Oxygen is electronegative, so the –OH is polar: B is a polar, uncharged, hydrophilic side chain.
- ㄷ — **O** — A CH2 plus a benzene ring is all carbon and hydrogen — nonpolar and hydrophobic — so it clusters away from water inside the fold.

*Concepts linked:* R-group classes · Ionic bonds between side chains · Polarity from electronegativity (Module 2)

---

**Q159. [보기 Combination · 3점] One backbone, twenty personalities**

Which statements in <보기> are correct?

> **(가)** Every amino acid has an amino group, a carboxyl group and an H bonded to its α carbon; only the fourth group, the R group, differs.
>
> **(나)** At physiological pH, a free amino acid carries H3N+ and COO-.

**<보기>**
- ㄱ. Because of (나), every amino acid in a folded protein carries both an H3N+ and a COO- on its backbone.
- ㄴ. Two different proteins of the same length share the same repeating backbone (N–C–C–N–C–C…); they differ only in the side chains attached to it.
- ㄷ. Proline is the only amino acid whose α carbon carries two identical groups.

① ㄴ　 ② ㄷ　 ③ ㄱ, ㄴ　 ④ ㄱ, ㄷ　 ⑤ ㄴ, ㄷ

**Answer: ① ㄴ**
- ㄱ — **X** — Inside a chain, the amino and carboxyl groups are used up in peptide bonds. Only the first amino acid keeps a free amino group (N-terminus) and only the last keeps a free carboxyl group (C-terminus).
- ㄴ — **O** — Peptide bonds always join the same backbone atoms, so sequence differences show up only in the R groups sticking out from the chain.
- ㄷ — **X** — That's glycine, whose R group is a second H — so its α carbon isn't asymmetric. Proline's α carbon carries four different groups; its side chain loops back to the nitrogen.

*Concepts linked:* Amino acid structure · Charged ends at physiological pH · N- and C-terminus

---

**Q160. [Matching · 3점] Which side chain fits?**

Using the diagram, match each description to the side chain that fits it.

**◇ Diagram — Eight side chains on the same backbone.** Eight panels lettered A to H, each drawing the same backbone, H₃N⁺, a central C and COO⁻ bonded in a row from left to right, with an H bonded above the central C and a different side chain bonded below it. Side chains, from the central C downward: A, CH₃; B, CH₂ then OH; C, CH₂ then COO⁻; D, four CH₂ groups in a row then NH₃⁺; E, CH₂ then SH; F, a second H; G, CH₂ then a benzene ring; H, three CH₂ groups that bond back to the backbone nitrogen (written H₂N⁺ in this panel), closing a five-membered ring of that N, the central C and the three CH₂ groups.

**Description**
A. Gives the backbone the most freedom to rotate and bend
B. Restricts rotation of the backbone, limiting folding around it
C. Can form a covalent cross-bridge with another copy of itself
D. Positively charged at physiological pH
E. Polar but uncharged; hydrogen-bonds with water

**Side chain**
1. H: ring bonded back to the nitrogen
2. E: –CH2–SH
3. B: –CH2–OH
4. F: –H
5. D: –(CH2)4–NH3+

**Correct matches:**
- A = Gives the backbone the most freedom to rotate and bend → F: –H
- B = Restricts rotation of the backbone, limiting folding around it → H: ring bonded back to the nitrogen
- C = Can form a covalent cross-bridge with another copy of itself → E: –CH2–SH
- D = Positively charged at physiological pH → D: –(CH2)4–NH3+
- E = Polar but uncharged; hydrogen-bonds with water → B: –CH2–OH

F is glycine (side chain H): tiny, so the C–N bond rotates freely. H is proline: its side chain loops back to the amino nitrogen and locks rotation. E is cysteine: two –SH groups can join as an S–S disulfide bridge. D is lysine (basic, +). B is serine: its –OH is polar but uncharged.

*Concepts linked:* Special amino acids: glycine, proline, cysteine · Charged vs polar side chains

---

**Q161. [True / False · 1점]**

> In the diagram, H's side chain is bonded to the backbone at two points: the α carbon and the nitrogen.

**◇ Diagram — Eight side chains on the same backbone.** Eight panels lettered A to H, each drawing the same backbone, H₃N⁺, a central C and COO⁻ bonded in a row from left to right, with an H bonded above the central C and a different side chain bonded below it. Side chains, from the central C downward: A, CH₃; B, CH₂ then OH; C, CH₂ then COO⁻; D, four CH₂ groups in a row then NH₃⁺; E, CH₂ then SH; F, a second H; G, CH₂ then a benzene ring; H, three CH₂ groups that bond back to the backbone nitrogen (written H₂N⁺ in this panel), closing a five-membered ring of that N, the central C and the three CH₂ groups.

**Claim:** Because its side chain forms a ring, H gives the backbone more freedom to rotate than F does.

**Answer: False**

The ring locks the N–Cα bond, restricting rotation and limiting folding around proline (H). F (glycine, side chain –H) is the most flexible: its tiny side chain lets the C–N bond rotate freely.

*Concepts linked:* Proline restricts rotation · Glycine adds flexibility

---

**Q162. [True / False · 2점]**

> Collagen, the main protein of bone and tendon, is a tight triple helix. Every third amino acid is glycine, and each glycine sits at the crowded centre where the three chains meet.

**Claim:** A mutation replacing one of these glycines with a bulkier amino acid can weaken collagen, because only glycine's single H atom fits in that central position.

**Answer: True**

Glycine has the smallest side chain (H). A larger side chain can't pack into the helix centre and distorts the triple helix — the cause of many cases of osteogenesis imperfecta (brittle bone disease).

*Concepts linked:* Glycine: the smallest side chain · Real-world: brittle bone disease

---

**Q163. [True / False · 2점]**

> A perm breaks the disulfide bonds in hair keratin with a reducing agent, holds the hair around curlers, then re-forms disulfide bonds with an oxidizing agent.

**Claim:** The curl lasts because the disulfide bonds re-form between exactly the same pairs of cysteines as before.

**Answer: False**

While the hair is held in its new shape, cysteines that weren't partners before sit side by side, and the oxidizer links them into new S–S bridges. Covalent bridges in new positions hold the new shape; re-forming the old pairs would simply restore the original shape.

*Concepts linked:* Disulfide cross-bridges · Real-world: perms

---

**Q164. [True / False · 1점]**

> One lab reports a tripeptide as K-G-V. Another lab reports a tripeptide as V-G-K.

**Claim:** These are the same molecule: both contain lysine, glycine and valine, just written from opposite ends.

**Answer: False**

Sequences are written from the N-terminus to the C-terminus. K-G-V has lysine at the free amino end; V-G-K has valine there and lysine at the carboxyl end. A backbone has direction (amino end ≠ carboxyl end), so these are different peptides.

*Concepts linked:* Primary structure is written N→C · Polypeptides have direction

---

**Q165. [True / False · 2점]**

> In sickle-cell disease, one glutamic acid (side chain –CH2–CH2–COO-) on the surface of hemoglobin is replaced by valine (side chain –CH(CH3)2).

**Claim:** The swap makes that surface patch more attractive to water, so the altered hemoglobin may dissolve even better in the red blood cell's cytoplasm.

**Answer: False**

The opposite: a charged, hydrophilic side chain is replaced by a nonpolar one, creating a hydrophobic patch. When hemoglobin isn't carrying oxygen, these patches stick to neighbouring hemoglobin molecules, which link into long fibres that bend red blood cells into sickles.

*Concepts linked:* Hydrophobic vs charged side chains · Real-world: sickle-cell disease

---

**Q166. [True / False · 1점]**

> Proteins are built from 20 kinds of amino acids, like words built from an alphabet.

**Claim:** A chain only 3 amino acids long already has 8,000 possible sequences.

**Answer: True**

Each of the 3 positions can hold any of 20 amino acids: 20 × 20 × 20 = 8,000. For a 300-amino-acid protein the number is 20^300 — one reason proteins can be so diverse and versatile.

*Concepts linked:* 20 amino acids as an alphabet · Protein diversity

---

**Q167. [True / False · 1점]**

> Keratin in hair, the enzyme amylase in saliva, and antibodies in blood are all proteins with very different jobs.

**Claim:** Their different jobs come mainly from different kinds of bonds in their backbones rather than from their amino acid sequences.

**Answer: False**

All proteins share the same backbone, linked by the same peptide bonds. What differs is the sequence of side chains, which folds each chain into its own shape — and shape determines function.

*Concepts linked:* Proteins are diverse and versatile · Sequence → shape → function

---

**Q168. [Multiple Choice · 2점]**

A protein engineer replaces a glycine in a tight hairpin turn with alanine (side chain –CH3). The protein no longer folds correctly. Which explanation fits best?

① Alanine's side chain is charged and repels nearby side chains in the turn　 ② Alanine's side chain bonds back to its own backbone nitrogen, closing a ring　 ③ Its side chain limits backbone rotation, so the chain can't bend as sharply　 ④ Alanine forms a disulfide bridge that locks the turn in the wrong place　 ⑤ Alanine can't form peptide bonds because its α carbon is asymmetric

**Answer: ③ Its side chain limits backbone rotation, so the chain can't bend as sharply**

Glycine's side chain is just H, so the backbone can rotate freely about the C–N bond — the flexibility a tight turn needs. Even a small –CH3 restricts that rotation. Alanine is nonpolar and uncharged, has no –SH for disulfides and no ring (that's proline), and every amino acid forms peptide bonds.

*Concepts linked:* Glycine flexibility · Sequence determines folding

---

**Q169. [Multiple Choice · 2점]**

The peptide Val–Gly–Ala–His–Lys is assembled from free amino acids by condensation reactions. How many water molecules are released, and which amino acid carries the free amino group?

① 5 water molecules; Val　 ② 4 water molecules; Lys　 ③ 5 water molecules; Lys　 ④ 3 water molecules; Val　 ⑤ 4 water molecules; Val

**Answer: ⑤ 4 water molecules; Val**

Five amino acids are joined by 4 peptide bonds, and each dehydration releases one H2O. Sequences are written from the amino (N) end to the carboxyl (C) end, so Val, written first, keeps the free amino group, and Lys keeps the free carboxyl group.

*Concepts linked:* Peptide bonds form by dehydration · N- and C-terminus · Writing sequences N→C

---

**Q170. [Multiple Choice · 1점]**

Using the diagram, a side chain on a protein's surface changes from D to B. What is the main change at that spot?

**◇ Diagram — Eight side chains on the same backbone.** Eight panels lettered A to H, each drawing the same backbone, H₃N⁺, a central C and COO⁻ bonded in a row from left to right, with an H bonded above the central C and a different side chain bonded below it. Side chains, from the central C downward: A, CH₃; B, CH₂ then OH; C, CH₂ then COO⁻; D, four CH₂ groups in a row then NH₃⁺; E, CH₂ then SH; F, a second H; G, CH₂ then a benzene ring; H, three CH₂ groups that bond back to the backbone nitrogen (written H₂N⁺ in this panel), closing a five-membered ring of that N, the central C and the three CH₂ groups.

① It becomes negatively charged and repels nearby negative groups　 ② It loses its positive charge but can still hydrogen-bond with water　 ③ It becomes nonpolar and tends to move into the folded protein's interior　 ④ It gains the ability to form a disulfide bridge with another copy　 ⑤ Nothing changes, since both side chains end in a polar group

**Answer: ② It loses its positive charge but can still hydrogen-bond with water**

D (–(CH2)4–NH3+, lysine) is basic and positively charged at physiological pH; B (–CH2–OH, serine) is polar but uncharged. The spot loses its positive charge — and any ionic bond it made — but B's –OH still hydrogen-bonds with water, so it can stay at the surface.

*Concepts linked:* Basic vs polar side chains · Charge at physiological pH

---

**Q171. [Multiple Choice · 1점]**

Using the diagram, which pair of side chains, brought together by folding, could join two parts of a protein with a covalent bond?

**◇ Diagram — Eight side chains on the same backbone.** Eight panels lettered A to H, each drawing the same backbone, H₃N⁺, a central C and COO⁻ bonded in a row from left to right, with an H bonded above the central C and a different side chain bonded below it. Side chains, from the central C downward: A, CH₃; B, CH₂ then OH; C, CH₂ then COO⁻; D, four CH₂ groups in a row then NH₃⁺; E, CH₂ then SH; F, a second H; G, CH₂ then a benzene ring; H, three CH₂ groups that bond back to the backbone nitrogen (written H₂N⁺ in this panel), closing a five-membered ring of that N, the central C and the three CH₂ groups.

① E and E (two –CH2–SH groups)　 ② C and D (–COO- and –NH3+)　 ③ B and B (two –CH2–OH groups)　 ④ A and G (–CH3 and the ring)　 ⑤ F and F (two –H atoms)

**Answer: ① E and E (two –CH2–SH groups)**

Two cysteine –SH groups can be joined into an S–S disulfide bond — a covalent cross-bridge, far stronger than the ionic bond C and D can form, the hydrogen bonds between B's –OH groups, or the weak van der Waals contacts between nonpolar A and G.

*Concepts linked:* Disulfide bonds are covalent · Bond strengths (Module 2)

---

### Levels of Protein Structure

**Q172. [보기 Combination · 3점] Two backbone patterns**

Using the diagram, which statements in <보기> are correct?

**◇ Diagram — Backbone hydrogen bonds in a helix and a sheet.** Left, labelled helix: a ribbon coil rising upward through residues numbered 1 at the bottom (N-terminus) to 11 at the top (C-terminus), its front strands climbing from lower left to upper right; dashed lines join residues 1–5, 2–6, 3–7, 4–8, 5–9, 6–10 and 7–11, the 1–5 line labelled C=O at residue 1 and H–N at residue 5, and short stubs labelled R point outward from residues 4 and 8 on the left edge and 2, 6 and 10 on the right edge. Right, labelled sheet: two strands drawn as broad arrows, the top one running from N at its left end to C at its arrowhead on the right and the bottom one from N at its right end to C at its arrowhead on the left, each with five residues directly across from the other strand&#x27;s, and every facing pair joined by a vertical dashed line, one of them labelled C=O at the top strand and H–N at the bottom strand. Each sheet residue carries a circle labelled R on the outer side of its strand, alternately filled and hollow along each strand, with facing residues matching; a key reads filled R circle, R above the sheet; hollow R circle, R below the sheet; dashed line, hydrogen bond.

> **(가)** The helix and the sheet are parts of the same protein.
>
> **(나)** Each R marks a side chain sticking out from the backbone.

**<보기>**
- ㄱ. Every dashed bond links a backbone C=O to a backbone N–H; none involves an R group.
- ㄴ. The sheet's two strands are parallel, since they lie side by side.
- ㄷ. In the helix, the C=O of residue 3 bonds to the N–H of residue 7.

① ㄴ　 ② ㄷ　 ③ ㄱ, ㄴ　 ④ ㄱ, ㄷ　 ⑤ ㄱ, ㄴ, ㄷ

**Answer: ④ ㄱ, ㄷ**
- ㄱ — **O** — Secondary structure comes from hydrogen bonds between backbone groups of nearby amino acids; side chains aren't part of these bonds.
- ㄴ — **X** — Side by side doesn't mean the same direction: the top strand runs N→C left to right and the bottom strand right to left, so they're antiparallel. Sheets can be either.
- ㄷ — **O** — Each C=O bonds to the N–H four residues along (1–5, 2–6, 3–7…), which ties each turn of the helix to the next.

*Concepts linked:* Secondary structure: backbone H-bonds only · α helix · β sheet

---

**Q173. [보기 Combination · 3점] What holds the fold together?**

Using the diagram, which statements in <보기> are correct?

**◇ Diagram — Four contacts inside one folded chain.** One chain, drawn as a thick line from N to C, folds back and forth inside a roughly round dashed outline, with the word water written outside the outline. Four contacts between different parts of the chain are lettered: A, CH₂–S–S–CH₂, one CH₂ on each of two segments; B, just inside the outline at the top, COO⁻ on one segment facing ⁺H₃N on another, joined by a dotted line; C, OH on one segment facing O=C on another, joined by a dotted line; D, in the middle of the fold, a CH(CH₃)₂, two CH₃ groups and a six-membered ring reaching in from the segments around it, packed together inside a lightly shaded oval.

> **(가)** One sample is treated with a reducing agent that turns each –S–S– back into two –SH groups.
>
> **(나)** A second sample is moved from pH 7 into strong acid (pH 1).

**<보기>**
- ㄱ. In (나), contact B gets stronger, because the extra H+ adds positive charge to both of its groups.
- ㄴ. In (나), contact D is lost, because nonpolar groups pick up H+ and become charged.
- ㄷ. (가) breaks contact A but leaves the protein's primary structure unchanged.

① ㄴ　 ② ㄷ　 ③ ㄱ, ㄷ　 ④ ㄴ, ㄷ　 ⑤ ㄱ, ㄴ, ㄷ

**Answer: ② ㄷ**
- ㄱ — **X** — COO- picks up H+ and becomes uncharged COOH, so the attraction between opposite charges at B is lost, not strengthened.
- ㄴ — **X** — Nonpolar groups such as –CH3 don't gain or lose H+ at all, so D is not changed by pH. pH acts on ionizable groups like those at B.
- ㄷ — **O** — A disulfide bridge links two side chains; it isn't part of the backbone, so the amino acid sequence (held by peptide bonds) is untouched.

*Concepts linked:* Tertiary interactions · Disulfide vs ionic bonds · pH and ionization (Module 2)

---

**Q174. [Matching · 3점] Four ways to unfold a protein**

Using the diagram, match each treatment to the contact it most directly disrupts.

**◇ Diagram — Four contacts inside one folded chain.** One chain, drawn as a thick line from N to C, folds back and forth inside a roughly round dashed outline, with the word water written outside the outline. Four contacts between different parts of the chain are lettered: A, CH₂–S–S–CH₂, one CH₂ on each of two segments; B, just inside the outline at the top, COO⁻ on one segment facing ⁺H₃N on another, joined by a dotted line; C, OH on one segment facing O=C on another, joined by a dotted line; D, in the middle of the fold, a CH(CH₃)₂, two CH₃ groups and a six-membered ring reaching in from the segments around it, packed together inside a lightly shaded oval.

**Treatment**
A. A reducing agent that adds H to each sulfur
B. Concentrated salt, whose ions crowd around charged groups
C. Urea, which hydrogen-bonds to polar groups in place of their partners
D. A detergent whose nonpolar tails coat nonpolar groups

**Contact**
1. D: the clustered nonpolar groups
2. B: COO- facing H3N+
3. C: OH facing O=C
4. A: CH2–S–S–CH2

**Correct matches:**
- A = A reducing agent that adds H to each sulfur → A: CH2–S–S–CH2
- B = Concentrated salt, whose ions crowd around charged groups → B: COO- facing H3N+
- C = Urea, which hydrogen-bonds to polar groups in place of their partners → C: OH facing O=C
- D = A detergent whose nonpolar tails coat nonpolar groups → D: the clustered nonpolar groups

Each treatment targets one interaction type: reducing agents split covalent disulfides (A); ions shield the charges of an ionic bond (B); urea competes for hydrogen bonds (C); detergents surround nonpolar groups so they no longer need to hide from water (D).

*Concepts linked:* Tertiary interactions · Bond types (Module 2) · Denaturation

---

**Q175. [Matching · 3점] Which level of structure?**

Match each observation to the level of protein structure it describes.

**Observation**
A. A mutation swaps the 6th amino acid of the chain for a different one
B. Part of a keratin chain coils into a right-handed spiral held by backbone hydrogen bonds
C. A single enzyme chain folds so that amino acids 40, 95 and 210 come together as its active site
D. Two identical chains must pair up before an enzyme works

**Level**
1. Primary
2. Tertiary
3. Quaternary
4. Secondary

**Correct matches:**
- A = A mutation swaps the 6th amino acid of the chain for a different one → Primary
- B = Part of a keratin chain coils into a right-handed spiral held by backbone hydrogen bonds → Secondary
- C = A single enzyme chain folds so that amino acids 40, 95 and 210 come together as its active site → Tertiary
- D = Two identical chains must pair up before an enzyme works → Quaternary

Primary = the sequence; secondary = local backbone H-bond patterns (α helix, β sheet); tertiary = the whole folded shape of one chain, which brings distant residues together; quaternary = how separate chains (subunits) assemble.

*Concepts linked:* Four levels of protein structure

---

**Q176. [True / False · 2점]**

> An isolated 11-residue segment folds into the helix in the diagram, with residue 1 at its N-terminus.

**◇ Diagram — Backbone hydrogen bonds in a helix and a sheet.** Left, labelled helix: a ribbon coil rising upward through residues numbered 1 at the bottom (N-terminus) to 11 at the top (C-terminus), its front strands climbing from lower left to upper right; dashed lines join residues 1–5, 2–6, 3–7, 4–8, 5–9, 6–10 and 7–11, the 1–5 line labelled C=O at residue 1 and H–N at residue 5, and short stubs labelled R point outward from residues 4 and 8 on the left edge and 2, 6 and 10 on the right edge. Right, labelled sheet: two strands drawn as broad arrows, the top one running from N at its left end to C at its arrowhead on the right and the bottom one from N at its right end to C at its arrowhead on the left, each with five residues directly across from the other strand&#x27;s, and every facing pair joined by a vertical dashed line, one of them labelled C=O at the top strand and H–N at the bottom strand. Each sheet residue carries a circle labelled R on the outer side of its strand, alternately filled and hollow along each strand, with facing residues matching; a key reads filled R circle, R above the sheet; hollow R circle, R below the sheet; dashed line, hydrogen bond.

**Claim:** Residues 8–11 have their N–H groups hydrogen-bonded within the segment, but their C=O groups have no partner.

**Answer: True**

Each C=O of residue n pairs with the N–H of residue n + 4. Residues 8–11 would need partners 12–15, which don't exist, while their own N–H groups receive bonds from residues 4–7. At the other end, residues 1–4 have unpaired N–H groups.

*Concepts linked:* α helix: residue n bonds to n + 4

---

**Q177. [True / False · 2점]**

> In the diagram, contact D sits in the middle of the fold, surrounded by the chain, while water lies outside.

**◇ Diagram — Four contacts inside one folded chain.** One chain, drawn as a thick line from N to C, folds back and forth inside a roughly round dashed outline, with the word water written outside the outline. Four contacts between different parts of the chain are lettered: A, CH₂–S–S–CH₂, one CH₂ on each of two segments; B, just inside the outline at the top, COO⁻ on one segment facing ⁺H₃N on another, joined by a dotted line; C, OH on one segment facing O=C on another, joined by a dotted line; D, in the middle of the fold, a CH(CH₃)₂, two CH₃ groups and a six-membered ring reaching in from the segments around it, packed together inside a lightly shaded oval.

**Claim:** D holds together mainly because nonpolar groups attract one another strongly, much as the opposite charges at B do.

**Answer: False**

Nonpolar groups attract one another only weakly (van der Waals forces). They cluster mainly because water molecules hydrogen-bond with each other and push nonpolar groups aside (Module 2), so the groups end up hidden in the interior.

*Concepts linked:* Hydrophobic interior · Water drives nonpolar clustering (Module 2)

---

**Q178. [True / False · 1점]**

> Insulin consists of two short chains, A and B, cut from one longer precursor polypeptide after it has folded.

**Claim:** In mature insulin, the A and B chains are joined end to end by peptide bonds.

**Answer: False**

Once the connecting segment is cut out, the chains are held together by two disulfide bonds — covalent bridges between cysteine side chains, not backbone peptide bonds. Cutting an inactive precursor into its active form is also a post-translational modification.

*Concepts linked:* Disulfide bridges between chains · Post-translational cleavage

---

**Q179. [True / False · 2점]**

> A peptide alternates nonpolar valine with charged lysine or glutamate: Val–Lys–Val–Glu–Val–Lys–Val–Glu.

**Claim:** Stretched out as a strand, all its valine side chains point to one side and all its charged side chains to the other.

**Answer: True**

R groups alternate sides along an extended chain, so residues 1, 3, 5 and 7 (Val) share one side and residues 2, 4, 6 and 8 the other — one nonpolar face and one charged face. Such a strand can lie with its nonpolar face against the protein interior and its charged face toward water.

*Concepts linked:* R groups alternate sides · Nonpolar and charged faces

---

**Q180. [True / False · 1점]**

> In the diagram's sheet, a third strand is added below the bottom strand, running from N at the left to C at the right.

**◇ Diagram — Backbone hydrogen bonds in a helix and a sheet.** Left, labelled helix: a ribbon coil rising upward through residues numbered 1 at the bottom (N-terminus) to 11 at the top (C-terminus), its front strands climbing from lower left to upper right; dashed lines join residues 1–5, 2–6, 3–7, 4–8, 5–9, 6–10 and 7–11, the 1–5 line labelled C=O at residue 1 and H–N at residue 5, and short stubs labelled R point outward from residues 4 and 8 on the left edge and 2, 6 and 10 on the right edge. Right, labelled sheet: two strands drawn as broad arrows, the top one running from N at its left end to C at its arrowhead on the right and the bottom one from N at its right end to C at its arrowhead on the left, each with five residues directly across from the other strand&#x27;s, and every facing pair joined by a vertical dashed line, one of them labelled C=O at the top strand and H–N at the bottom strand. Each sheet residue carries a circle labelled R on the outer side of its strand, alternately filled and hollow along each strand, with facing residues matching; a key reads filled R circle, R above the sheet; hollow R circle, R below the sheet; dashed line, hydrogen bond.

**Claim:** The new strand is antiparallel to the bottom strand and parallel to the top strand.

**Answer: True**

The bottom strand runs N→C right to left and the top strand left to right. A strand running left to right is opposite to the bottom strand (antiparallel) and matches the top one (parallel). Both arrangements occur in sheets.

*Concepts linked:* β sheets: parallel and antiparallel strands

**Q181. [Multiple Choice · 2점]**

Proline's side chain bonds to its own backbone nitrogen, so in a chain that nitrogen carries no H. If residue 9 of the helix in the diagram were proline, which hydrogen bond could not form?

**◇ Diagram — Backbone hydrogen bonds in a helix and a sheet.** Left, labelled helix: a ribbon coil rising upward through residues numbered 1 at the bottom (N-terminus) to 11 at the top (C-terminus), its front strands climbing from lower left to upper right; dashed lines join residues 1–5, 2–6, 3–7, 4–8, 5–9, 6–10 and 7–11, the 1–5 line labelled C=O at residue 1 and H–N at residue 5, and short stubs labelled R point outward from residues 4 and 8 on the left edge and 2, 6 and 10 on the right edge. Right, labelled sheet: two strands drawn as broad arrows, the top one running from N at its left end to C at its arrowhead on the right and the bottom one from N at its right end to C at its arrowhead on the left, each with five residues directly across from the other strand&#x27;s, and every facing pair joined by a vertical dashed line, one of them labelled C=O at the top strand and H–N at the bottom strand. Each sheet residue carries a circle labelled R on the outer side of its strand, alternately filled and hollow along each strand, with facing residues matching; a key reads filled R circle, R above the sheet; hollow R circle, R below the sheet; dashed line, hydrogen bond.

① The one between residue 6's C=O and residue 10's N–H　 ② The one between residue 4's C=O and residue 8's N–H　 ③ The one between residue 7's C=O and residue 11's N–H　 ④ The one between residue 5's C=O and residue 9's N–H　 ⑤ None, since helix H-bonds involve only backbone atoms

**Answer: ④ The one between residue 5's C=O and residue 9's N–H**

Residue 9's N–H is the partner of residue 5's C=O (n to n + 4). Proline has no N–H to give, so that bond is lost. Here a side chain matters after all: proline's ring changes a backbone atom itself, one reason proline disrupts helices.

*Concepts linked:* α helix pattern · Proline restricts the backbone

---

**Q182. [Multiple Choice · 2점]**

An enzyme is a homodimer: two identical chains held together by side-chain contacts. A mutation changes one amino acid on the surface where the two chains touch. Which outcome is most likely?

① Nothing changes, since surface amino acids don't affect quaternary structure　 ② The two chains now differ in sequence, so the enzyme becomes a heterodimer　 ③ Each chain can still fold, but the pair may not assemble into the enzyme　 ④ The peptide bonds that join the two chains to each other are broken　 ⑤ The chains form a disulfide bridge at that spot, locking them together

**Answer: ③ Each chain can still fold, but the pair may not assemble into the enzyme**

Subunits are held together by the same side-chain interactions as tertiary structure, so a changed contact residue can block pairing. Both chains come from the same mutated gene, so they're still identical (still a homodimer), and subunits aren't joined by peptide bonds.

*Concepts linked:* Quaternary structure · Homodimer vs heterodimer

---

**Q183. [Multiple Choice · 2점]**

An enzyme is unfolded with chemicals that break its hydrogen bonds and disulfide bonds, leaving its peptide bonds intact. When the chemicals are slowly removed, the chain refolds into its active shape. What does this show?

① The amino acid sequence alone holds the information for the folded shape　 ② The enzyme's shape was stored in disulfide bonds that survived the treatment　 ③ Peptide bonds broke and re-formed in their original order　 ④ Refolding needed a correctly folded copy of the enzyme as a template　 ⑤ Secondary and tertiary structure don't depend on the sequence

**Answer: ① The amino acid sequence alone holds the information for the folded shape**

Nothing but the sequence (primary structure) remained, yet the original shape returned, so primary structure determines secondary and tertiary structure. (A cooked egg white can't refold because its unfolded chains tangle into aggregates instead.)

*Concepts linked:* Primary structure determines higher levels · Denaturation and refolding

---

**Q184. [Multiple Choice · 2점]**

In the diagram's sheet, R groups alternate between above the sheet (filled) and below it (hollow). The sheet lies against the protein's nonpolar interior on one face, with water on the other. Which pattern along each strand fits best?

**◇ Diagram — Backbone hydrogen bonds in a helix and a sheet.** Left, labelled helix: a ribbon coil rising upward through residues numbered 1 at the bottom (N-terminus) to 11 at the top (C-terminus), its front strands climbing from lower left to upper right; dashed lines join residues 1–5, 2–6, 3–7, 4–8, 5–9, 6–10 and 7–11, the 1–5 line labelled C=O at residue 1 and H–N at residue 5, and short stubs labelled R point outward from residues 4 and 8 on the left edge and 2, 6 and 10 on the right edge. Right, labelled sheet: two strands drawn as broad arrows, the top one running from N at its left end to C at its arrowhead on the right and the bottom one from N at its right end to C at its arrowhead on the left, each with five residues directly across from the other strand&#x27;s, and every facing pair joined by a vertical dashed line, one of them labelled C=O at the top strand and H–N at the bottom strand. Each sheet residue carries a circle labelled R on the outer side of its strand, alternately filled and hollow along each strand, with facing residues matching; a key reads filled R circle, R above the sheet; hollow R circle, R below the sheet; dashed line, hydrogen bond.

① Nonpolar side chains at every position along the strand　 ② Charged side chains at every position along the strand　 ③ Nonpolar side chains on one strand, polar side chains on the other　 ④ Glycine at every position so that the sheet can bend　 ⑤ Nonpolar and polar side chains alternating along the strand

**Answer: ⑤ Nonpolar and polar side chains alternating along the strand**

Consecutive residues point to opposite faces, so alternating nonpolar and polar residues put every nonpolar side chain on the interior face and every polar one on the water face. An all-nonpolar strand would leave half its side chains facing water.

*Concepts linked:* R groups alternate sides · Faces of a β sheet

### Translation Machinery & Genetic Code

**Q185. [보기 Combination · 3점] From gene to anticodon**

Using the diagram, which statements in <보기> are correct?

**◇ Diagram — One codon traced from gene to tRNA.** Left: a tRNA cloverleaf, one strand folded into base-paired stems, with the acceptor stem at the top, where one strand ends at 5′ and the other continues past the stem as unpaired C, C, A to the 3′ end, labelled amino acid attachment site; below are a left loop, a right loop and a bottom loop labelled anticodon loop, in which three empty boxes are labelled anticodon. Right: four aligned rows of three bases in boxes: DNA nontemplate strand 5′ G C A 3′; DNA template strand 3′ C G T 5′; mRNA codon 5′ G C A 3′; tRNA anticodon 3′ C G U 5′. An arrow labelled transcription runs from the template-strand row to the mRNA row, and an arrow labelled base pairing runs from the mRNA row to the anticodon row.

> **(가)** The four rows show one codon's worth of a gene, its mRNA and the tRNA that reads it.
>
> **(나)** Unlabelled sequences are written 5'→3'.

**<보기>**
- ㄱ. The mRNA is copied from the nontemplate strand, which is why their sequences match.
- ㄴ. Written in the standard 5'→3' direction, the anticodon is UGC.
- ㄷ. The anticodon has the same sequence and orientation as the DNA template strand, except that it has U where the template has T.

① ㄱ　 ② ㄴ　 ③ ㄱ, ㄴ　 ④ ㄱ, ㄷ　 ⑤ ㄴ, ㄷ

**Answer: ⑤ ㄴ, ㄷ**
- ㄱ — **X** — RNA polymerase reads the template strand. The mRNA matches the nontemplate strand only because both are complementary to the template (Module 4).
- ㄴ — **O** — The anticodon is 3'-CGU-5'; read from its 5' end it is 5'-UGC-3'.
- ㄷ — **O** — The template strand and the anticodon are both complementary to the codon, so they match base for base (3'-CGT-5' vs 3'-CGU-5'), with U in RNA where DNA has T.

*Concepts linked:* Codon–anticodon pairing is antiparallel · Template vs nontemplate strand (Module 4)

---

**Q186. [보기 Combination · 3점] Freeze-frame on a ribosome**

Using the diagram, which statements in <보기> are correct?

**◇ Diagram — A ribosome partway along an mRNA.** A ribosome, its large subunit on top and its small subunit below, sits on an mRNA that runs between them from 5′ on the left to 3′ on the right, written in codons as 5′ … ACC AUG GCU UGG AAA UAG GCA … 3′. Inside the large subunit are three slots labelled E, P and A, from left to right, directly over the codons AUG, GCU and UGG; ACC lies to the left of the ribosome and AAA, UAG and GCA to its right. The E slot holds a tRNA with anticodon 3′-UAC-5′ paired to AUG and nothing attached; the P slot holds a tRNA with anticodon 3′-CGA-5′ paired to GCU, carrying a two-bead chain whose bead Ala is bonded to the tRNA and whose bead Met is the free end, reaching up out of the large subunit; the A slot is empty above UGG. Outside the ribosome, upper right, is a tRNA carrying one bead labelled ? with anticodon ? ? ?..

> **(가)** UGG codes for Trp and AAA for Lys; UAG is a stop codon.
>
> **(나)** The ribosome will keep translating this mRNA until the polypeptide is finished.

**<보기>**
- ㄱ. The incoming tRNA marked ? must carry the anticodon 3'-ACC-5' and the amino acid Trp.
- ㄴ. The finished polypeptide will be Met–Ala–Trp–Lys, with Met at its N-terminus.
- ㄷ. The tRNA in the E site is uncharged and is about to leave the ribosome.

① ㄱ　 ② ㄷ　 ③ ㄱ, ㄴ　 ④ ㄴ, ㄷ　 ⑤ ㄱ, ㄴ, ㄷ

**Answer: ⑤ ㄱ, ㄴ, ㄷ**
- ㄱ — **O** — The A site holds codon 5'-UGG-3'; its antiparallel partner is 3'-ACC-5', and the tRNA with that anticodon is charged with Trp.
- ㄴ — **O** — AUG GCU UGG AAA, then UAG (stop): Met–Ala–Trp–Lys. Met, the first amino acid, keeps the free amino group.
- ㄷ — **O** — It delivered Met, passed it on when the first peptide bond formed, and now exits empty from the E site.

*Concepts linked:* A, P and E sites · Codon–anticodon pairing · Start and stop codons

---

**Q187. [Matching · 3점] Which component is responsible?**

Match each observation to the translation component that best explains it.

**Observation**
A. A tRNA with a mutated anticodon inserts its amino acid at the wrong codons
B. A tRNA is loaded with the wrong amino acid but still pairs with the correct codon
C. An antibiotic binds rRNA in the large subunit and blocks peptide bond formation
D. In an extract missing one protein, finished chains stay attached to tRNAs at stop codons
E. A human mRNA without a 5' cap isn't translated in a human cell extract

**Component**
1. An aminoacyl tRNA synthetase
2. The release factor
3. Initiation factors that assemble at the cap
4. The tRNA's anticodon
5. The peptidyl transferase center

**Correct matches:**
- A = A tRNA with a mutated anticodon inserts its amino acid at the wrong codons → The tRNA's anticodon
- B = A tRNA is loaded with the wrong amino acid but still pairs with the correct codon → An aminoacyl tRNA synthetase
- C = An antibiotic binds rRNA in the large subunit and blocks peptide bond formation → The peptidyl transferase center
- D = In an extract missing one protein, finished chains stay attached to tRNAs at stop codons → The release factor
- E = A human mRNA without a 5' cap isn't translated in a human cell extract → Initiation factors that assemble at the cap

Each component has one job: the anticodon reads codons; synthetases load the right amino acid; the rRNA peptidyl transferase center joins amino acids; release factors free the finished chain at a stop codon; eukaryotic initiation factors bring the small subunit to the 5' cap.

*Concepts linked:* Components of translation

**Q188. [True / False · 2점]**

> In the diagram, the middle base of the DNA template strand (G) is mutated to A. (GCA = Ala, GUA = Val, GAA = Glu)

**◇ Diagram — One codon traced from gene to tRNA.** Left: a tRNA cloverleaf, one strand folded into base-paired stems, with the acceptor stem at the top, where one strand ends at 5′ and the other continues past the stem as unpaired C, C, A to the 3′ end, labelled amino acid attachment site; below are a left loop, a right loop and a bottom loop labelled anticodon loop, in which three empty boxes are labelled anticodon. Right: four aligned rows of three bases in boxes: DNA nontemplate strand 5′ G C A 3′; DNA template strand 3′ C G T 5′; mRNA codon 5′ G C A 3′; tRNA anticodon 3′ C G U 5′. An arrow labelled transcription runs from the template-strand row to the mRNA row, and an arrow labelled base pairing runs from the mRNA row to the anticodon row.

**Claim:** The mRNA codon becomes GUA, so the protein carries valine in place of alanine.

**Answer: True**

The template's middle base pairs with the codon's middle base. Template G → A means the mRNA gets U instead of C: GCA becomes GUA, which codes for Val. One base change in the gene, one amino acid change in the protein.

*Concepts linked:* Template strand → codon → amino acid
