**Q189. [True / False · 1점]**

> Using the genetic code table, count the codons for each amino acid.

**◇ Diagram — The standard genetic code (mRNA codons, read 5′→3′).** A 4 × 4 table of the 64 mRNA codons: row blocks by 1st base (U, C, A, G, down the left), columns by 2nd base (U, C, A, G, across the top), and the 3rd base (U, C, A, G) listed down the right of every row block; each codon is followed by a three-letter amino-acid abbreviation or Stop, the three Stop entries are tinted, and AUG Met carries a small start tag. Entries by row block (2nd base U, C, A, G in turn): 1st base U: UUU Phe, UUC Phe, UUA Leu, UUG Leu, UCU Ser, UCC Ser, UCA Ser, UCG Ser, UAU Tyr, UAC Tyr, UAA Stop, UAG Stop, UGU Cys, UGC Cys, UGA Stop, UGG Trp; 1st base C: CUU Leu, CUC Leu, CUA Leu, CUG Leu, CCU Pro, CCC Pro, CCA Pro, CCG Pro, CAU His, CAC His, CAA Gln, CAG Gln, CGU Arg, CGC Arg, CGA Arg, CGG Arg; 1st base A: AUU Ile, AUC Ile, AUA Ile, AUG Met, ACU Thr, ACC Thr, ACA Thr, ACG Thr, AAU Asn, AAC Asn, AAA Lys, AAG Lys, AGU Ser, AGC Ser, AGA Arg, AGG Arg; 1st base G: GUU Val, GUC Val, GUA Val, GUG Val, GCU Ala, GCC Ala, GCA Ala, GCG Ala, GAU Asp, GAC Asp, GAA Glu, GAG Glu, GGU Gly, GGC Gly, GGA Gly, GGG Gly.

**Claim:** Every amino acid is encoded by at least two codons, which is what makes the code redundant.

**Answer: False**

Methionine (AUG) and tryptophan (UGG) each have only one codon. The code is still redundant because most amino acids have two to six codons: 61 coding codons for 20 amino acids.

*Concepts linked:* Redundancy of the genetic code · AUG start codon

---

**Q190. [True / False · 2점]**

> In a classic experiment, the cysteine already attached to a charged tRNA-Cys was chemically converted to alanine, leaving the tRNA's anticodon unchanged. The tRNA was then added to a protein-making system.

**Claim:** Alanine was inserted wherever the mRNA had a cysteine codon, because the ribosome checks only codon–anticodon pairing, not which amino acid the tRNA carries.

**Answer: True**

tRNAs are adapters: the anticodon reads the codon, and the amino acid simply rides along. That's why the accuracy of aminoacyl tRNA synthetases, which load each tRNA with the right amino acid, matters so much.

*Concepts linked:* tRNAs as adapters · Accuracy depends on tRNA synthetases

---

**Q191. [True / False · 2점]**

> A cell runs short of free leucine, so many tRNAs whose anticodons read leucine codons stay uncharged.

**Claim:** Ribosomes can skip the leucine codons and keep going in frame, making shorter proteins that lack leucine.

**Answer: False**

An uncharged tRNA has no amino acid to deliver. A ribosome that reaches a leucine codon waits, with that codon in its A site, until a charged tRNA arrives; it doesn't skip codons.

*Concepts linked:* Charged vs uncharged tRNA · The A site

---

**Q192. [True / False · 2점]**

> Suppose codons overlapped, so that bases 1-2-3, then 2-3-4, then 3-4-5 were each read as a codon.

**Claim:** A single base change could then alter up to three neighbouring amino acids, whereas in the real code it alters at most one.

**Answer: True**

In an overlapping code each base belongs to three codons. Real codons are a nonoverlapping series of triplets, so each base sits in exactly one codon, and a base change can affect only that codon's amino acid.

*Concepts linked:* Nonoverlapping triplets

---

**Q193. [True / False · 2점]**

> Translation is the last step in the flow of information from DNA to protein.

**Claim:** Given a protein's amino acid sequence, you could work out the exact mRNA sequence that coded for it.

**Answer: False**

The code is redundant: most amino acids have two to six codons, so one protein sequence fits many possible mRNAs. Information can be read from codon to amino acid, but not reconstructed exactly the other way.

*Concepts linked:* Redundancy of the code · Central dogma

---

**Q194. [True / False · 1점]**

> A student is asked for the anticodon that pairs with the mRNA codon 5'-AUG-3'.

**Claim:** Pairing base by base gives UAC, so written 5'→3' the anticodon is 5'-UAC-3'.

**Answer: False**

Pairing is antiparallel: the codon's first (5') base pairs with the anticodon's last (3') base. The anticodon is 3'-UAC-5', which written 5'→3' is 5'-CAU-3'.

*Concepts linked:* Codon–anticodon pairing is antiparallel

---

**Q195. [Multiple Choice · 1점]**

Using the genetic code table, which single-base change in the mRNA codon CUU would NOT change the amino acid?

**◇ Diagram — The standard genetic code (mRNA codons, read 5′→3′).** A 4 × 4 table of the 64 mRNA codons: row blocks by 1st base (U, C, A, G, down the left), columns by 2nd base (U, C, A, G, across the top), and the 3rd base (U, C, A, G) listed down the right of every row block; each codon is followed by a three-letter amino-acid abbreviation or Stop, the three Stop entries are tinted, and AUG Met carries a small start tag. Entries by row block (2nd base U, C, A, G in turn): 1st base U: UUU Phe, UUC Phe, UUA Leu, UUG Leu, UCU Ser, UCC Ser, UCA Ser, UCG Ser, UAU Tyr, UAC Tyr, UAA Stop, UAG Stop, UGU Cys, UGC Cys, UGA Stop, UGG Trp; 1st base C: CUU Leu, CUC Leu, CUA Leu, CUG Leu, CCU Pro, CCC Pro, CCA Pro, CCG Pro, CAU His, CAC His, CAA Gln, CAG Gln, CGU Arg, CGC Arg, CGA Arg, CGG Arg; 1st base A: AUU Ile, AUC Ile, AUA Ile, AUG Met, ACU Thr, ACC Thr, ACA Thr, ACG Thr, AAU Asn, AAC Asn, AAA Lys, AAG Lys, AGU Ser, AGC Ser, AGA Arg, AGG Arg; 1st base G: GUU Val, GUC Val, GUA Val, GUG Val, GCU Ala, GCC Ala, GCA Ala, GCG Ala, GAU Asp, GAC Asp, GAA Glu, GAG Glu, GGU Gly, GGC Gly, GGA Gly, GGG Gly.

① CUU → CCU　 ② CUU → CUC　 ③ CUU → AUU　 ④ CUU → CAU　 ⑤ CUU → UUU

**Answer: ② CUU → CUC**

CUU and CUC both code for Leu. The others give Pro (CCU), Ile (AUU), His (CAU) and Phe (UUU). Third-position changes are the ones most often silent, because the code is redundant: many amino acids have codons that differ only in their third base.

*Concepts linked:* Redundancy of the genetic code · Silent changes

---

**Q196. [Multiple Choice · 2점]**

Using the genetic code table, a ribosome starts at the first AUG of the mRNA 5'-CCAUGGUUCAUUGAGC-3'. What polypeptide is made?

**◇ Diagram — The standard genetic code (mRNA codons, read 5′→3′).** A 4 × 4 table of the 64 mRNA codons: row blocks by 1st base (U, C, A, G, down the left), columns by 2nd base (U, C, A, G, across the top), and the 3rd base (U, C, A, G) listed down the right of every row block; each codon is followed by a three-letter amino-acid abbreviation or Stop, the three Stop entries are tinted, and AUG Met carries a small start tag. Entries by row block (2nd base U, C, A, G in turn): 1st base U: UUU Phe, UUC Phe, UUA Leu, UUG Leu, UCU Ser, UCC Ser, UCA Ser, UCG Ser, UAU Tyr, UAC Tyr, UAA Stop, UAG Stop, UGU Cys, UGC Cys, UGA Stop, UGG Trp; 1st base C: CUU Leu, CUC Leu, CUA Leu, CUG Leu, CCU Pro, CCC Pro, CCA Pro, CCG Pro, CAU His, CAC His, CAA Gln, CAG Gln, CGU Arg, CGC Arg, CGA Arg, CGG Arg; 1st base A: AUU Ile, AUC Ile, AUA Ile, AUG Met, ACU Thr, ACC Thr, ACA Thr, ACG Thr, AAU Asn, AAC Asn, AAA Lys, AAG Lys, AGU Ser, AGC Ser, AGA Arg, AGG Arg; 1st base G: GUU Val, GUC Val, GUA Val, GUG Val, GCU Ala, GCC Ala, GCA Ala, GCG Ala, GAU Asp, GAC Asp, GAA Glu, GAG Glu, GGU Gly, GGC Gly, GGA Gly, GGG Gly.

① Met–Val–His–Trp (4 amino acids)　 ② Pro–Trp–Phe–Ile–Glu (5 amino acids)　 ③ Met–Val (2 amino acids)　 ④ Met–Val–His (3 amino acids)　 ⑤ Met–Val–His–Stop (4 amino acids)

**Answer: ④ Met–Val–His (3 amino acids)**

Starting at AUG sets the reading frame: AUG (Met) GUU (Val) CAU (His) UGA (stop). A stop codon has no amino acid (and UGA is not UGG, Trp), so the chain is 3 long. Reading from the first base would give a different frame (CCA UGG UUC…) and a different protein.

*Concepts linked:* Start codon sets the reading frame · Stop codons

---

**Q197. [Multiple Choice · 2점]**

Which mutation in the middle of a gene's coding sequence is least likely to destroy the protein's function?

① Deleting a single nucleotide from the middle of a codon　 ② Inserting two nucleotides between two codons　 ③ Inserting one nucleotide just after the start codon　 ④ Deleting the three nucleotides of one whole codon　 ⑤ Changing one codon in the middle to UAA

**Answer: ④ Deleting the three nucleotides of one whole codon**

Codons are read three at a time from the start codon. Removing one whole codon deletes one amino acid but keeps the reading frame, so everything downstream is unchanged. Adding or removing 1 or 2 nucleotides shifts the frame and garbles every later codon, and UAA ends the chain early.

*Concepts linked:* Reading frame · Nonoverlapping triplets · Stop codons

---

**Q198. [Multiple Choice · 2점]**

In the diagram, which way is the ribosome moving along the mRNA, and how can you tell?

**◇ Diagram — A ribosome partway along an mRNA.** A ribosome, its large subunit on top and its small subunit below, sits on an mRNA that runs between them from 5′ on the left to 3′ on the right, written in codons as 5′ … ACC AUG GCU UGG AAA UAG GCA … 3′. Inside the large subunit are three slots labelled E, P and A, from left to right, directly over the codons AUG, GCU and UGG; ACC lies to the left of the ribosome and AAA, UAG and GCA to its right. The E slot holds a tRNA with anticodon 3′-UAC-5′ paired to AUG and nothing attached; the P slot holds a tRNA with anticodon 3′-CGA-5′ paired to GCU, carrying a two-bead chain whose bead Ala is bonded to the tRNA and whose bead Met is the free end, reaching up out of the large subunit; the A slot is empty above UGG. Outside the ribosome, upper right, is a tRNA carrying one bead labelled ? with anticodon ? ? ?..

① Toward 5': tRNA anticodons are written 3'→5'　 ② Toward 5': Met at the chain's free end was the last amino acid added　 ③ Toward 3': the large subunit always sits on the mRNA's 3' side　 ④ It stays put while the mRNA slides back and forth through it　 ⑤ Toward 3': the start codon AUG has already moved into the E site

**Answer: ⑤ Toward 3': the start codon AUG has already moved into the E site**

Ribosomes read mRNA 5'→3'. The start codon AUG — read first — is already in the E site, and the unread codons lie on the 3' side. Met at the chain's free end was the first amino acid added, not the last, which also shows the ribosome began at the 5' end.

*Concepts linked:* Ribosomes move 5'→3' · A, P and E sites · The N-terminus is made first

---

**Q199. [Multiple Choice · 1점]**

The standard genetic code has 64 codons. How many of them are read by tRNAs carrying an amino acid?

① 64　 ② 61　 ③ 20　 ④ 60　 ⑤ 63

**Answer: ② 61**

Three codons (UAA, UAG, UGA) are stop codons: no tRNA reads them, and a release factor binds instead. That leaves 61 codons for 20 amino acids, so most amino acids have several codons.

*Concepts linked:* Stop codons · A redundant code

---

**Q200. [Multiple Choice · 1점]**

Which RNAs are made by transcription but never translated into protein?

① mRNA and tRNA　 ② mRNA only　 ③ tRNA and rRNA　 ④ rRNA and mRNA　 ⑤ tRNA only

**Answer: ③ tRNA and rRNA**

Only mRNA carries a code that ribosomes translate. tRNA and rRNA are noncoding RNAs: they're transcribed from DNA (rRNA in the nucleolus) and work as RNA — tRNA as the adapter, rRNA as the ribosome's structural and catalytic core.

*Concepts linked:* Central dogma · Noncoding RNAs · Ribosomes are RNA + protein

---

### Translation Stages: Prok vs Euk

**Q201. [보기 Combination · 3점] Where can a ribosome start?**

Using the diagram, which statements in <보기> are correct?

**◇ Diagram — Two mRNAs: where can a ribosome start?.** Two mRNAs, each drawn 5′ to 3′ from left to right. mRNA 1: a cap at the 5′ end, a short stretch, an AUG box, a long coding bar that contains a second, smaller AUG mark partway along it, a UAA box, a short stretch and a tail AAAA…A. mRNA 2: no cap; three coding regions in a row, each preceded by a small box labelled AGGAGGU and then an AUG box, followed by a coding bar and a box reading UAA, UGA and UAG respectively, with short stretches between the regions..

> **(가)** Each coding bar is read from its AUG to the stop codon that follows.
>
> **(나)** The inner AUG of mRNA 1 lies in the same reading frame as its first AUG.

**<보기>**
- ㄱ. mRNA 2 can yield three separate polypeptides, because a ribosome can begin at each AGGAGGU sequence.
- ㄴ. On mRNA 1, translation starts at the first AUG after the cap, and the inner AUG is read as methionine within the same polypeptide.
- ㄷ. mRNA 1 comes from a bacterium, since it ends in a tail of A's.

① ㄱ　 ② ㄴ　 ③ ㄱ, ㄴ　 ④ ㄴ, ㄷ　 ⑤ ㄱ, ㄴ, ㄷ

**Answer: ③ ㄱ, ㄴ**
- ㄱ — **O** — Each coding region has its own Shine-Dalgarno sequence (AGGAGGU) upstream of its AUG, so one bacterial mRNA can code for several polypeptides.
- ㄴ — **O** — The initiation complex assembles at the 5' cap and scans to the first AUG; a later AUG in the same frame simply codes for Met inside the chain.
- ㄷ — **X** — A 5' cap and a poly(A) tail are eukaryotic processing marks (Module 4). Bacterial mRNAs have no cap; mRNA 2's Shine-Dalgarno sequences mark it as bacterial.

*Concepts linked:* Cap and scanning vs Shine-Dalgarno · Polycistronic mRNA

---

**Q202. [보기 Combination · 3점] Endgame for this mRNA**

Using the diagram, which statements in <보기> are correct?

**◇ Diagram — A ribosome partway along an mRNA.** A ribosome, its large subunit on top and its small subunit below, sits on an mRNA that runs between them from 5′ on the left to 3′ on the right, written in codons as 5′ … ACC AUG GCU UGG AAA UAG GCA … 3′. Inside the large subunit are three slots labelled E, P and A, from left to right, directly over the codons AUG, GCU and UGG; ACC lies to the left of the ribosome and AAA, UAG and GCA to its right. The E slot holds a tRNA with anticodon 3′-UAC-5′ paired to AUG and nothing attached; the P slot holds a tRNA with anticodon 3′-CGA-5′ paired to GCU, carrying a two-bead chain whose bead Ala is bonded to the tRNA and whose bead Met is the free end, reaching up out of the large subunit; the A slot is empty above UGG. Outside the ribosome, upper right, is a tRNA carrying one bead labelled ? with anticodon ? ? ?..

> **(가)** The ribosome continues along the mRNA until it reaches UAG.
>
> **(나)** No tRNA has an anticodon that pairs with UAG.

**<보기>**
- ㄱ. When UAG reaches the A site, a release factor protein binds there instead of a tRNA.
- ㄴ. The chain is freed by breaking the peptide bond between its last two amino acids.
- ㄷ. The ribosome will translate GCA, the codon after UAG, as alanine before it lets go.

① ㄱ　 ② ㄷ　 ③ ㄱ, ㄷ　 ④ ㄴ, ㄷ　 ⑤ ㄱ, ㄴ, ㄷ

**Answer: ① ㄱ**
- ㄱ — **O** — Stop codons are recognized by a protein release factor, not by a tRNA.
- ㄴ — **X** — Release breaks the bond between the finished chain and the tRNA holding it. All peptide bonds stay intact, and the last amino acid's carboxyl group becomes the C-terminus.
- ㄷ — **X** — Translation ends at the stop codon; codons beyond it, such as GCA, aren't read.

*Concepts linked:* Termination: release factor · Stop codons · C-terminus

---

**Q203. [Matching · 3점] Where does it happen?**

Match each event to where it happens.

**Event**
A. A charged tRNA first pairs with its codon
B. The tRNA holding the growing chain waits for the next amino acid
C. An uncharged tRNA leaves the ribosome
D. A eukaryotic ribosome first assembles on an mRNA
E. A bacterial ribosome first binds an mRNA

**Where**
1. The 5' cap
2. The P site
3. The A site
4. The E site
5. AGGAGGU, upstream of an AUG

**Correct matches:**
- A = A charged tRNA first pairs with its codon → The A site
- B = The tRNA holding the growing chain waits for the next amino acid → The P site
- C = An uncharged tRNA leaves the ribosome → The E site
- D = A eukaryotic ribosome first assembles on an mRNA → The 5' cap
- E = A bacterial ribosome first binds an mRNA → AGGAGGU, upstream of an AUG

A = aminoacyl-tRNA entry, P = peptidyl-tRNA, E = exit. Eukaryotic initiation begins at the 5' cap and scans to AUG; bacterial ribosomes bind Shine-Dalgarno sequences (AGGAGGU) just upstream of each AUG.

*Concepts linked:* A, P and E sites · Initiation in eukaryotes vs bacteria

---

**Q204. [True / False · 2점]**

> A researcher deletes only the first AGGAGGU sequence of mRNA 2 in the diagram.

**◇ Diagram — Two mRNAs: where can a ribosome start?.** Two mRNAs, each drawn 5′ to 3′ from left to right. mRNA 1: a cap at the 5′ end, a short stretch, an AUG box, a long coding bar that contains a second, smaller AUG mark partway along it, a UAA box, a short stretch and a tail AAAA…A. mRNA 2: no cap; three coding regions in a row, each preceded by a small box labelled AGGAGGU and then an AUG box, followed by a coding bar and a box reading UAA, UGA and UAG respectively, with short stretches between the regions..

**Claim:** The first polypeptide stops being made, while the second and third are still translated.

**Answer: True**

In bacteria, each coding region's own Shine-Dalgarno sequence recruits a ribosome, which starts at the next AUG. Removing the first one blocks only the first region; the other two keep their binding sites.

*Concepts linked:* Shine-Dalgarno sequence · Polycistronic mRNA

---

**Q205. [True / False · 2점]**

> mRNA 1 in the diagram is added to a bacterial extract containing bacterial ribosomes and translation factors, but no eukaryotic initiation factors.

**◇ Diagram — Two mRNAs: where can a ribosome start?.** Two mRNAs, each drawn 5′ to 3′ from left to right. mRNA 1: a cap at the 5′ end, a short stretch, an AUG box, a long coding bar that contains a second, smaller AUG mark partway along it, a UAA box, a short stretch and a tail AAAA…A. mRNA 2: no cap; three coding regions in a row, each preceded by a small box labelled AGGAGGU and then an AUG box, followed by a coding bar and a box reading UAA, UGA and UAG respectively, with short stretches between the regions..

**Claim:** The bacterial ribosomes can bind the cap and start translating at mRNA 1's first AUG.

**Answer: False**

Bacterial ribosomes find start sites by pairing with Shine-Dalgarno sequences, not caps, and mRNA 1 has none. That's why genes moved into bacteria are given a bacterial ribosome-binding sequence upstream of the AUG.

*Concepts linked:* Shine-Dalgarno vs cap-dependent initiation

---

**Q206. [True / False · 2점]**

> In the diagram, Met–Ala is attached to the tRNA in the P site, and a charged tRNA now enters the A site.

**◇ Diagram — A ribosome partway along an mRNA.** A ribosome, its large subunit on top and its small subunit below, sits on an mRNA that runs between them from 5′ on the left to 3′ on the right, written in codons as 5′ … ACC AUG GCU UGG AAA UAG GCA … 3′. Inside the large subunit are three slots labelled E, P and A, from left to right, directly over the codons AUG, GCU and UGG; ACC lies to the left of the ribosome and AAA, UAG and GCA to its right. The E slot holds a tRNA with anticodon 3′-UAC-5′ paired to AUG and nothing attached; the P slot holds a tRNA with anticodon 3′-CGA-5′ paired to GCU, carrying a two-bead chain whose bead Ala is bonded to the tRNA and whose bead Met is the free end, reaching up out of the large subunit; the A slot is empty above UGG. Outside the ribosome, upper right, is a tRNA carrying one bead labelled ? with anticodon ? ? ?..

**Claim:** When the next peptide bond forms, the new amino acid is added onto the chain, which stays attached to the P-site tRNA.

**Answer: False**

The reverse: the whole chain is transferred from the P-site tRNA onto the amino acid carried by the A-site tRNA. The chain (now Met–Ala–Trp) hangs from the A-site tRNA until the ribosome shifts one codon and moves that tRNA into the P site.

*Concepts linked:* Elongation: transfer to the A-site tRNA · Translocation

---

**Q207. [True / False · 1점]**

> Translation initiation differs between bacteria and eukaryotes.

**Claim:** Elongation and termination differ just as much, since bacterial ribosomes lack A, P and E sites.

**Answer: False**

The differences are in initiation (cap and scanning vs Shine-Dalgarno sequences). Both kinds of ribosome have A, P and E sites, and elongation and termination work the same way.

*Concepts linked:* Elongation and termination are shared

---

**Q208. [True / False · 1점]**

> Electron micrographs often show several ribosomes spaced along one mRNA at the same time.

**Claim:** Each ribosome makes only part of the polypeptide, and the pieces are joined together afterward.

**Answer: False**

Each ribosome starts at the start codon and runs to the stop codon, making its own complete chain. Several ribosomes on one mRNA simply make several copies at once.

*Concepts linked:* Many ribosomes per mRNA · Ribosomes read 5'→3'

---

**Q209. [True / False · 2점]**

> A mutation creates an extra AUG in the 5' untranslated region of a human mRNA, between the cap and the normal start codon, in a different reading frame.

**Claim:** Scanning ribosomes can start at this new AUG instead, making a short, wrong polypeptide and less of the normal protein.

**Answer: True**

Eukaryotic ribosomes start at the first AUG they reach while scanning from the cap. An upstream AUG in another frame diverts them into the wrong reading frame — a known cause of some inherited diseases.

*Concepts linked:* Scanning from the cap · Reading frame

---

**Q210. [True / False · 2점]**

> A drug blocks only one step of translation: the large ribosomal subunit joining the small subunit at the start codon.

**Claim:** Ribosomes already partway along an mRNA when the drug is added will finish their proteins, but no new proteins will be started.

**Answer: True**

Joining of the large subunit is part of initiation. Ribosomes already in elongation don't need that step again, so they run on to the stop codon; new rounds of initiation are blocked.

*Concepts linked:* Three stages of translation

---

**Q211. [Multiple Choice · 2점]**

Put these events of eukaryotic translation in order: (1) the large subunit joins; (2) the small subunit, initiation factors and Met-tRNA bind at the 5' cap; (3) the complex scans to the first AUG; (4) the first peptide bond forms.

① 2 → 3 → 1 → 4　 ② 3 → 2 → 1 → 4　 ③ 2 → 1 → 3 → 4　 ④ 1 → 2 → 3 → 4　 ⑤ 2 → 3 → 4 → 1

**Answer: ① 2 → 3 → 1 → 4**

The small subunit with Met-tRNA and initiation factors loads at the cap and scans; only when it reaches AUG does the large subunit join (and the factors leave). Peptide bonds form in the large subunit, so the first one comes last.

*Concepts linked:* Eukaryotic initiation · Stages of translation

---

**Q212. [Multiple Choice · 2점]**

In the diagram, a tRNA enters the A site, a peptide bond forms, and the ribosome then shifts one codon. Which codons now sit in the E, P and A sites?

**◇ Diagram — A ribosome partway along an mRNA.** A ribosome, its large subunit on top and its small subunit below, sits on an mRNA that runs between them from 5′ on the left to 3′ on the right, written in codons as 5′ … ACC AUG GCU UGG AAA UAG GCA … 3′. Inside the large subunit are three slots labelled E, P and A, from left to right, directly over the codons AUG, GCU and UGG; ACC lies to the left of the ribosome and AAA, UAG and GCA to its right. The E slot holds a tRNA with anticodon 3′-UAC-5′ paired to AUG and nothing attached; the P slot holds a tRNA with anticodon 3′-CGA-5′ paired to GCU, carrying a two-bead chain whose bead Ala is bonded to the tRNA and whose bead Met is the free end, reaching up out of the large subunit; the A slot is empty above UGG. Outside the ribosome, upper right, is a tRNA carrying one bead labelled ? with anticodon ? ? ?..

① E: AUG, P: GCU, A: UGG　 ② E: GCU, P: UGG, A: AAA　 ③ E: UGG, P: AAA, A: UAG　 ④ E: ACC, P: AUG, A: GCU　 ⑤ E: GCU, P: UGG, A: UAG

**Answer: ② E: GCU, P: UGG, A: AAA**

Translocation moves the ribosome one codon toward the 3' end: the tRNA on UGG (now holding the chain) moves to P, the empty tRNA on GCU moves to E, and the next codon, AAA, enters the A site.

*Concepts linked:* Translocation by one codon · Ribosomes move 5'→3'

---

**Q213. [Multiple Choice · 2점]**

A mutation changes codon 40 of a gene's 300 codons (counting AUG as codon 1) into UAG. What does the cell make from this mRNA?

① A full-length chain lacking only its 40th amino acid　 ② A 40-amino-acid chain ending in a special stop amino acid　 ③ No chain at all, since translation can't begin　 ④ A 39-amino-acid chain, freed when a release factor binds UAG　 ⑤ A full-length chain, since a tRNA reads UAG as an amino acid

**Answer: ④ A 39-amino-acid chain, freed when a release factor binds UAG**

Codons 1–39 are translated normally. UAG in the A site is recognized by a release factor (no tRNA reads it), so a shortened 39-amino-acid chain is released — usually nonfunctional.

*Concepts linked:* Stop codons · Termination by release factor

### Regulation & Protein Sorting

**Q214. [보기 Combination · 3점] Four snapshots at the ER**

Using the diagram, which statements in <보기> are correct?

**◇ Diagram — Four snapshots at the rough ER membrane.** Four numbered panels, 1 to 4, joined by arrows, each a stretch of rough ER membrane with the cytosol above and the ER lumen below, and in every panel the membrane holds an SRP receptor and, beside it, a channel. In panel 1 a ribosome on an mRNA sits well above the membrane, with a short polypeptide coming out of it whose tip, the signal sequence, is held by an SRP particle that also touches the ribosome, and the channel is closed; in panel 2 the same ribosome, SRP and short polypeptide sit with the SRP on the SRP receptor and the ribosome beside the closed channel. In panel 3 the ribosome sits directly on the open channel, its polypeptide runs through the channel into the lumen with the signal sequence at the far end, and the SRP is by itself in the cytosol. In panel 4 a folded polypeptide lies free in the lumen, a short signal-sequence piece lies by itself near the closed channel, and the ribosome&#x27;s two subunits lie apart from each other and from the mRNA in the cytosol..

> **(가)** Panels 1–4 follow one ribosome making one protein.
>
> **(나)** In panel 1, the SRP is bound to the signal sequence as it emerges from the ribosome.

**<보기>**
- ㄱ. Translation pauses while the SRP is bound and resumes once the ribosome is handed to the channel.
- ㄴ. Because the protein in panel 4 is in the ER lumen, it can only ever stay inside the ER.
- ㄷ. The signal sequence lies at the protein's amino end, since it is the first part of the chain to emerge from the ribosome.

① ㄴ　 ② ㄱ, ㄴ　 ③ ㄱ, ㄷ　 ④ ㄴ, ㄷ　 ⑤ ㄱ, ㄴ, ㄷ

**Answer: ③ ㄱ, ㄷ**
- ㄱ — **O** — The SRP halts translation until the ribosome docks at the ER; when the SRP lets go, synthesis resumes and the chain is threaded through the channel.
- ㄴ — **X** — From the lumen, a protein may stay in the ER, travel in vesicles to another organelle's lumen (such as the Golgi or a lysosome), or be secreted (Module 3).
- ㄷ — **O** — Chains are made N-terminus first, so an amino-terminal signal is the first segment out — early enough to be caught before the protein is finished.

*Concepts linked:* SRP targeting · Fates of rough-ER proteins (Module 3)

---

**Q215. [보기 Combination · 3점] Which end faces out?**

Using the diagram, which statements in <보기> are correct?

**◇ Diagram — One membrane protein, two membranes.** Two panels joined by an arrow labelled vesicle traffic. Left panel, rough ER: a membrane with the cytosol above and the ER lumen below, crossed once by a protein chain whose NH₃⁺ end lies in the lumen and whose COO⁻ end lies in the cytosol; the straight stretch inside the membrane is highlighted and labelled signal-anchor sequence. Right panel, plasma membrane: a membrane with the extracellular fluid above and the cytosol below, crossed once by the same protein chain, with the same highlighted straight stretch inside the membrane; one end of the chain lies above the membrane and one below, and each end is labelled ?..

> **(가)** The protein's orientation in the ER membrane is set while it is being made.
>
> **(나)** It reaches the plasma membrane in vesicles that bud from one membrane and fuse with the next.

**<보기>**
- ㄱ. The signal-anchor segment stays in the membrane because its side chains are mostly charged and cling to the phosphate heads.
- ㄴ. Vesicle traffic turns the protein over, so the COO- end ends up outside the cell.
- ㄷ. In the right panel, the NH3+ end lies in the extracellular fluid.

① ㄱ　 ② ㄷ　 ③ ㄱ, ㄴ　 ④ ㄱ, ㄷ　 ⑤ ㄴ, ㄷ

**Answer: ② ㄷ**
- ㄱ — **X** — A segment spanning the membrane sits among the lipid tails, so its side chains are mostly nonpolar — the feature that marks integral membrane proteins (Module 3).
- ㄴ — **X** — Vesicles keep a protein's orientation: the COO- end, in the cytosol at the ER, stays in the cytosol at the plasma membrane.
- ㄷ — **O** — Budding and fusion never flip a membrane: the side facing the ER lumen stays inside the vesicles and faces outside the cell after fusion (Module 3). So the N-terminus, in the lumen, ends up outside.

*Concepts linked:* Signal-anchor sequence · The lumen side becomes the outside (Module 3)

---

**Q216. [보기 Combination · 3점] One gene, two cell types**

Which statements in <보기> are correct?

> **(가)** In muscle cells, the gene for enzyme Z is wrapped tightly with histones and is never transcribed.
>
> **(나)** In liver cells, the gene is transcribed and enzyme Z is made in an inactive form, switched on by phosphorylation when blood sugar drops.

**<보기>**
- ㄱ. In muscle, expression of Z is blocked at the level of DNA accessibility.
- ㄴ. In liver, phosphorylation switches Z on without changing its amino acid sequence.
- ㄷ. Muscle and liver cells can carry identical copies of the gene yet differ in whether they make active enzyme Z.

① ㄱ　 ② ㄴ　 ③ ㄱ, ㄷ　 ④ ㄴ, ㄷ　 ⑤ ㄱ, ㄴ, ㄷ

**Answer: ⑤ ㄱ, ㄴ, ㄷ**
- ㄱ — **O** — Tightly packed DNA can't be reached by the transcription machinery, so the gene stays silent.
- ㄴ — **O** — Adding a phosphate group is a post-translational modification: the sequence stays the same, but the shape and activity change.
- ㄷ — **O** — All body cells carry the same genes; regulation decides which are expressed, and when.

*Concepts linked:* Levels of gene regulation · Post-translational modification

---

**Q217. [Matching · 3점] Address labels**

Match each protein to the sorting signal it needs.

**Protein**
A. A glycolysis enzyme that works in the cytosol
B. A citric acid cycle enzyme in the mitochondrial matrix
C. A DNA polymerase that copies chromosomes
D. Insulin, a hormone secreted by pancreatic cells
E. A receptor that spans the plasma membrane

**Sorting signal**
1. An internal signal sequence
2. An SRP-recognized ER signal alone; the chain ends up free in the lumen
3. An ER signal plus a signal-anchor sequence that keeps it in the membrane
4. An amino-terminal signal for organelle import (made on free ribosomes)
5. No signal sequence

**Correct matches:**
- A = A glycolysis enzyme that works in the cytosol → No signal sequence
- B = A citric acid cycle enzyme in the mitochondrial matrix → An amino-terminal signal for organelle import (made on free ribosomes)
- C = A DNA polymerase that copies chromosomes → An internal signal sequence
- D = Insulin, a hormone secreted by pancreatic cells → An SRP-recognized ER signal alone; the chain ends up free in the lumen
- E = A receptor that spans the plasma membrane → An ER signal plus a signal-anchor sequence that keeps it in the membrane

Where a protein works decides its label: none for the cytosol; an amino-terminal signal for mitochondria or chloroplasts; an internal signal for the nucleus; an SRP-recognized amino-terminal signal for the ER and secretory route; plus a signal-anchor sequence to stay in a membrane.

*Concepts linked:* Signal sequences and destinations

---

**Q218. [True / False · 1점]**

> A secretory protein's gene is altered so that its ER signal sequence is deleted; nothing else changes.

**Claim:** The protein will be made on free ribosomes and stay in the cytosol, since the SRP has nothing to recognize.

**Answer: True**

Translation always begins in the cytosol. Without a signal, the SRP never binds and the ribosome never goes to the ER; a protein with no signal remains in the cytosol.

*Concepts linked:* No signal → cytosol · SRP needs the signal sequence

---

**Q219. [True / False · 1점]**

> Panel 4 of the diagram shows the signal sequence as a separate fragment near the channel.

**◇ Diagram — Four snapshots at the rough ER membrane.** Four numbered panels, 1 to 4, joined by arrows, each a stretch of rough ER membrane with the cytosol above and the ER lumen below, and in every panel the membrane holds an SRP receptor and, beside it, a channel. In panel 1 a ribosome on an mRNA sits well above the membrane, with a short polypeptide coming out of it whose tip, the signal sequence, is held by an SRP particle that also touches the ribosome, and the channel is closed; in panel 2 the same ribosome, SRP and short polypeptide sit with the SRP on the SRP receptor and the ribosome beside the closed channel. In panel 3 the ribosome sits directly on the open channel, its polypeptide runs through the channel into the lumen with the signal sequence at the far end, and the SRP is by itself in the cytosol. In panel 4 a folded polypeptide lies free in the lumen, a short signal-sequence piece lies by itself near the closed channel, and the ribosome&#x27;s two subunits lie apart from each other and from the mRNA in the cytosol..

**Claim:** So the mature protein in the lumen has lost amino acids from its carboxyl (C-terminal) end.

**Answer: False**

The signal sequence is the first part of the chain made, so it sits at the amino (N-terminal) end; cutting it off removes amino acids from the N-terminus. The mature protein is shorter than the chain the ribosome made.

*Concepts linked:* Signal sequence at the N-terminus · Cleavage in the ER

---

**Q220. [True / False · 2점]**

> Many plasma-membrane receptors carry sugar chains, which are attached in the lumen of the ER and Golgi.

**◇ Diagram — One membrane protein, two membranes.** Two panels joined by an arrow labelled vesicle traffic. Left panel, rough ER: a membrane with the cytosol above and the ER lumen below, crossed once by a protein chain whose NH₃⁺ end lies in the lumen and whose COO⁻ end lies in the cytosol; the straight stretch inside the membrane is highlighted and labelled signal-anchor sequence. Right panel, plasma membrane: a membrane with the extracellular fluid above and the cytosol below, crossed once by the same protein chain, with the same highlighted straight stretch inside the membrane; one end of the chain lies above the membrane and one below, and each end is labelled ?..

**Claim:** On the protein in the diagram, sugar chains attached in the ER lumen would end up on the NH3+ end's side of the membrane, outside the cell.

**Answer: True**

Sugars are added to the part of the protein facing the lumen — the NH3+ end's side. Through vesicle traffic that side becomes the outside face of the plasma membrane, which is why glycoprotein sugars face outward.

*Concepts linked:* Membrane protein orientation · Glycoproteins face outward (Module 3)

---

**Q221. [True / False · 1점]**

> A liver cell and a neuron from the same person carry the same genes but make very different sets of proteins.

**Claim:** This is possible because the two cell types can read their genes with different genetic codes.

**Answer: False**

Both use the same standard code. They differ in which genes are expressed and how much, controlled at several levels: DNA accessibility, transcription factors, RNA processing and post-translational modification.

*Concepts linked:* Levels of gene regulation · One genetic code

---

**Q222. [True / False · 1점]**

> In a human cell, an mRNA for a cytosolic protein is transcribed, processed, exported and translated.

**Claim:** Ribosomes translate it inside the nucleus, so the protein is finished before the mRNA passes through a nuclear pore.

**Answer: False**

Transcription and processing (cap, tail, splicing) happen in the nucleus; the mature mRNA leaves through nuclear pores, and translation happens on ribosomes in the cytosol.

*Concepts linked:* Overview of eukaryotic gene expression · Nuclear pores (Module 3)

---

**Q223. [Multiple Choice · 2점]**

In a mutant cell the SRP receptor is missing, but the SRP and the signal sequence are normal. What happens to a protein destined for secretion?

**◇ Diagram — Four snapshots at the rough ER membrane.** Four numbered panels, 1 to 4, joined by arrows, each a stretch of rough ER membrane with the cytosol above and the ER lumen below, and in every panel the membrane holds an SRP receptor and, beside it, a channel. In panel 1 a ribosome on an mRNA sits well above the membrane, with a short polypeptide coming out of it whose tip, the signal sequence, is held by an SRP particle that also touches the ribosome, and the channel is closed; in panel 2 the same ribosome, SRP and short polypeptide sit with the SRP on the SRP receptor and the ribosome beside the closed channel. In panel 3 the ribosome sits directly on the open channel, its polypeptide runs through the channel into the lumen with the signal sequence at the far end, and the SRP is by itself in the cytosol. In panel 4 a folded polypeptide lies free in the lumen, a short signal-sequence piece lies by itself near the closed channel, and the ribosome&#x27;s two subunits lie apart from each other and from the mRNA in the cytosol..

① SRP still binds and pauses synthesis, but the ribosome can't dock at the ER　 ② The SRP can't bind the signal sequence, so translation never pauses　 ③ The protein enters the ER normally, since the channel alone recognizes the signal　 ④ The protein enters the ER, but its signal sequence is never removed　 ⑤ The protein is redirected to the mitochondria by its signal sequence

**Answer: ① SRP still binds and pauses synthesis, but the ribosome can't dock at the ER**

The SRP recognizes the signal and halts translation, but docking requires the SRP receptor on the ER membrane. Without it, the ribosome is never handed to the channel, so the protein doesn't enter the ER or follow the secretory route.

*Concepts linked:* SRP and SRP receptor · Translation starts in the cytosol

**Q224. [Multiple Choice · 1점]**

Stomach cells secrete pepsin as an inactive precursor, pepsinogen, which becomes active only after part of its chain is cut off in the acidic stomach. Which level of gene-expression control does this illustrate?

① DNA accessibility　 ② Transcription factors　 ③ RNA processing　 ④ Translation initiation at the cap　 ⑤ Post-translational modification

**Answer: ⑤ Post-translational modification**

The protein is already made; activating it by cutting the chain is a post-translational modification. Making it inactive protects the cells that produce it from digesting their own proteins.

*Concepts linked:* Post-translational modification · Real-world: digestive enzymes

## Module 6 — Making Life Work

### Energy & Metabolism

**Q225. [보기 Combination · 3점] Who goes where?**

Using the diagram, which statements in <보기> are correct?

**◇ Diagram — Sorting organisms by energy source and carbon source.** A 2 by 2 grid. The two columns are headed energy from light and energy from chemical compounds; the two rows are headed carbon from CO₂ and carbon from organic molecules. Each cell holds only a letter: A (light, CO₂), B (chemical compounds, CO₂), C (light, organic molecules) and D (chemical compounds, organic molecules).

> **(가)** Cyanobacteria use light energy to build sugars from CO2.
>
> **(나)** Sulfur-oxidizing bacteria get energy by oxidizing hydrogen sulfide (H2S) and build sugars from CO2.

**<보기>**
- ㄱ. Both are phototrophs, since both make sugar from CO2.
- ㄴ. Cyanobacteria belong in cell A and sulfur-oxidizing bacteria in cell B.
- ㄷ. A human belongs in cell C, since humans get their carbon from organic molecules.

① ㄴ　 ② ㄷ　 ③ ㄱ, ㄴ　 ④ ㄱ, ㄷ　 ⑤ ㄴ, ㄷ

**Answer: ① ㄴ**
- ㄱ — **X** — Making sugar from CO2 makes them both autotrophs. 'Phototroph' describes the energy source, and only the cyanobacteria use light.
- ㄴ — **O** — Both take their carbon from CO2 (top row); cyanobacteria get energy from light (A), sulfur bacteria from a chemical compound (B).
- ㄷ — **X** — Humans do get carbon from organic molecules, but their energy comes from chemical compounds in food, not light — cell D.

*Concepts linked:* Phototroph vs chemotroph · Autotroph vs heterotroph

---

**Q226. [보기 Combination · 3점] Build it up, break it down**

Which statements in <보기> are correct?

> **(가)** After a meal, liver cells link glucose molecules into glycogen.
>
> **(나)** During a fast, the same cells break glycogen back down into glucose.

**<보기>**
- ㄱ. Since (가) and (나) are reverse processes, running them back to back (glycogen → glucose → glycogen) costs the cell no usable energy.
- ㄴ. (가) is anabolic and (나) is catabolic.
- ㄷ. (가) needs an input of energy, which cells supply by coupling it to ATP or a similar molecule.

① ㄱ　 ② ㄷ　 ③ ㄱ, ㄷ　 ④ ㄴ, ㄷ　 ⑤ ㄱ, ㄴ, ㄷ

**Answer: ④ ㄴ, ㄷ**
- ㄱ — **X** — Every conversion loses some usable energy as heat (second law), so cycling between them always costs energy.
- ㄴ — **O** — Building a polymer from smaller units is anabolism; breaking it into smaller units is catabolism.
- ㄷ — **O** — Anabolic reactions are endergonic. Linking sugars by condensation (Module 2) is driven by coupling it to the hydrolysis of phosphate bonds.

*Concepts linked:* Anabolism vs catabolism · Condensation and hydrolysis (Module 2) · Second law

---

**Q227. [Matching · 3점] Where is the energy?**

Match each example to the form of energy it best shows.

**Example**
A. Water held behind a dam
B. The bonds of a glucose molecule
C. A sperm cell's beating flagellum
D. Heat given off by a working muscle
E. A high concentration of Na+ outside a cell

**Form of energy**
1. Kinetic energy: movement of a whole structure
2. Kinetic energy: random motion of molecules
3. Potential energy stored in a gradient
4. Potential energy of position
5. Chemical energy stored in bonds

**Correct matches:**
- A = Water held behind a dam → Potential energy of position
- B = The bonds of a glucose molecule → Chemical energy stored in bonds
- C = A sperm cell's beating flagellum → Kinetic energy: movement of a whole structure
- D = Heat given off by a working muscle → Kinetic energy: random motion of molecules
- E = A high concentration of Na+ outside a cell → Potential energy stored in a gradient

Potential energy is stored — by position, in bonds, or in a concentration difference; kinetic energy is motion, whether of a whole flagellum or of molecules jiggling as heat. One form converts into another: the dam's water spins a turbine, and the Na+ gradient powers transport (Module 3).

*Concepts linked:* Potential vs kinetic energy · Chemical energy · Gradients (Module 3)

---

**Q228. [True / False · 1점]**

> Heliobacteria use light to make ATP but cannot fix CO2; they absorb organic molecules such as pyruvate as their carbon source.

**◇ Diagram — Sorting organisms by energy source and carbon source.** A 2 by 2 grid. The two columns are headed energy from light and energy from chemical compounds; the two rows are headed carbon from CO₂ and carbon from organic molecules. Each cell holds only a letter: A (light, CO₂), B (chemical compounds, CO₂), C (light, organic molecules) and D (chemical compounds, organic molecules).

**Claim:** Heliobacteria belong in cell C of the diagram.

**Answer: True**

Energy from light puts them in the left column; carbon from organic molecules puts them in the bottom row: cell C, the photoheterotrophs.

*Concepts linked:* Photoheterotrophs

---

**Q229. [True / False · 2점]**

> The notes link bond strength to potential energy: strong bonds hold less potential energy and weak bonds hold more.

**Claim:** So a reaction that replaces weaker bonds with stronger ones ends with products of lower potential energy, releasing energy.

**Answer: True**

Stronger bonds store less potential energy. If the products are held by stronger bonds than the reactants, the difference is released — the hallmark of an exergonic reaction.

*Concepts linked:* Bond strength and potential energy · Exergonic reactions

---

**Q230. [True / False · 1점]**

> A mature red blood cell has no nucleus or mitochondria and never moves on its own, yet it keeps using ATP.

**Claim:** It needs almost no ATP, since cellular work means movement and a red blood cell never moves by itself.

**Answer: False**

Cellular work also includes pumping ions across membranes and running chemical reactions. Red blood cells spend much of their ATP on ion pumps such as the Na+/K+ pump (Module 3), making it by glycolysis.

*Concepts linked:* Kinds of cellular work · ATP-driven pumps (Module 3)

---

**Q231. [Multiple Choice · 2점]**

Using the diagram, a deep-sea vent bacterium builds all its organic molecules from CO2 and gets its energy by oxidizing hydrogen gas (H2). A fungus rotting a log gets both its energy and its carbon from the wood. Which cells do they belong in?

**◇ Diagram — Sorting organisms by energy source and carbon source.** A 2 by 2 grid. The two columns are headed energy from light and energy from chemical compounds; the two rows are headed carbon from CO₂ and carbon from organic molecules. Each cell holds only a letter: A (light, CO₂), B (chemical compounds, CO₂), C (light, organic molecules) and D (chemical compounds, organic molecules).

① The bacterium in A; the fungus in D　 ② The bacterium in B; the fungus in C　 ③ The bacterium in B; the fungus in D　 ④ Both in D, since neither uses light　 ⑤ The bacterium in D; the fungus in B

**Answer: ③ The bacterium in B; the fungus in D**

The vent bacterium: chemical energy + carbon from CO2 → B (a chemoautotroph, like the notes' hydrogen bacteria). The fungus: chemical energy + organic carbon → D (a chemoheterotroph); fungi are heterotrophs (Module 1).

*Concepts linked:* Chemoautotrophs vs chemoheterotrophs · Fungi are heterotrophs (Module 1)

---

**Q232. [Multiple Choice · 2점]**

Using the diagram, a mutation inactivates enzyme e2. Which molecule builds up first, and what can happen to it?

**◇ Diagram — A branched metabolic pathway.** Flow diagram of a branched pathway: boxes A, B, C and D in a top row joined by arrows labelled e1 (A to B), e2 (B to C) and e3 (C to D); a second arrow labelled e4 leaves B and leads down to E, and an arrow labelled e5 leads from E to F in a lower row. D and F are each labelled end product.

① C builds up, since it can no longer be converted into D　 ② A builds up, since B can no longer be made from it　 ③ D builds up, since nothing downstream uses it　 ④ B builds up, and more of it may flow to E and F　 ⑤ Nothing builds up, since each enzyme works independently

**Answer: ④ B builds up, and more of it may flow to E and F**

In a pathway, each product is the next step's reactant. With e2 gone, B can't become C, so B accumulates (and C and D run low); e4 can still use B, so the excess may flow toward E and F. Inherited diseases such as PKU work this way: a missing enzyme makes its substrate pile up and be diverted into side products.

*Concepts linked:* Metabolic pathways · Real-world: inherited enzyme defects

---

### ATP & Energetic Coupling

**Q233. [보기 Combination · 3점] Reading the ATP diagram**

Using the diagram, which statements in <보기> are correct?

**◇ Diagram — ATP, piece by piece.** A molecule drawn left to right: three phosphate groups, each a circle marked P with a minus charge mark, joined in a row by bonds; a CH₂ group bonded to a five-membered ribose ring with O at its top vertex and OH on each of its two lower carbons; and adenine, a six-membered ring fused to a five-membered ring, bonded to the ribose. Italic tags mark three bonds: x between the CH₂ side and the nearest phosphate, y between the nearest and middle phosphates, and z between the middle and outermost phosphates. Below, four nested brackets numbered 1 to 4 all end at adenine&#x27;s right edge: 1 spans adenine and ribose, 2 adds the nearest phosphate, 3 adds the middle phosphate, and 4 spans all three phosphates.

> **(가)** Each numbered bracket encloses one named molecule.
>
> **(나)** x, y and z mark three bonds.

**<보기>**
- ㄱ. Bracket 3 encloses ADP, and bracket 2 encloses AMP.
- ㄴ. Hydrolyzing ATP to ADP + Pi breaks bond z.
- ㄷ. At physiological pH each phosphate group is negatively charged, so the three phosphates repel one another.

① ㄱ　 ② ㄷ　 ③ ㄱ, ㄴ　 ④ ㄱ, ㄷ　 ⑤ ㄱ, ㄴ, ㄷ

**Answer: ⑤ ㄱ, ㄴ, ㄷ**
- ㄱ — **O** — Adenine + ribose is adenosine (1); adding one phosphate gives AMP (2), two gives ADP (3), and three gives ATP (4).
- ㄴ — **O** — Removing the outermost phosphate breaks the bond between the second and third phosphates: z.
- ㄷ — **O** — That crowding of negative charges is one reason ATP holds so much potential energy and its hydrolysis is exergonic.

*Concepts linked:* ATP, ADP and AMP · ATP hydrolysis · Charge repulsion

---

**Q234. [보기 Combination · 3점] Pushing glucose uphill**

Which statements in <보기> are correct?

> **(가)** Glucose + Pi → glucose-6-phosphate + H2O, ΔG = +3.3 kcal/mol
>
> **(나)** ATP + H2O → ADP + Pi, ΔG = −7.3 kcal/mol

**<보기>**
- ㄱ. On its own, (가) is endergonic and does not proceed spontaneously.
- ㄴ. If the phosphate passes directly from ATP to glucose, the overall reaction glucose + ATP → glucose-6-phosphate + ADP has ΔG = −4.0 kcal/mol.
- ㄷ. Coupling works because the enzyme lowers (가)'s ΔG from +3.3 to below zero.

① ㄴ　 ② ㄷ　 ③ ㄱ, ㄴ　 ④ ㄴ, ㄷ　 ⑤ ㄱ, ㄴ, ㄷ

**Answer: ③ ㄱ, ㄴ**
- ㄱ — **O** — A positive ΔG means the products have more free energy than the reactants.
- ㄴ — **O** — ΔG values of coupled reactions add: +3.3 + (−7.3) = −4.0 kcal/mol. The shared phosphate links the two reactions.
- ㄷ — **X** — Enzymes never change ΔG. Coupling works by adding (가) to the more exergonic (나), so the combined ΔG is negative.

*Concepts linked:* Energetic coupling · Enzymes don't change ΔG

---

**Q235. [True / False · 2점]**

> Some reactions split ATP into AMP and pyrophosphate (two phosphates still joined together) instead of ADP + Pi.

**◇ Diagram — ATP, piece by piece.** A molecule drawn left to right: three phosphate groups, each a circle marked P with a minus charge mark, joined in a row by bonds; a CH₂ group bonded to a five-membered ribose ring with O at its top vertex and OH on each of its two lower carbons; and adenine, a six-membered ring fused to a five-membered ring, bonded to the ribose. Italic tags mark three bonds: x between the CH₂ side and the nearest phosphate, y between the nearest and middle phosphates, and z between the middle and outermost phosphates. Below, four nested brackets numbered 1 to 4 all end at adenine&#x27;s right edge: 1 spans adenine and ribose, 2 adds the nearest phosphate, 3 adds the middle phosphate, and 4 spans all three phosphates.

**Claim:** These reactions break bond y in the diagram rather than bond z.

**Answer: True**

AMP keeps only the phosphate nearest the ribose, so the break must come between the first and second phosphates — bond y — releasing the outer two phosphates together as pyrophosphate. RNA polymerase splits each incoming nucleotide the same way (Module 4).

*Concepts linked:* ADP vs AMP · Pyrophosphate release (Module 4)

---

**Q236. [True / False · 1점]**

> A sprinter's leg muscles hold only enough ATP for a few seconds of contraction, yet they can keep working far longer.

**Claim:** This works because muscle cells store most of their energy as a large reserve of ATP.

**Answer: False**

Cells keep only a small amount of ATP and recycle it constantly: catabolism (breaking down sugars and fats) uses the energy it releases to rebuild ATP from ADP + Pi as fast as it's spent.

*Concepts linked:* ATP is a currency, not long-term storage · Catabolism regenerates ATP

---

**Q237. [True / False · 1점]**

> A reaction has ΔG = +5.0 kcal/mol. A cell couples it to the hydrolysis of one ATP (ΔG = −7.3 kcal/mol), the two reactions sharing a phosphate intermediate.

**Claim:** The coupled reaction has ΔG = −2.3 kcal/mol, so it proceeds spontaneously.

**Answer: True**

ΔG values of coupled reactions add: +5.0 + (−7.3) = −2.3 kcal/mol. A negative net ΔG makes the combined process exergonic.

*Concepts linked:* Energetic coupling

---

**Q238. [True / False · 1점]**

> Cells regenerate ATP by the reaction ADP + Pi → ATP + H2O.

**Claim:** This reaction has ΔG = −7.3 kcal/mol, the same as ATP hydrolysis, so it runs on its own.

**Answer: False**

Reversing a reaction reverses the sign of ΔG: making ATP is +7.3 kcal/mol, endergonic. It happens only when coupled to energy released by catabolism, such as the breakdown of glucose.

*Concepts linked:* Reverse reactions flip the sign of ΔG · Catabolism regenerates ATP

---

**Q239. [Multiple Choice · 1점]**

According to the notes, which feature of ATP best explains why ATP + H2O → ADP + Pi releases energy at physiological pH?

① Water is a high-energy molecule that passes its energy on to ATP　 ② The bond between adenine and ribose holds most of ATP's energy　 ③ Its negative phosphates repel, so ADP + Pi has less potential energy　 ④ Its phosphates are uncharged and pack tightly, storing energy　 ⑤ The reaction is endergonic, so it takes in energy that the cell can then use

**Answer: ③ Its negative phosphates repel, so ADP + Pi has less potential energy**

At physiological pH the phosphates are negatively charged and resist one another; splitting one off relieves that strain, so the products (ADP + Pi) have lower potential energy and the reaction is exergonic (−7.3 kcal/mol).

*Concepts linked:* ATP hydrolysis is exergonic · Ionized phosphates (Module 2)

---

**Q240. [Multiple Choice · 2점]**

A biosynthetic step has ΔG = +10.0 kcal/mol. It can be coupled to the hydrolysis of ATP (−7.3 kcal/mol each). What is the smallest number of ATP that must be hydrolyzed to make the overall process spontaneous?

① 1 ATP, giving a net ΔG of −2.7 kcal/mol　 ② 2 ATP, giving a net ΔG of −4.6 kcal/mol　 ③ 1 ATP, since +2.7 kcal/mol is close enough　 ④ 3 ATP, giving a net ΔG of −11.9 kcal/mol　 ⑤ None; an enzyme can lower the ΔG instead

**Answer: ② 2 ATP, giving a net ΔG of −4.6 kcal/mol**

One ATP gives +10.0 − 7.3 = +2.7 kcal/mol, still positive. Two give +10.0 − 14.6 = −4.6 kcal/mol, which is negative, so the coupled process can proceed. Enzymes don't change ΔG.

*Concepts linked:* Energetic coupling arithmetic

---

**Q241. [Multiple Choice · 1점]**

Which process does NOT require ATP (or another nucleoside triphosphate) as its energy source?

① The Na+/K+ pump moving ions against their gradients　 ② Linking amino acids into a growing polypeptide chain on a ribosome　 ③ Moving vesicles along cytoskeleton tracks　 ④ Adding nucleotides to a growing RNA strand　 ⑤ Glucose diffusing through a carrier, down its gradient

**Answer: ⑤ Glucose diffusing through a carrier, down its gradient**

Facilitated diffusion is passive: glucose moves down its gradient, so no ATP is needed (Module 3). Pumping against a gradient, building proteins and RNA, and moving vesicles are all cellular work powered by ATP or other nucleoside triphosphates.

*Concepts linked:* Cellular work · Passive vs active transport (Module 3)

### Thermodynamics & Free Energy

**Q242. [보기 Combination · 3점] Reading two energy profiles**

Using the diagram, which statements in <보기> are correct?

**◇ Diagram — Free-energy profiles of reactions I and II.** Two free-energy plots side by side, panels I and II. In each, the y-axis is free energy (kJ/mol) labelled 0, 20, 40, 60, 80, 100 and 120 with small unlabelled ticks halfway between, the x-axis is course of reaction, and dashed lines run from each plateau and from the peak back to the y-axis. Panel I: a reactants plateau at 60 rises in one smooth hump to a peak at 110 and falls to a products plateau at 20. Panel II: a reactants plateau at 30 rises to a peak at 55 and falls to a products plateau at 50.

> **(가)** Read all values from the free-energy axes.
>
> **(나)** Both reactions run at the same temperature, with no enzyme.

**<보기>**
- ㄱ. Reaction I has ΔG = −40 kJ/mol, so it is spontaneous.
- ㄴ. Reaction II has the lower activation barrier, so it is the spontaneous one.
- ㄷ. The reverse of reaction II would have ΔG = −20 kJ/mol and an activation barrier of only 5 kJ/mol.

① ㄱ　 ② ㄷ　 ③ ㄱ, ㄴ　 ④ ㄱ, ㄷ　 ⑤ ㄴ, ㄷ

**Answer: ④ ㄱ, ㄷ**
- ㄱ — **O** — ΔG = G(products) − G(reactants) = 20 − 60 = −40 kJ/mol: exergonic and thermodynamically favourable.
- ㄴ — **X** — A low barrier makes a reaction fast, not favourable. Reaction II's products sit above its reactants (ΔG = 50 − 30 = +20 kJ/mol), so it is endergonic.
- ㄷ — **O** — Run backward, II starts at 50 and ends at 30 (−20 kJ/mol), and the climb from 50 to the peak at 55 is just 5 kJ/mol.

*Concepts linked:* ΔG from free-energy profiles · Spontaneous ≠ fast · Activation energy

---

**Q243. [Matching · 3점] Which way does the energy flow?**

Match each process to the description of its energetics.

**Process**
A. Building a protein from amino acids
B. Oxidizing glucose to CO2 and water
C. ATP + H2O → ADP + Pi
D. ADP + Pi → ATP + H2O
E. Glucose + ATP → glucose-6-phosphate + ADP

**Energetics**
1. Endergonic; builds a large molecule from smaller ones
2. Endergonic; powered by catabolism
3. Exergonic; breaks a fuel down into smaller molecules
4. Coupled reaction with a negative net ΔG
5. Exergonic; used to drive other reactions

**Correct matches:**
- A = Building a protein from amino acids → Endergonic; builds a large molecule from smaller ones
- B = Oxidizing glucose to CO2 and water → Exergonic; breaks a fuel down into smaller molecules
- C = ATP + H2O → ADP + Pi → Exergonic; used to drive other reactions
- D = ADP + Pi → ATP + H2O → Endergonic; powered by catabolism
- E = Glucose + ATP → glucose-6-phosphate + ADP → Coupled reaction with a negative net ΔG

Anabolic building needs energy (+ΔG); catabolic breakdown releases it (−ΔG). ATP hydrolysis is the exergonic half that cells couple to endergonic steps; ATP is rebuilt with energy from catabolism; and adding the coupled ΔG values gives a negative total.

*Concepts linked:* Exergonic vs endergonic · Anabolism vs catabolism · Coupling

---

**Q244. [True / False · 1점]**

> Reaction I in the diagram has a large, negative ΔG.

**◇ Diagram — Free-energy profiles of reactions I and II.** Two free-energy plots side by side, panels I and II. In each, the y-axis is free energy (kJ/mol) labelled 0, 20, 40, 60, 80, 100 and 120 with small unlabelled ticks halfway between, the x-axis is course of reaction, and dashed lines run from each plateau and from the peak back to the y-axis. Panel I: a reactants plateau at 60 rises in one smooth hump to a peak at 110 and falls to a products plateau at 20. Panel II: a reactants plateau at 30 rises to a peak at 55 and falls to a products plateau at 50.

**Claim:** So reaction I will proceed rapidly on its own as soon as the reactants are mixed.

**Answer: False**

ΔG tells whether a reaction can run on its own, not how fast. Reaction I must first climb a 50 kJ/mol activation barrier (60 → 110), which can make it very slow without a catalyst.

*Concepts linked:* Spontaneous ≠ fast · Activation energy

---

**Q245. [True / False · 2점]**

> A growing cell builds highly ordered proteins and membranes from simpler, more disordered molecules.

**Claim:** This doesn't violate the second law, because the cell releases heat and simple waste molecules that increase the entropy of its surroundings by more.

**Answer: True**

The second law applies to the cell plus its surroundings. Order inside is paid for with energy from food or light, much of it released as heat and as CO2 and water, so total entropy still rises.

*Concepts linked:* The second law and living systems · Entropy

---

**Q246. [True / False · 2점]**

> Dissolving the salt in an instant cold pack (ammonium nitrate) absorbs heat, with ΔH = +26 kJ/mol, but greatly increases disorder: TΔS = +33 kJ/mol at room temperature.

**Claim:** ΔG for dissolving is −7 kJ/mol, so it happens spontaneously even though it absorbs heat.

**Answer: True**

ΔG = ΔH − TΔS = +26 − 33 = −7 kJ/mol. A large enough increase in entropy can make a heat-absorbing process spontaneous.

*Concepts linked:* ΔG = ΔH − TΔS · Real-world: cold packs

---

**Q247. [True / False · 1점]**

> In the blood, CO2 + H2O ⇌ H2CO3 (carbonic acid). During hard exercise, working muscles release more CO2 into the blood.

**Claim:** The extra CO2 pushes this reaction back toward CO2 and water, raising the blood's pH.

**Answer: False**

Adding a reactant pushes a reversible reaction forward: more CO2 makes more H2CO3, which releases H+ (Module 2) and lowers the blood's pH slightly. The concentrations of reactants and products set the direction.

*Concepts linked:* Concentrations set reaction direction · Acids and pH (Module 2)

---

**Q248. [True / False · 2점]**

> The reaction A → B in a pathway has a positive ΔG when A and B are present at equal concentrations.

**Claim:** A cell can still keep A → B running forward by keeping A plentiful and quickly using up B in the next step.

**Answer: True**

The notes give two ways cells run +ΔG reactions. One is to keep the reactant-to-product ratio above its equilibrium value — here by supplying A and draining B. The other is coupling to an exergonic reaction.

*Concepts linked:* Running +ΔG reactions: concentrations · Metabolic pathways

---

**Q249. [True / False · 1점]**

> A gazelle absorbs energy from the grass it eats. That energy ends up as movement, body heat, and chemical bonds in its tissues.

**Claim:** Since much of it ends up as heat, the total energy in these forms is less than the energy absorbed; the rest was destroyed.

**Answer: False**

First law: energy is never destroyed. Movement + heat + stored chemical energy add up to everything absorbed. Heat is just the least useful form (second law), not missing energy.

*Concepts linked:* First law: conservation of energy

---

**Q250. [True / False · 1점]**

> Chlorophyll extracted from leaves glows red when lit in a test tube: its excited electrons drop back to lower energy levels with no chloroplast machinery to capture the energy.

**Claim:** The red glow is the energy given off as the electrons fall back, which shows the energy is converted rather than destroyed.

**Answer: True**

When electrons move from higher to lower energy levels, the energy is released as heat or light — converted, not lost (first law). Inside a chloroplast, that same energy would be captured to make ATP and sugar instead.

*Concepts linked:* Electrons dropping release energy · First law

---

**Q251. [True / False · 1점]**

> In glycolysis, one step has a positive ΔG under standard conditions, yet it runs forward inside cells.

**Claim:** This is possible because the enzyme for that step can make its ΔG negative.

**Answer: False**

Enzymes never change ΔG; they only lower the activation energy. The step runs forward because the next enzyme keeps using up its product, holding the product concentration low (other steps are pushed along by coupling to ATP).

*Concepts linked:* Running +ΔG reactions · Enzymes don't change ΔG

---

**Q252. [Multiple Choice · 2점]**

Using the diagram, what is reaction I's activation energy in the forward direction, and what would it be for the reverse reaction?

**◇ Diagram — Free-energy profiles of reactions I and II.** Two free-energy plots side by side, panels I and II. In each, the y-axis is free energy (kJ/mol) labelled 0, 20, 40, 60, 80, 100 and 120 with small unlabelled ticks halfway between, the x-axis is course of reaction, and dashed lines run from each plateau and from the peak back to the y-axis. Panel I: a reactants plateau at 60 rises in one smooth hump to a peak at 110 and falls to a products plateau at 20. Panel II: a reactants plateau at 30 rises to a peak at 55 and falls to a products plateau at 50.

① 50 kJ/mol forward; 90 kJ/mol reverse　 ② 110 kJ/mol forward; 90 kJ/mol reverse　 ③ 50 kJ/mol in both directions　 ④ 40 kJ/mol forward; 50 kJ/mol reverse　 ⑤ 90 kJ/mol forward; 50 kJ/mol reverse

**Answer: ① 50 kJ/mol forward; 90 kJ/mol reverse**

Activation energy is the climb from the starting level to the peak: forward 110 − 60 = 50 kJ/mol; in reverse, starting from the products, 110 − 20 = 90 kJ/mol. The reverse barrier is larger by exactly the size of ΔG (40 kJ/mol).

*Concepts linked:* Activation energy · Transition state

---

**Q253. [Multiple Choice · 1점]**

Muscle converts the chemical energy of glucose into movement with roughly 25% efficiency. What happens to the other 75%?

① It's destroyed during the conversion, exactly as the second law states　 ② It stays stored in the muscle as a reserve of extra ATP　 ③ It's converted back into glucose for later use　 ④ It leaves the muscle mostly in the form of light　 ⑤ It's released mostly as heat, raising entropy; none is destroyed

**Answer: ⑤ It's released mostly as heat, raising entropy; none is destroyed**

First law: energy is conserved, only converted. Second law: no conversion is 100% efficient, and the 'lost' energy is mostly heat that increases entropy — which is why exercise warms you and shivering keeps you warm.

*Concepts linked:* First and second laws · Real-world: body heat

---

**Q254. [Multiple Choice · 2점]**

A reaction has ΔH = −20 kJ/mol and makes things more ordered (ΔS < 0). Using ΔG = ΔH − TΔS, what happens to its ΔG as temperature rises?

① ΔG becomes more negative, since heat speeds up every reaction　 ② ΔG doesn't change, since only ΔH determines ΔG　 ③ ΔG becomes less negative and eventually positive　 ④ ΔG becomes more negative, because T is measured in kelvin　 ⑤ The reaction becomes spontaneous only above some temperature

**Answer: ③ ΔG becomes less negative and eventually positive**

With ΔS negative, the −TΔS term is positive and grows with T, eating into the favourable ΔH, so the reaction is spontaneous only below some temperature. (Temperature also changes reaction speed, but that's about activation energy, not ΔG.)

*Concepts linked:* ΔG = ΔH − TΔS · Temperature and entropy

---

**Q255. [Multiple Choice · 1점]**

In CO2 + H2O → H2CO3, which description of the bonds is correct?

① Every bond in both molecules breaks, and the atoms recombine at random　 ② Water's bonds stay intact while the whole H2O molecule bonds to the carbon　 ③ Some of the carbon atoms are converted into oxygen atoms along the way　 ④ One C–O bond of CO2 and one O–H of water break; new C–O and O–H bonds form　 ⑤ No bonds break; the two molecules simply cling by hydrogen bonds

**Answer: ④ One C–O bond of CO2 and one O–H of water break; new C–O and O–H bonds form**

Atoms keep their identity; only bonds change. In the notes' example, one of the bonds between C and O in CO2 and one O–H bond in water break, and new bonds form between CO2's carbon and water's oxygen and between a CO2 oxygen and water's hydrogen.

*Concepts linked:* Chemical reactions rearrange bonds, not atoms

---

**Q256. [Multiple Choice · 1점]**

A firefly's light comes from a reaction that leaves a product molecule with an electron raised to a higher energy level. What actually produces the light?

① New energy being created as the reaction's product bonds form　 ② The excited electron falling back, releasing energy as light　 ③ Heat from the reaction making the insect's tissues glow　 ④ ATP being converted directly into light with no energy lost　 ⑤ Electrons being destroyed, releasing their energy as light

**Answer: ② The excited electron falling back, releasing energy as light**

When an electron moves from a higher to a lower energy level, the difference is released as heat or light — here, mostly light. Chemical energy is converted into light energy, not created, and electrons aren't destroyed (first law); some energy is still lost as heat (second law).

*Concepts linked:* Electrons dropping release energy · First law · Real-world: fireflies
