### Enzymes & Their Regulation

**Q257. [보기 Combination · 3점] Two paths, one reaction**

Using the diagram, which statements in <보기> are correct?

**◇ Diagram — One reaction by two different paths.** Plot of free energy (y-axis, no numbers) against course of reaction (x-axis). Two curves leave the same reactants plateau on the left and end on the same, lower products plateau on the right: curve 1 (solid) rises to a tall peak and curve 2 (dashed) to a much lower peak at the same point. A dashed line extends the reactant level to the right. Double-headed arrow a spans from the reactant level up to the peak of curve 1, arrow b from the reactant level up to the peak of curve 2, and arrow c, near the products, from the reactant level down to the product level.

> **(가)** Curves 1 and 2 show the same reaction, with and without an enzyme.
>
> **(나)** a, b and c mark three energy differences.

**<보기>**
- ㄱ. Curve 2 is the enzyme-catalyzed path; adding the enzyme replaces barrier a with the smaller barrier b.
- ㄴ. c is the same for both curves, so the enzyme doesn't change how much free energy the reaction releases.
- ㄷ. Because the enzyme is a folded protein, heating it until it unfolds would return the reaction to curve 1.

① ㄱ　 ② ㄴ　 ③ ㄱ, ㄴ　 ④ ㄱ, ㄷ　 ⑤ ㄱ, ㄴ, ㄷ

**Answer: ⑤ ㄱ, ㄴ, ㄷ**
- ㄱ — **O** — Enzymes speed reactions by lowering the activation energy needed to reach the transition state.
- ㄴ — **O** — c is ΔG, set by the reactant and product levels; it is the same with and without an enzyme.
- ㄷ — **O** — The lower barrier depends on the active site, which exists only in the folded protein; a denatured enzyme no longer catalyzes (Module 5).

*Concepts linked:* Enzymes lower activation energy · ΔG unchanged · Active site needs folding (Module 5)

---

**Q258. [보기 Combination · 3점] Three encounters at the enzyme**

Using the diagram, which statements in <보기> are correct?

**◇ Diagram — One enzyme meets three different molecules.** Three panels numbered 1 to 3, each showing the same enzyme: a large rounded shape with a notch, the active site, in its top edge. Panel 1: the substrate, a small wedge that exactly fits the notch, sits in it. Panel 2: a molecule X with the same wedge outline but a different colour sits in the notch, and the substrate is drawn just above it, not bound, beside a small ✕. Panel 3: a small round molecule Y is bound to the lower left side of the enzyme, away from the notch; the notch is drawn as a narrow V, narrower than the base of the substrate, which is drawn above it beside a small ✕.

> **(가)** X resembles the substrate in shape.
>
> **(나)** Y binds away from the active site and changes the enzyme's shape.

**<보기>**
- ㄱ. Extra substrate would overcome Y's effect just as well, since Y also blocks the active site directly.
- ㄴ. Adding a large excess of substrate could reduce X's effect, because substrate and X compete for the same site.
- ㄷ. If X binds only briefly and then falls off, its inhibition is reversible.

① ㄴ　 ② ㄱ, ㄴ　 ③ ㄱ, ㄷ　 ④ ㄴ, ㄷ　 ⑤ ㄱ, ㄴ, ㄷ

**Answer: ④ ㄴ, ㄷ**
- ㄱ — **X** — Y is a noncompetitive inhibitor: it binds elsewhere and distorts the active site, so adding substrate can't push it out.
- ㄴ — **O** — X is a competitive inhibitor: the more substrate there is, the more often substrate rather than X wins the active site.
- ㄷ — **O** — Inhibitors that bind and come off again are reversible; those that bind permanently are irreversible.

*Concepts linked:* Competitive vs noncompetitive inhibition · Reversible vs irreversible

---

**Q259. [보기 Combination · 3점] Brakes on a branched pathway**

Using the diagram, which statements in <보기> are correct?

**◇ Diagram — A branched metabolic pathway.** Flow diagram of a branched pathway: boxes A, B, C and D in a top row joined by arrows labelled e1 (A to B), e2 (B to C) and e3 (C to D); a second arrow labelled e4 leaves B and leads down to E, and an arrow labelled e5 leads from E to F in a lower row. D and F are each labelled end product.

> **(가)** D inhibits e2, the first enzyme on D's own branch.
>
> **(나)** F inhibits e4, the first enzyme on F's own branch.

**<보기>**
- ㄱ. When D is plentiful, B can still be used to make E and F instead of being spent on more D.
- ㄴ. If D instead inhibited e1, a surplus of D would leave the making of F unaffected.
- ㄷ. D must bind e2's active site in place of B, since feedback inhibition is a kind of competitive inhibition.

① ㄱ　 ② ㄴ　 ③ ㄷ　 ④ ㄱ, ㄷ　 ⑤ ㄴ, ㄷ

**Answer: ① ㄱ**
- ㄱ — **O** — Blocking e2 shuts only D's branch; B remains available to e4, so making F continues.
- ㄴ — **X** — e1 makes B, which both branches need. Inhibiting e1 would starve the F branch too — which is why feedback usually targets the first step after a branch.
- ㄷ — **X** — End products usually look nothing like the first substrate; they typically bind a separate (allosteric) site and change the enzyme's shape, as isoleucine does to threonine dehydratase.

*Concepts linked:* Feedback inhibition · Allosteric enzymes · Metabolic pathways

---

**Q260. [Matching · 3점] What explains it?**

Match each observation about an enzyme to the explanation that fits.

**Observation**
A. Boiling an enzyme destroys its activity for good
B. Mercury ions bond permanently to the enzyme's cysteines
C. A pathway's end product shuts down its first enzyme
D. A substrate look-alike slows the reaction, but extra substrate overcomes it
E. A molecule binding away from the active site makes the enzyme work faster

**Explanation**
1. Heat unfolds the chain, destroying the active site
2. Feedback inhibition of an allosteric enzyme
3. Competitive inhibition
4. Irreversible inhibition
5. An activator

**Correct matches:**
- A = Boiling an enzyme destroys its activity for good → Heat unfolds the chain, destroying the active site
- B = Mercury ions bond permanently to the enzyme's cysteines → Irreversible inhibition
- C = A pathway's end product shuts down its first enzyme → Feedback inhibition of an allosteric enzyme
- D = A substrate look-alike slows the reaction, but extra substrate overcomes it → Competitive inhibition
- E = A molecule binding away from the active site makes the enzyme work faster → An activator

Activity depends on the folded active site, which heat destroys. Permanent bonding = irreversible inhibition; product-triggered shutdown = feedback inhibition at an allosteric site; look-alikes compete for the active site; activators increase activity.

*Concepts linked:* Enzyme regulation · The active site needs folding

---

**Q261. [True / False · 1점]**

> In the diagram, arrow c points down from the reactant level to the product level.

**◇ Diagram — One reaction by two different paths.** Plot of free energy (y-axis, no numbers) against course of reaction (x-axis). Two curves leave the same reactants plateau on the left and end on the same, lower products plateau on the right: curve 1 (solid) rises to a tall peak and curve 2 (dashed) to a much lower peak at the same point. A dashed line extends the reactant level to the right. Double-headed arrow a spans from the reactant level up to the peak of curve 1, arrow b from the reactant level up to the peak of curve 2, and arrow c, near the products, from the reactant level down to the product level.

**Claim:** So this reaction is endergonic, and the enzyme supplies the energy it needs to proceed.

**Answer: False**

Products below reactants means a negative ΔG: the reaction is exergonic. Enzymes don't supply energy or change ΔG; they only lower the barrier (a → b).

*Concepts linked:* Reading ΔG from a profile · What enzymes do

---

**Q262. [True / False · 2점]**

> Aspirin permanently attaches a small chemical group (an acetyl group) to an amino acid in the active site of the COX enzyme, which helps make inflammatory signals.

**Claim:** Aspirin is therefore an irreversible inhibitor: the affected enzyme molecules stay inactive until the cell makes new ones.

**Answer: True**

A covalent change to the active site doesn't wear off, so the enzyme stays blocked; only newly made COX restores activity. Blood platelets can't make new protein, which is why one aspirin's effect on them lasts for days.

*Concepts linked:* Irreversible inhibition · Real-world: aspirin

---

**Q263. [True / False · 1점]**

> Isoleucine, the end product of a five-step pathway that starts from threonine, binds the pathway's first enzyme (threonine dehydratase) at a site separate from its active site.

**Claim:** When isoleucine runs low, threonine dehydratase stays inhibited, because this kind of inhibition can't be reversed.

**Answer: False**

Allosteric feedback inhibition is reversible: as isoleucine is used up, it leaves the enzyme, the enzyme reactivates, and the pathway makes more. That's how the pathway matches supply to demand without wasting energy.

*Concepts linked:* Feedback inhibition is reversible · Allosteric regulation

---

**Q264. [True / False · 1점]**

> When glucose binds hexokinase, the enzyme's two lobes swing closed around it like a clamp.

**Claim:** This fits the lock-and-key model better than induced fit, since the enzyme ends up perfectly shaped around glucose.

**Answer: False**

In the lock-and-key model the site already has the right shape. Hexokinase changes shape when glucose binds — the defining feature of induced fit.

*Concepts linked:* Induced fit vs lock-and-key

---

**Q265. [True / False · 2점]**

> Carbonic anhydrase speeds up CO2 + H2O → H2CO3 in body tissues and H2CO3 → CO2 + H2O in the lungs.

**Claim:** One enzyme speeds both directions because it lowers the barrier for the forward and reverse reactions alike, while concentrations decide which way the reaction runs.

**Answer: True**

An enzyme lowers the transition-state peak, which both directions must cross. Where CO2 is high (tissues) the reaction runs forward; where CO2 is being breathed out (lungs) it runs backward.

*Concepts linked:* Enzymes speed both directions · Concentrations set direction

---

**Q266. [True / False · 2점]**

> Some drugs are designed to resemble a reaction's transition state rather than its substrate.

**Claim:** Such drugs bind an enzyme's active site even more tightly than the substrate does, since the active site is shaped to stabilize the transition state.

**Answer: True**

Enzymes lower activation energy by binding and stabilizing the transition state. A stable look-alike of that state fits the active site best of all and blocks it — the design idea behind several drugs.

*Concepts linked:* The active site stabilizes the transition state

---

**Q267. [Multiple Choice · 2점]**

Methanol poisoning is treated with ethanol. Both are substrates of the same liver enzyme, which turns methanol into a toxic product. Which panel of the diagram best shows how ethanol helps?

**◇ Diagram — One enzyme meets three different molecules.** Three panels numbered 1 to 3, each showing the same enzyme: a large rounded shape with a notch, the active site, in its top edge. Panel 1: the substrate, a small wedge that exactly fits the notch, sits in it. Panel 2: a molecule X with the same wedge outline but a different colour sits in the notch, and the substrate is drawn just above it, not bound, beside a small ✕. Panel 3: a small round molecule Y is bound to the lower left side of the enzyme, away from the notch; the notch is drawn as a narrow V, narrower than the base of the substrate, which is drawn above it beside a small ✕.

① Panel 2: ethanol competes with methanol for the active site　 ② Panel 3: ethanol binds elsewhere on the enzyme and reshapes it　 ③ Panel 1: ethanol helps methanol fit into the active site　 ④ Panel 3: ethanol permanently destroys the enzyme　 ⑤ Panel 2: ethanol binds permanently, so no product forms

**Answer: ① Panel 2: ethanol competes with methanol for the active site**

Ethanol and methanol fit the same active site, so plenty of ethanol keeps the enzyme busy (competitive inhibition) and slows toxic-product formation while the kidneys clear the methanol. Ethanol eventually leaves the site: the effect is reversible.

*Concepts linked:* Competitive inhibition · Real-world: methanol poisoning

---

**Q268. [Multiple Choice · 1점]**

In a single-chain enzyme, the three amino acids that form the active site are numbers 40, 95 and 210 in the sequence. How can residues so far apart work together?

① Peptide bonds link them directly to one another　 ② They lie next to each other in the primary structure　 ③ Folding brings them together in three-dimensional space　 ④ Each one comes from a different subunit　 ⑤ They pair with each other through hydrogen-bonded bases

**Answer: ③ Folding brings them together in three-dimensional space**

The active site is formed by specific amino acids, often far apart in the sequence, that tertiary folding places side by side. Unfold the chain and the active site disappears.

*Concepts linked:* Active site formed by folding · Tertiary structure (Module 5)

---

**Q269. [Multiple Choice · 1점]**

One molecule of an enzyme can convert thousands of substrate molecules per second. Which feature of the cycle E + S ⇌ ES ⇌ EP ⇌ E + P makes this possible?

① The enzyme becomes part of each product molecule　 ② The enzyme is released unchanged at the end of each cycle　 ③ Each cycle transfers energy from the enzyme to the substrate　 ④ The enzyme makes ΔG more negative, so products form instantly　 ⑤ The ES complex is permanent, so binding never has to repeat

**Answer: ② The enzyme is released unchanged at the end of each cycle**

After converting substrate to product in the active site, the enzyme lets the product go (EP → E + P) and is free to bind the next substrate. It is a catalyst: not consumed, not a source of energy, and no change to ΔG.

*Concepts linked:* The enzyme–substrate cycle · Catalysts aren't consumed

---

**Q270. [Multiple Choice · 1점]**

Which observation supports the induced-fit model rather than the lock-and-key model?

① The substrate already fits the active site exactly before it binds　 ② The enzyme acts on only one particular substrate　 ③ The enzyme speeds the reaction without being used up　 ④ ΔG is the same with and without the enzyme　 ⑤ The enzyme's shape changes measurably when the substrate binds

**Answer: ⑤ The enzyme's shape changes measurably when the substrate binds**

Both models explain specificity and catalysis; what sets induced fit apart is that binding itself reshapes the enzyme around the substrate. A perfect pre-existing fit is the lock-and-key picture.

*Concepts linked:* Induced fit vs lock-and-key

## Connecting Modules 1–6 — Cross-Module Synthesis

### Connecting Modules 1–4

**Q271. [보기 Combination · 3점] Why DNA hugs histones**

Which statements in <보기> are correct?

> **(가)** Histone proteins are rich in amino acids whose R groups contain amino groups, which are positively charged at cellular pH (Module 2).
>
> **(나)** DNA wraps around histones to form nucleosomes, the 10 nm fiber (Module 4).

**<보기>**
- ㄱ. The binding works through hydrogen bonds to specific bases, so histones wrap only particular DNA sequences.
- ㄴ. Neutralizing DNA's phosphate charges would make it bind histones even more tightly.
- ㄷ. Attraction between the histones' positive charges and DNA's negatively charged phosphate backbone helps the DNA wrap tightly.

① ㄱ　 ② ㄷ　 ③ ㄱ, ㄴ　 ④ ㄱ, ㄷ　 ⑤ ㄴ, ㄷ

**Answer: ② ㄷ**
- ㄱ — **X** — The attraction is to the backbone phosphates, which are the same along every sequence, so histones package DNA largely regardless of sequence.
- ㄴ — **X** — Removing the negative charges would remove the electrostatic attraction, loosening the DNA–histone interaction.
- ㄷ — **O** — Opposite charges attract: the ionized phosphates on the outside of the helix meet the positive amino groups of the histones.

*Concepts linked:* Amino-group charge (Mod 2) · Negative backbone · Nucleosomes

---

**Q272. [보기 Combination · 3점] Macromolecules in one membrane**

Using the diagram and Module 2, which statements in <보기> are correct?

**◇ Diagram — The fluid mosaic model: what sits where in the membrane.** Cross-section of a plasma membrane: a phospholipid bilayer; a channel protein spanning the whole bilayer; a peripheral protein sitting on the cytoplasmic face; cholesterol in both layers, its small polar end level with the phospholipid heads and its body among the tails; and sugar chains of a glycolipid and a glycoprotein on the extracellular side.

> **(가)** Phospholipids and cholesterol, channel and peripheral proteins, and sugar chains all appear in the diagram.
>
> **(나)** Module 2 grouped biological macromolecules into carbohydrates, lipids, proteins, and nucleic acids.

**<보기>**
- ㄱ. Three of the four macromolecule classes are structural parts of the plasma membrane; nucleic acids are not.
- ㄴ. Cholesterol, a steroid with four fused rings, is completely nonpolar, so it sits entirely inside the hydrophobic core.
- ㄷ. The sugar chains are built from monosaccharides linked by glycosidic bonds and are covalently attached to lipids or proteins on the extracellular side.

① ㄱ　 ② ㄱ, ㄴ　 ③ ㄱ, ㄷ　 ④ ㄴ, ㄷ　 ⑤ ㄱ, ㄴ, ㄷ

**Answer: ③ ㄱ, ㄷ**
- ㄱ — **O** — Membranes are made of lipids, proteins, and carbohydrates.
- ㄴ — **X** — Cholesterol has a polar OH group that sits near the phospholipid heads; only its rings and tail are buried among the tails.
- ㄷ — **O** — Glycolipids and glycoproteins carry covalently attached carbohydrates, and all of them face the extracellular fluid.

*Concepts linked:* Membrane components · Glycosidic bonds (Mod 2) · Cholesterol structure

---

**Q273. [보기 Combination · 2점] Cell properties, molecular machinery**

Module 1 listed basic properties shared by cells. Which statements in <보기> correctly link a property to Module 3–4 machinery?

> **(가)** Cells respond to stimuli.
>
> **(나)** Cells acquire and use energy, and they follow a genetic program.

**<보기>**
- ㄱ. Acquiring and using energy relies on mitochondria, which count as part of the endomembrane system because they are enclosed by membranes.
- ㄴ. Responding to a protein hormone relies on receptor proteins in the plasma membrane, because the hormone cannot cross the bilayer.
- ㄷ. Following a genetic program relies on the central dogma, in which information flows from DNA to RNA to protein.

① ㄱ　 ② ㄴ　 ③ ㄱ, ㄷ　 ④ ㄴ, ㄷ　 ⑤ ㄱ, ㄴ, ㄷ

**Answer: ④ ㄴ, ㄷ**
- ㄱ — **X** — Mitochondria do convert energy from sugars into ATP, but they are not part of the endomembrane system; membership depends on bridge or vesicle connections, not on having membranes.
- ㄴ — **O** — Receptors let the cell receive signals from macromolecules that stay outside.
- ㄷ — **O** — Genes (DNA) are transcribed into RNA, and mRNA is translated into the proteins that carry out the program.

*Concepts linked:* Basic cell properties (Mod 1) · Receptors · Mitochondria · Central dogma

---

**Q274. [보기 Combination · 2점] Why study E. coli?**

Which statements in <보기> are correct?

> **(가)** Module 1 lists E. coli as a model organism for studying DNA replication, transcription, and translation.
>
> **(나)** In E. coli, transcription and translation are coupled.

**<보기>**
- ㄱ. Because E. coli has no nucleus, it has no RNA polymerase and uses its ribosomes to copy DNA into RNA.
- ㄴ. In E. coli, a researcher could see ribosomes translating an mRNA that is still attached to its DNA through RNA polymerase — something never seen inside a human nucleus.
- ㄷ. E. coli is a poor model for human transcription, since transcription works completely differently in prokaryotes.

① ㄱ　 ② ㄴ　 ③ ㄷ　 ④ ㄱ, ㄴ　 ⑤ ㄴ, ㄷ

**Answer: ② ㄴ**
- ㄱ — **X** — E. coli has a single type of RNA polymerase that transcribes all its genes; ribosomes translate.
- ㄴ — **O** — Coupling is possible only without a nuclear envelope; in human cells translation happens in the cytoplasm after export.
- ㄷ — **X** — Despite the difference in location, transcription is a similar process in prokaryotes and eukaryotes — which is why E. coli is a useful model.

*Concepts linked:* Model organisms (Mod 1) · Coupled transcription–translation

---

**Q275. [보기 Combination · 3점] Building a pore from amino acids**

Which statements in <보기> are correct?

> **(가)** In a channel protein, the amino acids lining the pore have polar or charged R groups, while those on the outer surface facing the lipid tails have nonpolar R groups.
>
> **(나)** A mutation replaces one charged, pore-lining amino acid with a nonpolar one.

**<보기>**
- ㄱ. The nonpolar outer surface lets the protein sit stably within the hydrophobic interior of the bilayer.
- ㄴ. The polar lining creates an aqueous pore through which ions can pass without contacting the lipid tails.
- ㄷ. Because the changed amino acid lines the pore, the mutant protein can no longer insert into the membrane.

① ㄱ　 ② ㄴ　 ③ ㄱ, ㄴ　 ④ ㄱ, ㄷ　 ⑤ ㄱ, ㄴ, ㄷ

**Answer: ③ ㄱ, ㄴ**
- ㄱ — **O** — Nonpolar R groups interact favourably with the lipid tails, anchoring the integral protein.
- ㄴ — **O** — Channels are integral proteins surrounding an aqueous pore — the hydrophilic path that ions need.
- ㄷ — **X** — Insertion depends on the nonpolar outer surface, which is unchanged. The swap alters the pore's lining, which could change which ions pass — one R-group change can alter function.

*Concepts linked:* R groups (Mod 2) · Channel proteins · Structure determines function

---

**Q276. [보기 Combination · 3점] One ring or two?**

Which statements in <보기> are correct?

> **(가)** A copying error places an A opposite a G at one site in a DNA double helix.
>
> **(나)** An RNA molecule folds back on itself, so that two stretches of the same strand pair with each other.

**<보기>**
- ㄱ. At the A–G site in (가), the helix would bulge outward, because both bases have two rings.
- ㄴ. The paired stretches in (나) would contain A–T pairs, since adenine always pairs with thymine.
- ㄷ. The folded RNA in (나) would be less chemically stable than a DNA molecule of the same length, because of the 2'-OH on each ribose.

① ㄱ　 ② ㄴ　 ③ ㄱ, ㄷ　 ④ ㄴ, ㄷ　 ⑤ ㄱ, ㄴ, ㄷ

**Answer: ③ ㄱ, ㄷ**
- ㄱ — **O** — A and G are both purines; every normal rung is two rings plus one ring (purine + pyrimidine), which keeps the width constant.
- ㄴ — **X** — RNA has uracil instead of thymine, so its adenines pair with U — and U, like T, is a one-ring pyrimidine.
- ㄷ — **O** — The 2'-hydroxyl makes RNA more reactive, so RNA is less stable than DNA.

*Concepts linked:* Purines vs pyrimidines (Mod 2) · Uracil · Ribose 2'-OH

---

**Q277. [보기 Combination · 3점] Energy conversions at membranes**

Module 3 lists energy transduction among membrane functions. Which statements in <보기> are correct?

> **(가)** Chloroplast thylakoid membranes hold chlorophyll.
>
> **(나)** The inner mitochondrial membrane holds the electron transport chain and ATP synthase.

**<보기>**
- ㄱ. Because both organelles have membranes that do this work, both belong to the endomembrane system.
- ㄴ. Thylakoid membranes convert light energy into chemical energy, an example of energy transduction.
- ㄷ. The inner mitochondrial membrane converts energy from food molecules into ATP — energy transduction serving the Module 1 property 'acquires and uses energy'.

① ㄱ　 ② ㄷ　 ③ ㄱ, ㄴ　 ④ ㄱ, ㄷ　 ⑤ ㄴ, ㄷ

**Answer: ⑤ ㄴ, ㄷ**
- ㄱ — **X** — Mitochondria and chloroplasts are membranous but not part of the endomembrane system; membership depends on bridge or vesicle connections.
- ㄴ — **O** — Pigments in the thylakoid membrane collect the light energy that the chloroplast uses to make sugars.
- ㄷ — **O** — The ETC and ATP synthase in the inner membrane harness energy from sugars to make ATP.

*Concepts linked:* Energy transduction · Thylakoid · Inner membrane · EMS membership

---

**Q278. [보기 Combination · 2점] Membranes aren't membership**

Which statements in <보기> are correct?

> **(가)** Module 1 classified mitochondria, chloroplasts, peroxisomes, ribosomes, the nucleolus, and the cytoskeleton as non-endomembrane structures.
>
> **(나)** Mitochondria and chloroplasts are semi-autonomous: they grow and divide on their own and contain their own circular genomes.

**<보기>**
- ㄱ. Mitochondria and chloroplasts are excluded from the endomembrane system because they are not linked to it by membrane bridges or vesicle traffic, not because they lack membranes.
- ㄴ. Ribosomes and the nucleolus are non-endomembrane for a simpler reason: neither is enclosed by a membrane.
- ㄷ. Because both organelles carry their own DNA, they need no proteins or support from the rest of the cell.

① ㄱ　 ② ㄱ, ㄴ　 ③ ㄱ, ㄷ　 ④ ㄴ, ㄷ　 ⑤ ㄱ, ㄴ, ㄷ

**Answer: ② ㄱ, ㄴ**
- ㄱ — **O** — Both have double membranes; what they lack is a physical or vesicle connection to the ER–Golgi network.
- ㄴ — **O** — Ribosomes are RNA–protein machines, and the nucleolus is a region of the nucleus with no membrane of its own.
- ㄷ — **X** — 'Semi'-autonomous means partly independent: they have their own genomes and divide on their own but still depend on the host cell.

*Concepts linked:* EMS vs non-EMS (Mod 1) · Semi-autonomous organelles

---

**Q279. [보기 Combination · 2점] How many genomes in one cell?**

Which statements in <보기> are correct?

> **(가)** A genome is the genetic material of an organism — for example, a bacterial genome, a nuclear genome, or a mitochondrial genome.
>
> **(나)** Gene expression is regulated: genes can be turned on or off.

**<보기>**
- ㄱ. A human liver cell contains at least two genomes: the nuclear genome and the mitochondrial genome.
- ㄴ. Every gene in the nuclear genome is expressed in every cell at all times.
- ㄷ. The mitochondrial genome is circular like a typical bacterial chromosome — evidence consistent with endosymbiosis.

① ㄱ　 ② ㄴ　 ③ ㄷ　 ④ ㄱ, ㄷ　 ⑤ ㄴ, ㄷ

**Answer: ④ ㄱ, ㄷ**
- ㄱ — **O** — The nucleus holds the nuclear genome, and each mitochondrion carries its own small, circular genome.
- ㄴ — **X** — Gene expression is regulated; different cell types turn on different subsets of genes.
- ㄷ — **O** — Circular genomes are one of the bacterial features mitochondria retain.

*Concepts linked:* Genome types · Endosymbiosis · Regulated gene expression

**Q280. [Matching · 3점] Polarity decides the route**

Using Module 2's ideas about polarity, size, and charge, match each molecule to how it can cross a plasma membrane.

**Molecule**
A. O2
B. Estrogen (a steroid hormone)
C. Na+
D. H2O
E. Insulin (a protein)

**How it crosses**
1. Small and nonpolar: dissolves straight through the lipid core
2. Charged: blocked by the hydrophobic core; needs a channel or pump
3. Small and uncharged but polar: crosses the bilayer, and faster through aquaporin channels
4. Too large to cross; acts through a surface receptor instead
5. A lipid: crosses by simple diffusion to reach receptors inside the cell

**Correct matches:**
- A = O2 → Small and nonpolar: dissolves straight through the lipid core
- B = Estrogen (a steroid hormone) → A lipid: crosses by simple diffusion to reach receptors inside the cell
- C = Na+ → Charged: blocked by the hydrophobic core; needs a channel or pump
- D = H2O → Small and uncharged but polar: crosses the bilayer, and faster through aquaporin channels
- E = Insulin (a protein) → Too large to cross; acts through a surface receptor instead

The hydrophobic interior lets small or nonpolar molecules through and stops charged and large ones — polarity, charge, and size (Module 2) predict each route.

*Concepts linked:* Selective permeability · Polarity (Mod 2)

---

**Q281. [Matching · 4점] Real-world phenomena, cellular explanations**

Match each real-world phenomenon to the Module 1–4 mechanism behind it.

**Phenomenon**
A. Salting meat keeps bacteria from growing
B. Soap and water destroy enveloped viruses
C. Digoxin strengthens heart contractions
D. Oral rehydration solution contains both salt and sugar
E. Death-cap mushroom poisoning destroys liver cells
F. Rifampin treats tuberculosis without killing human cells

**Mechanism**
1. It blocks the single bacterial RNA polymerase, which differs from human polymerases
2. Partial Na+/K+ pump block weakens the Na+ gradient that powers Ca2+ removal
3. A hypertonic environment pulls water out of microbial cells by osmosis
4. Amphipathic molecules break a lipid membrane apart into micelles
5. A toxin blocks the eukaryotic polymerase that makes mRNA
6. Na+/glucose symport draws solute into intestinal cells, and water follows

**Correct matches:**
- A = Salting meat keeps bacteria from growing → A hypertonic environment pulls water out of microbial cells by osmosis
- B = Soap and water destroy enveloped viruses → Amphipathic molecules break a lipid membrane apart into micelles
- C = Digoxin strengthens heart contractions → Partial Na+/K+ pump block weakens the Na+ gradient that powers Ca2+ removal
- D = Oral rehydration solution contains both salt and sugar → Na+/glucose symport draws solute into intestinal cells, and water follows
- E = Death-cap mushroom poisoning destroys liver cells → A toxin blocks the eukaryotic polymerase that makes mRNA
- F = Rifampin treats tuberculosis without killing human cells → It blocks the single bacterial RNA polymerase, which differs from human polymerases

Each case turns on one mechanism from this course: osmosis, amphipathic self-assembly, primary vs secondary active transport, co-transport, and the different RNA polymerases of bacteria and eukaryotes.

*Concepts linked:* Osmosis · Amphipathic lipids · Active transport · RNA polymerases

---

**Q282. [Matching · 3점] One bond type, many jobs**

Match each Module 3–4 structure or process to the Module 2 bond or interaction it depends on.

**Structure or process**
A. Holding the two DNA strands together
B. Linking nucleotides within an RNA strand
C. Keeping phospholipid tails together in the bilayer
D. Attaching sugar chains to membrane proteins
E. Holding peripheral proteins to the membrane surface

**Bond or interaction**
1. Phosphodiester bonds, formed as pyrophosphate is released
2. Noncovalent bonds that can break without disrupting the bilayer
3. Hydrogen bonds between complementary bases
4. Hydrophobic interactions: water excludes the nonpolar tails
5. Covalent bonds added by Golgi enzymes (glycosylation)

**Correct matches:**
- A = Holding the two DNA strands together → Hydrogen bonds between complementary bases
- B = Linking nucleotides within an RNA strand → Phosphodiester bonds, formed as pyrophosphate is released
- C = Keeping phospholipid tails together in the bilayer → Hydrophobic interactions: water excludes the nonpolar tails
- D = Attaching sugar chains to membrane proteins → Covalent bonds added by Golgi enzymes (glycosylation)
- E = Holding peripheral proteins to the membrane surface → Noncovalent bonds that can break without disrupting the bilayer

Strong covalent bonds build each polymer, while weak noncovalent forces hold assemblies together — which is why DNA can unzip, bilayers self-heal, and peripheral proteins come off easily.

*Concepts linked:* Covalent vs noncovalent (Mod 2) · DNA · Membranes

---

**Q283. [True / False · 2점]**

> Lysosomes keep their interior near pH 5, while the surrounding cytosol stays near pH 7.

**Claim:** Since pH 5 is only two units below pH 7, the lysosome holds only about twice the H+ concentration of the cytosol, so maintaining it takes little energy.

**Answer: False**

pH is a log scale: two units is a 10² = 100-fold difference in [H+]. Keeping H+ 100× more concentrated inside requires the lysosome's proton pump to move H+ against its gradient — active transport powered by ATP.

*Concepts linked:* pH log scale (Mod 2) · Lysosome proton pump · Active transport

---

**Q284. [True / False · 1점]**

> When phospholipids are shaken in water, they spontaneously form closed bilayer vesicles. If the water is replaced by a nonpolar solvent such as hexane, the vesicles fall apart.

**◇ Diagram — Phospholipid shape decides what forms in water.** Phospholipids with one tail and a large head pack into a micelle with their tails in the core; phospholipids with two tails and a smaller head form a bilayer sheet with the tails sandwiched inside, which closes into a liposome that encloses water and has no exposed edges.

**Claim:** This shows that the bilayer is held together mainly by water's tendency to exclude the nonpolar tails, not by bonds joining the phospholipids to one another.

**Answer: True**

In water, the hydrophobic effect pushes the tails together and the heads outward; take the water away and nothing holds the arrangement, because no covalent bonds join neighbouring phospholipids. The same effect makes the bilayer self-healing.

*Concepts linked:* Hydrophobic effect (Mod 2) · Self-assembly · Self-healing

---

**Q285. [True / False · 1점]**

> HIV consists of an RNA genome and the enzyme reverse transcriptase, packed inside a protein coat and an outer envelope.

**Claim:** Because HIV carries its own genetic material and its own enzyme, it can reproduce on its own outside a host cell.

**Answer: False**

Viruses are exceptions to the cell theory: outside a host they are inert. HIV must use the host cell's machinery — nucleotides, RNA polymerase, ribosomes — to make new viruses.

*Concepts linked:* Viruses as exceptions (Mod 1) · Reverse transcription

---

**Q286. [True / False · 2점]**

> Ribosomal subunits are assembled in the nucleolus (Module 1), a region of the nucleus without its own membrane, from rRNA and proteins.

**Claim:** Because ribosomal subunits are large RNA–protein complexes, they must leave the nucleus through nuclear pores by active transport rather than by passive diffusion.

**Answer: True**

Small molecules and ions diffuse through nuclear pores, but large proteins and RNA — and complexes of the two — require active transport.

*Concepts linked:* Nucleolus (Mod 1) · Nuclear pores · Ribosomes

---

**Q287. [True / False · 1점]**

> Soft-stemmed plants wilt when they run short of water, even though their cellulose cell walls remain intact (Module 1).

**Claim:** Their upright posture depends on turgor pressure from water-filled vacuoles pushing against the walls, so losing water weakens support even with intact walls.

**Answer: True**

A wall resists expansion but can't hold a soft plant up by itself; water entering by osmosis keeps the cells turgid, and wilting is the loss of that pressure.

*Concepts linked:* Plant wall and vacuole (Mod 1) · Turgor

---

**Q288. [True / False · 1점]**

> Lysosomal enzymes break proteins, nucleic acids, lipids, and complex carbohydrates down into smaller building blocks.

**Claim:** These digestive reactions are condensation reactions, because each bond broken releases a water molecule.

**Answer: False**

Breaking polymers apart is hydrolysis: each bond split consumes a water molecule. Condensation is the reverse — it joins monomers and releases water (Module 2).

*Concepts linked:* Hydrolysis vs condensation (Mod 2) · Lysosomal digestion

---

**Q289. [True / False · 1점]**

> DNA polymerase, RNA polymerase, and reverse transcriptase all build nucleic acids by adding nucleotides to the 3' end.

**Claim:** So all three enzymes form phosphodiester bonds between the 3'-OH of the growing strand and the 5' phosphate of the incoming nucleotide (Module 2).

**Answer: True**

Whatever the template (DNA or RNA) and product, chain growth uses the same chemistry: a 3'-OH attacks the incoming nucleotide's 5' phosphate, so every strand grows 5'→3'.

*Concepts linked:* Phosphodiester bond (Mod 2) · 5'→3' synthesis

---

**Q290. [True / False · 2점]**

> Mitochondria resemble proteobacteria, yet Module 1's tree places Eukarya closer to Archaea than to Bacteria.

**◇ Diagram — Two hypotheses for how the eukaryotic cell arose.** Two proposed routes to the eukaryotic cell, each drawn as four stages. Hypothesis 1: an archaeon; infolding forms a nucleus and endomembrane system; the cell engulfs a proteobacterium; a eukaryote with mitochondria. Hypothesis 2: an archaeon with a proteobacterium; the symbiont lives inside the host; infolding forms a nucleus; a eukaryote with mitochondria.

**Claim:** These facts contradict each other: if mitochondria came from bacteria, eukaryotes must have branched from Bacteria.

**Answer: False**

Eukaryotic cells are partnerships: the host lineage (nucleus and cytoplasm) is archaeal, while the mitochondria descend from a bacterium that was engulfed or taken in as a partner. Both facts fit the diagram.

*Concepts linked:* Three-domain tree (Mod 1) · Endosymbiosis

---

**Q291. [True / False · 1점]**

> Most cytosolic proteins fold properly and work near pH 7 (Module 2: pH affects protein structure).

**Claim:** If a lysosome leaked, pH-5 fluid could damage nearby cytosolic proteins by changing the charges on ionizable amino and carboxyl groups, disrupting their folding.

**Answer: True**

Extra H+ changes which groups carry charge, disturbing the ionic and hydrogen bonds that hold a protein's shape — one reason lysosomal contents must stay separated from the cytosol.

*Concepts linked:* pH and protein structure (Mod 2) · Lysosome compartmentalization

---

**Q292. [True / False · 1점]**

> Many viruses, including influenza, bind specific receptor molecules on a host cell's surface and are brought inside in coated vesicles.

**Claim:** Such a virus is taken up far more efficiently by cells that display its receptor than by cells that could take it in only by pinocytosis.

**Answer: True**

Receptor-mediated endocytosis concentrates specific cargo in coated pits, while pinocytosis just samples fluid. Which cells carry the receptor largely determines which cells a virus can infect.

*Concepts linked:* Receptor-mediated endocytosis · Viruses (Mod 1)

---

**Q293. [True / False · 1점]**

> Red blood cells keep their flexible disc shape because membrane proteins tie the plasma membrane to a network of cytoskeleton proteins just beneath it. In hereditary spherocytosis, some of these linking proteins are defective.

**Claim:** Patients' red blood cells would be expected to lose their disc shape and become rounder and more fragile, because the membrane is less firmly anchored to the cytoskeleton.

**Answer: True**

Anchoring — linking the membrane to other proteins such as the cytoskeleton, Module 1's protein scaffold — is one of the four membrane-protein functions. Without it, the membrane rounds up into spheres that break easily.

*Concepts linked:* Membrane protein functions · Cytoskeleton (Mod 1)

---

**Q294. [True / False · 2점]**

> The nucleolus, where rRNA is made and ribosomes are assembled (Module 1), lies inside the nucleus, the innermost organelle of the endomembrane system (Module 3).

**Claim:** The nucleolus is therefore part of the endomembrane system.

**Answer: False**

The nucleolus is a region of the nucleus with no membrane, and Module 1 lists it as non-endomembrane. What belongs to the system is the nuclear envelope, the double membrane continuous with the ER.

*Concepts linked:* Nucleolus vs nuclear envelope · EMS membership

---

**Q295. [True / False · 2점]**

> Electron micrographs of an unknown cell show a plasma membrane, a cell wall, and many ribosomes, but no internal membranes of any kind.

**Claim:** This cell cannot have a nucleus, ER, Golgi apparatus, or lysosomes, which fits a prokaryote such as a bacterium.

**Answer: True**

In eukaryotic cells, internal membranes define the organelles; with none, there is no nuclear envelope and no endomembrane compartment. Ribosomes have no membrane, so they can still be present — matching Module 1's prokaryote, which lacks a nucleus and membrane-bound organelles.

*Concepts linked:* Internal membranes define organelles · Prokaryotes (Mod 1)

---

**Q296. [True / False · 1점]**

> Influenza virus particles can remain infectious for hours on a doorknob.

**Claim:** While on the doorknob, the virus particles slowly carry out metabolism and make new virus particles.

**Answer: False**

Viruses are exceptions to the cell theory: outside a host cell they are inert, with no metabolism. They make new particles only after entering a host cell and using its machinery.

*Concepts linked:* Viruses as exceptions (Mod 1)

---

**Q297. [True / False · 1점]**

> ATP (Modules 1–2) consists of adenine, ribose, and three phosphate groups.

**Claim:** ATP has the same parts, attached at the same carbons, as the nucleotides RNA polymerase joins into RNA: the base on ribose's 1' carbon and the phosphates on its 3' carbon.

**Answer: False**

The phosphates attach at the 5' carbon; the 3' carbon carries the –OH that the next nucleotide bonds to. Otherwise the match is exact: ATP is itself one of RNA polymerase's four building blocks.

*Concepts linked:* Nucleotide structure · ATP is a nucleotide (Mod 1–2)

---

**Q298. [Multiple Choice · 2점]**

During its cycle, the Na+/K+ pump is phosphorylated by ATP. Which description of the energy source fits the Module 1–2 picture of ATP?

① The bond between adenine and ribose is broken, releasing the base to power the pump　 ② ATP is added whole to the pump, which then uses all three phosphates at once　 ③ Its outermost phosphate bond breaks, that phosphate goes to the pump, and ADP is released　 ④ ATP transfers two of its phosphates to the pump, leaving AMP behind　 ⑤ No bond is broken; ATP simply binds the pump and acts as a signal

**Answer: ③ Its outermost phosphate bond breaks, that phosphate goes to the pump, and ADP is released**

ATP stores energy in the bonds between its phosphates. Hydrolysis of the terminal bond (ATP → ADP + Pi) releases energy; here the Pi is transferred to the pump, and phosphorylation switches the pump's conformation.

*Concepts linked:* ATP structure (Mod 1–2) · Na+/K+ pump phosphorylation

---

**Q299. [Multiple Choice · 1점]**

Both hypotheses in the diagram begin with an archaeon as the host cell. How does that fit Module 1's three-domain tree of life?

**◇ Diagram — Two hypotheses for how the eukaryotic cell arose.** Two proposed routes to the eukaryotic cell, each drawn as four stages. Hypothesis 1: an archaeon; infolding forms a nucleus and endomembrane system; the cell engulfs a proteobacterium; a eukaryote with mitochondria. Hypothesis 2: an archaeon with a proteobacterium; the symbiont lives inside the host; infolding forms a nucleus; a eukaryote with mitochondria.

① It conflicts: the tree places Eukarya within Bacteria　 ② It conflicts: archaea have no membranes that could fold inward　 ③ It fits only if mitochondria came from archaea rather than from proteobacteria　 ④ It fits: the tree has Eukarya branching from within Archaea　 ⑤ It is irrelevant: the tree includes only prokaryotes

**Answer: ④ It fits: the tree has Eukarya branching from within Archaea**

Module 1's tree has Eukarya branching from within Archaea, matching an archaeal host; the mitochondria themselves were added from proteobacteria, which are Bacteria.

*Concepts linked:* Three-domain tree (Mod 1) · Origin of eukaryotes

---

**Q300. [Multiple Choice · 1점]**

Food chemists hydrogenate vegetable oils, converting C=C double bonds in the fatty acid tails to single bonds (Module 2). If a cell's membrane phospholipids were changed the same way, what would happen?

**◇ Diagram — Four membrane patches: tail length, double bonds, cholesterol.** Four membrane patches drawn side by side: phospholipids with long, straight saturated tails; phospholipids with short, straight saturated tails; phospholipids whose unsaturated tails are kinked at C=C double bonds and sit further apart; and phospholipids with cholesterol molecules wedged between them.

① The tails would kink even more, making the membrane more fluid　 ② The heads would lose their charge, so the bilayer would fall apart in water　 ③ Nothing would change, since fluidity depends only on cholesterol content　 ④ The membrane would become much more permeable to ions such as Na+　 ⑤ The tails would straighten and pack tightly, making the membrane less fluid

**Answer: ⑤ The tails would straighten and pack tightly, making the membrane less fluid**

Removing double bonds makes saturated, straight tails that pack tightly and restrict lateral movement — the same reason saturated fats are solid at room temperature.

*Concepts linked:* Saturated vs unsaturated (Mod 2) · Membrane fluidity · Real-world: hydrogenated oils

---

**Q301. [Multiple Choice · 1점]**

The Golgi adds carbohydrate chains to proteins. How are the sugar units within such a chain linked to one another?

① By peptide bonds formed through hydrolysis reactions　 ② By glycosidic bonds formed through condensation reactions　 ③ By phosphodiester bonds formed through condensation reactions　 ④ By hydrogen bonds only, so the chain can easily rearrange　 ⑤ By ionic bonds between charged sugar groups

**Answer: ② By glycosidic bonds formed through condensation reactions**

Monosaccharides join through glycosidic bonds in condensation reactions (Module 2); the Golgi's enzymes build and attach these chains during glycosylation.

*Concepts linked:* Glycosidic bonds (Mod 2) · Golgi glycosylation

---

**Q302. [Multiple Choice · 2점]**

Module 2 listed protein functions such as enzymes, transport, signaling, and support. Which description fits the Na+/K+-ATPase?

① It is only a transporter; enzymes cannot be embedded in membranes　 ② It is only an enzyme; it merely speeds up ion diffusion　 ③ It is a structural anchor linking the membrane to the cytoskeleton　 ④ Both a transporter and an enzyme: it moves ions and hydrolyzes ATP　 ⑤ It is a receptor that signals when Na+ levels change

**Answer: ④ Both a transporter and an enzyme: it moves ions and hydrolyzes ATP**

'ATPase' names its enzyme activity (hydrolyzing ATP), and it pumps Na+ and K+. Enzymes are one of the four membrane-protein functions, so a single protein can play two roles.

*Concepts linked:* Protein functions (Mod 2) · Membrane protein functions · Na+/K+-ATPase

---

**Q303. [Multiple Choice · 2점]**

In E. coli (Module 1), three genes involved in using the sugar lactose sit side by side and are transcribed from one promoter into a single mRNA. What does this imply?

① The single mRNA is spliced three different ways to make the three proteins　 ② Only the first gene's protein is made, since one mRNA can code for only one protein　 ③ The three proteins are switched on together, translated from one polycistronic mRNA　 ④ The mRNA must be exported through nuclear pores before any protein is made　 ⑤ Each gene still needs its own separate mRNA before its protein can be made

**Answer: ③ The three proteins are switched on together, translated from one polycistronic mRNA**

Prokaryotic mRNAs can be polycistronic, carrying several genes; a shared promoter lets the cell turn on a whole pathway at once. E. coli has no nucleus and doesn't splice.

*Concepts linked:* Polycistronic mRNA · E. coli (Mod 1) · Real-world: the lac genes

---

**Q304. [Multiple Choice · 2점]**

A drug that blocks transport through nuclear pores is added to cultures of two of Module 1's model organisms, Saccharomyces cerevisiae and Escherichia coli. Using the diagram, what is the expected outcome?

**◇ Diagram — Where transcription and translation happen.** Gene expression in two cells. Prokaryote (no nucleus): RNA polymerase transcribes the DNA while ribosomes already translate the growing mRNA, so transcription and translation are coupled, and one mRNA can carry several genes (polycistronic). Eukaryote: in the nucleus the gene is transcribed into a primary transcript that is processed (5′ cap, splicing of introns, poly-A tail), exported through a nuclear pore, and only then translated by ribosomes in the cytoplasm.

① S. cerevisiae: new mRNA is held in the nucleus and can't be translated; E. coli: unaffected (no nucleus)　 ② Both organisms stop making proteins, since both must export their mRNA to ribosomes　 ③ E. coli stops making proteins; S. cerevisiae continues because its ribosomes are inside the nucleus　 ④ Neither is affected, because mRNA can diffuse straight through lipid membranes　 ⑤ S. cerevisiae continues, because its transcription and translation are coupled, as in bacteria

**Answer: ① S. cerevisiae: new mRNA is held in the nucleus and can't be translated; E. coli: unaffected (no nucleus)**

Eukaryotic mRNA is made and processed in the nucleus and must be actively exported through nuclear pores before cytoplasmic ribosomes can translate it. Prokaryotes transcribe and translate in the same compartment.

*Concepts linked:* Model organisms (Mod 1) · Nuclear pores · Where transcription happens

### Connecting Modules 1–6

**Q305. [보기 Combination · 3점] Is every catalyst a protein?**

Which statements in <보기> are correct?

> **(가)** Module 6: chemical reactions in cells are catalyzed by proteins called enzymes.
>
> **(나)** Module 5: the peptidyl transferase center that forms each peptide bond is made of rRNA.

**<보기>**
- ㄱ. (나) shows that at least one biological catalyst is an RNA molecule rather than a protein.
- ㄴ. Like a protein enzyme, the rRNA catalyst speeds peptide bond formation by lowering its activation energy, without changing its ΔG.
- ㄷ. Because ribosomes contain RNA, they are membrane-bound organelles of the endomembrane system (Module 1).

① ㄱ　 ② ㄴ　 ③ ㄷ　 ④ ㄱ, ㄴ　 ⑤ ㄴ, ㄷ

**Answer: ④ ㄱ, ㄴ**
- ㄱ — **O** — The catalytic heart of the ribosome is rRNA, so 'enzymes are proteins' has an important exception (catalytic RNAs are called ribozymes).
- ㄴ — **O** — Every catalyst works by lowering the barrier to the transition state; none changes the free-energy difference between reactants and products.
- ㄷ — **X** — Ribosomes are RNA–protein complexes with no membrane. They are non-EMS whether free in the cytosol or bound to the ER (Module 1).

*Concepts linked:* Ribozymes · Catalysts lower activation energy · Ribosomes are non-EMS (Module 1)

---

**Q306. [보기 Combination · 3점] One gene, two hosts**

Which statements in <보기> are correct?

> **(가)** E. coli (Module 1) has no nucleus, and its mRNAs have no 5' cap.
>
> **(나)** Human mRNAs are capped and processed in the nucleus before export (Module 4).

**<보기>**
- ㄱ. An E. coli ribosome must wait until transcription of an mRNA is complete before it can begin translating it.
- ㄴ. Antibiotics that block bacterial ribosomes should block human cytosolic ribosomes just as well, since both have A, P and E sites.
- ㄷ. A human gene placed in E. coli needs a Shine-Dalgarno-type sequence upstream of its AUG to be translated efficiently.

① ㄱ　 ② ㄴ　 ③ ㄷ　 ④ ㄱ, ㄴ　 ⑤ ㄱ, ㄷ

**Answer: ③ ㄷ**
- ㄱ — **X** — With no nuclear envelope in the way, bacterial ribosomes start on the 5' end of an mRNA while RNA polymerase is still making the rest (Module 4).
- ㄴ — **X** — Both share the same stages and sites, but bacterial ribosomes are smaller and structurally different; many antibiotics exploit those differences and spare human cytosolic ribosomes.
- ㄷ — **O** — Bacterial ribosomes find start codons by pairing with Shine-Dalgarno sequences, not caps, so a bacterial ribosome-binding sequence must be supplied.

*Concepts linked:* Translation in prokaryotes vs eukaryotes (Modules 1, 4, 5)

---

**Q307. [보기 Combination · 3점] What it costs to build a protein**

Which statements in <보기> are correct?

> **(가)** Each amino acid is attached to its tRNA by a synthetase that spends ATP.
>
> **(나)** Joining amino acids is a condensation reaction (Module 2).

**<보기>**
- ㄱ. Building a protein is anabolic: it forms new bonds between monomers and needs an energy input.
- ㄴ. Peptide bond formation is strongly exergonic on its own, which is why making proteins needs no ATP.
- ㄷ. Spending ATP to charge tRNAs couples an exergonic reaction to the endergonic job of joining amino acids, making the overall process favourable.

① ㄱ　 ② ㄴ　 ③ ㄱ, ㄷ　 ④ ㄴ, ㄷ　 ⑤ ㄱ, ㄴ, ㄷ

**Answer: ③ ㄱ, ㄷ**
- ㄱ — **O** — Assembling a polymer from monomers is anabolism, and it is endergonic overall.
- ㄴ — **X** — Joining amino acids by condensation needs energy — it's the reverse of exergonic hydrolysis (Module 2). Cells pay with the ATP spent charging tRNAs.
- ㄷ — **O** — Energy from ATP hydrolysis is stored in the bond between the amino acid and its tRNA and released when the peptide bond forms — energetic coupling.

*Concepts linked:* Anabolism · Energetic coupling · Condensation (Module 2)

---

**Q308. [보기 Combination · 3점] From frying pan to stomach**

Which statements in <보기> are correct?

> **(가)** Cooking an egg denatures its proteins (Module 2).
>
> **(나)** In the stomach, the enzyme pepsin hydrolyzes their peptide bonds, working best near pH 2.

**<보기>**
- ㄱ. Pepsin speeds digestion by making the ΔG of peptide-bond hydrolysis more negative.
- ㄴ. Denatured proteins are easier for pepsin to cut, because unfolding exposes peptide bonds that were buried inside the fold.
- ㄷ. Pepsin is itself a protein, so in the small intestine it is eventually digested too.

① ㄱ　 ② ㄴ　 ③ ㄷ　 ④ ㄴ, ㄷ　 ⑤ ㄱ, ㄴ, ㄷ

**Answer: ④ ㄴ, ㄷ**
- ㄱ — **X** — Enzymes don't change ΔG; pepsin lowers the activation energy, so a reaction that was already favourable happens fast.
- ㄴ — **O** — An unfolded chain gives the enzyme's active site access to more of the backbone — one reason cooked protein is easier to digest.
- ㄷ — **O** — Pepsin is inactivated at the intestine's higher pH, and its peptide bonds are hydrolyzed by intestinal protein-digesting enzymes.

*Concepts linked:* Denaturation (Module 2) · Enzymes lower activation energy · Digestion

---

**Q309. [보기 Combination · 3점] Supplying the mitochondria**

Which statements in <보기> are correct?

> **(가)** Mitochondria have their own DNA and bacteria-like ribosomes (Module 3).
>
> **(나)** Most mitochondrial proteins are nonetheless encoded by genes in the nucleus (Module 5).

**<보기>**
- ㄱ. Nuclear-encoded mitochondrial proteins are made on free ribosomes in the cytosol and carry an amino-terminal signal for import.
- ㄴ. A drug that blocks only mitochondrial ribosomes would stop production of proteins encoded by mitochondrial DNA but not of those encoded in the nucleus.
- ㄷ. They travel from the rough ER to the mitochondria in vesicles, since mitochondria are part of the endomembrane system.

① ㄷ　 ② ㄱ, ㄴ　 ③ ㄱ, ㄷ　 ④ ㄴ, ㄷ　 ⑤ ㄱ, ㄴ, ㄷ

**Answer: ② ㄱ, ㄴ**
- ㄱ — **O** — Proteins for mitochondria and chloroplasts are made in the cytosol and directed by an amino-terminal signal sequence.
- ㄴ — **O** — Nuclear-encoded proteins are translated by cytosolic ribosomes, which the drug doesn't affect.
- ㄷ — **X** — Mitochondria aren't part of the endomembrane system (Module 3) and don't receive vesicles from the ER; their proteins are imported directly.

*Concepts linked:* Sorting to mitochondria · Semi-autonomous organelles (Module 3)

---

**Q310. [보기 Combination · 3점] The same push from water**

Which statements in <보기> are correct?

> **(가)** Phospholipid tails cluster in the interior of the bilayer (Module 3).
>
> **(나)** Nonpolar side chains cluster in the interior of a folded protein (Module 5).

**<보기>**
- ㄱ. Both are held together by covalent bonds between neighbouring nonpolar groups.
- ㄴ. Both clusterings are driven mainly by water molecules hydrogen-bonding with one another and excluding nonpolar groups (Module 2).
- ㄷ. The membrane-spanning segment of an integral membrane protein has mostly charged side chains facing the lipid tails.

① ㄱ　 ② ㄴ　 ③ ㄷ　 ④ ㄱ, ㄴ　 ⑤ ㄱ, ㄴ, ㄷ

**Answer: ② ㄴ**
- ㄱ — **X** — No covalent bonds join the tails or the clustered side chains; the clusters can rearrange, which is why membranes are fluid.
- ㄴ — **O** — Water's network of hydrogen bonds pushes nonpolar groups together; the attraction between the nonpolar groups themselves is weak.
- ㄷ — **X** — Side chains facing the lipid tails are mostly nonpolar; charged groups there would sit in a nonpolar environment (Module 3).

*Concepts linked:* Hydrophobic effect (Module 2) · Bilayers (Module 3) · Protein folding (Module 5)

---

**Q311. [보기 Combination · 3점] An antibiotic that garbles proteins**

Which statements in <보기> are correct?

> **(가)** Streptomycin binds the small subunit of bacterial ribosomes and makes them misread codons.
>
> **(나)** In bacteria, genes are transcribed by RNA polymerase and translated by ribosomes (Module 4).

**<보기>**
- ㄱ. Streptomycin works by blocking transcription, so bacterial mRNAs are never made.
- ㄴ. Misreading puts wrong amino acids at scattered positions, producing many misfolded, nonfunctional proteins.
- ㄷ. Because codons are read in triplets, each misreading shifts the reading frame for the rest of the protein.

① ㄱ　 ② ㄴ　 ③ ㄷ　 ④ ㄴ, ㄷ　 ⑤ ㄱ, ㄴ, ㄷ

**Answer: ② ㄴ**
- ㄱ — **X** — It acts on ribosomes (translation); transcription by RNA polymerase continues.
- ㄴ — **O** — A wrong codon–anticodon match inserts the wrong amino acid; changed primary structure can derail folding across the cell's proteins.
- ㄷ — **X** — Misreading a codon substitutes one amino acid; the ribosome still moves three bases at a time, so the frame is kept.

*Concepts linked:* Ribosome accuracy · Reading frame · Transcription vs translation (Module 4)

---

**Q312. [Matching · 3점] Module 2 chemistry, Module 5–6 jobs**

Match each event to the bond or reaction (from Module 2) behind it.

**Event**
A. Joining two amino acids on the ribosome
B. Linking two cysteines in hair keratin
C. A codon pairing with an anticodon
D. ATP + H2O → ADP + Pi
E. Holding an α helix in its coil

**Bond or reaction**
1. Hydrogen bonds between backbone C=O and N–H groups
2. A disulfide bond between sulfhydryl groups
3. A peptide (amide) bond formed by condensation
4. Hydrolysis of a bond between phosphate groups
5. Hydrogen bonds between complementary bases

**Correct matches:**
- A = Joining two amino acids on the ribosome → A peptide (amide) bond formed by condensation
- B = Linking two cysteines in hair keratin → A disulfide bond between sulfhydryl groups
- C = A codon pairing with an anticodon → Hydrogen bonds between complementary bases
- D = ATP + H2O → ADP + Pi → Hydrolysis of a bond between phosphate groups
- E = Holding an α helix in its coil → Hydrogen bonds between backbone C=O and N–H groups

The same few bond types do every job: condensation builds peptide bonds; –SH groups join as disulfides; complementary bases hydrogen-bond in nucleic acids; hydrolysis splits ATP's phosphate bonds; and backbone C=O and N–H groups hydrogen-bond in secondary structure.

*Concepts linked:* Bond types (Module 2) across Modules 5–6

---

**Q313. [Matching · 3점] Where in the cell?**

Match each event to where it happens in a human cell.

**Event**
A. Adding a 5' cap to a primary transcript
B. Charging tRNAs with their amino acids
C. Translating an mRNA for insulin once SRP has docked
D. Cutting off the signal sequence of a secreted protein
E. Making the proteins encoded by mitochondrial DNA

**Location**
1. Ribosomes on the rough ER
2. Mitochondrial matrix
3. Nucleus
4. Cytosol
5. ER lumen side of the ER membrane

**Correct matches:**
- A = Adding a 5' cap to a primary transcript → Nucleus
- B = Charging tRNAs with their amino acids → Cytosol
- C = Translating an mRNA for insulin once SRP has docked → Ribosomes on the rough ER
- D = Cutting off the signal sequence of a secreted protein → ER lumen side of the ER membrane
- E = Making the proteins encoded by mitochondrial DNA → Mitochondrial matrix

RNA processing happens in the nucleus; tRNA charging and the start of all translation happen in the cytosol; SRP moves a secretory protein's translation to the rough ER, where the signal sequence is removed on the lumen side; mitochondria translate their own few genes on their own ribosomes in the matrix (Module 3).

*Concepts linked:* Where each step of gene expression happens (Modules 1, 3, 4, 5)

---

**Q314. [True / False · 1점]**

> A 100-amino-acid protein is digested completely into free amino acids in the small intestine.

**Claim:** The digestion uses up 99 water molecules, because each peptide bond is broken by hydrolysis — the reverse of the dehydration that formed it (Module 2).

**Answer: True**

100 amino acids are held by 99 peptide bonds. Each was made by releasing one H2O, and each hydrolysis consumes one H2O. Digestion is catabolism: breaking a polymer into its monomers.

*Concepts linked:* Peptide bonds and hydrolysis (Module 2) · Catabolism

---

**Q315. [True / False · 2점]**

> Stomach acid has a pH of about 2. At low pH, ionizable groups pick up H+ (Module 2).

**Claim:** At pH 2, a free alanine molecule carries a net positive charge, because its carboxyl group is mostly protonated (–COOH) while its amino group stays –NH3+.

**Answer: True**

At physiological pH alanine carries H3N+ and COO- (no net charge). Lowering the pH adds H+ to the carboxylate and removes its negative charge, so mainly the +1 on the amino group remains.

*Concepts linked:* Amino acid charges · pH and ionization (Module 2)

---

**Q316. [True / False · 1점]**

> A folded protein is held together by many hydrogen bonds, ionic bonds and van der Waals contacts, plus a few disulfide bonds.

**Claim:** Because each noncovalent contact is weak, together they contribute little; the disulfide bonds do almost all the work of holding the shape.

**Answer: False**

Each weak interaction is far weaker than a covalent bond (Module 2), but a folded protein has hundreds of them, and together they hold the fold. Many proteins have no disulfide bonds at all.

*Concepts linked:* Weak bonds add up (Module 2) · Tertiary interactions

---

**Q317. [True / False · 1점]**

> Viruses carry genes but have no ribosomes and no metabolism of their own (Module 1).

**Claim:** Given only its genome and a supply of free amino acids in a test tube, a virus could make its own proteins.

**Answer: False**

Translation needs ribosomes, tRNAs, aminoacyl tRNA synthetases, initiation, elongation and release factors, and an energy supply (ATP) — all provided by a host cell. That's why viruses reproduce only inside cells.

*Concepts linked:* Components of translation · Viruses aren't cells (Module 1)

---

**Q318. [True / False · 2점]**

> Euglena, a single-celled protist (Module 1), photosynthesizes in the light; kept in the dark, it absorbs organic molecules from the water for both energy and carbon.

**◇ Diagram — Sorting organisms by energy source and carbon source.** A 2 by 2 grid. The two columns are headed energy from light and energy from chemical compounds; the two rows are headed carbon from CO₂ and carbon from organic molecules. Each cell holds only a letter: A (light, CO₂), B (chemical compounds, CO₂), C (light, organic molecules) and D (chemical compounds, organic molecules).

**Claim:** Euglena moves between cells A and D of the diagram, depending on the light.

**Answer: True**

In light: light energy + carbon from CO2 → A (photoautotroph). In the dark: chemical energy + organic carbon → D (chemoheterotroph). Protists vary in nutrition, and some, like Euglena, switch (Module 1).

*Concepts linked:* Metabolic classes · Protist nutrition (Module 1)

---

**Q319. [True / False · 1점]**

> Enzymes called kinases switch many proteins on or off by attaching a phosphate group to the –OH of a serine side chain.

**Claim:** This adds negative charge where a neutral polar side chain used to be, which can change the protein's shape or what it binds.

**Answer: True**

A phosphate group is ionized at physiological pH (Module 2), so phosphorylation turns a polar, uncharged side chain into a negatively charged one. New ionic attractions and repulsions can reshape the protein — a post-translational switch.

*Concepts linked:* Post-translational modification · Phosphate groups are charged (Module 2)

---

**Q320. [True / False · 1점]**

> A tRNA is a single RNA strand of 70–90 nucleotides folded into a cloverleaf.

**Claim:** Its cloverleaf is held together by base pairs between two separate RNA strands, as in double-stranded DNA.

**Answer: False**

One strand folds back on itself: complementary stretches within the same molecule pair (A–U, G–C) to form the stems, leaving loops such as the anticodon loop.

*Concepts linked:* tRNA structure · Base pairing (Modules 2, 4)

---

**Q321. [True / False · 1점]**

> Histones (Module 4) are made on free ribosomes in the cytosol, but they work in the nucleus.

**Claim:** They reach the nucleus in vesicles budding from the rough ER, like proteins headed for secretion.

**Answer: False**

Histones carry an internal signal sequence that directs them into the nucleus through the nuclear pores, which actively transport large proteins (Module 3). The ER and vesicle route serves secreted and endomembrane proteins; it doesn't deliver proteins into the nucleus.

*Concepts linked:* Internal signal → nucleus · Nuclear pores (Module 3)

---

**Q322. [True / False · 1점]**

> During a high fever, body temperature rises from 37°C to about 41°C.

**Claim:** Since molecules move faster at higher temperature, a fever can only speed up the body's enzymes.

**Answer: False**

Up to a point, more kinetic energy speeds reactions. But extra heat also disrupts the weak hydrogen bonds and other interactions that hold an enzyme's fold (Module 2); some enzymes start to unfold and lose activity at high temperatures.

*Concepts linked:* Temperature, kinetic energy and protein folding (Modules 2, 5, 6)

---

**Q323. [True / False · 1점]**

> Mature human red blood cells have lost their nucleus and their ribosomes.

**Claim:** They therefore can't replace damaged hemoglobin, since making new protein requires ribosomes to translate mRNA.

**Answer: True**

No ribosomes means no translation (and no nucleus means no new mRNA). A red blood cell works with the hemoglobin it was built with and is replaced after about 120 days.

*Concepts linked:* Ribosomes are required for translation · Cell structure and function (Module 1)

---

**Q324. [True / False · 2점]**

> RNA polymerase adds each nucleotide using energy from the incoming nucleoside triphosphate, releasing pyrophosphate (Module 4).

**Claim:** This is energetic coupling: splitting off pyrophosphate is exergonic and drives the endergonic formation of a phosphodiester bond.

**Answer: True**

Forming a phosphodiester bond by condensation needs energy; breaking the phosphate–phosphate bond of the incoming triphosphate releases it. The two steps are linked, so the net ΔG is negative — the same strategy ATP-driven reactions use.

*Concepts linked:* Energetic coupling · Nucleotide polymerization (Module 4)

---

**Q325. [True / False · 1점]**

> When water vapour condenses into liquid, hydrogen bonds form between water molecules (Module 2).

**Claim:** Forming these bonds requires an input of energy, so condensation absorbs heat from the surroundings.

**Answer: False**

Forming bonds releases energy; breaking them requires it. Condensing vapour releases heat (which is why steam burns are so severe), just as forming stronger bonds in a reaction's products releases energy.

*Concepts linked:* Bond formation releases energy · Hydrogen bonds in water (Module 2)

---

**Q326. [True / False · 1점]**

> In a polypeptide backbone, the O of each C=O and the N of each N–H are more electronegative than the atoms they're bonded to (Module 2).

**Claim:** This polarity is what allows a C=O of one residue and an N–H of another to form the hydrogen bonds of helices and sheets.

**Answer: True**

Electronegative O and N pull electrons toward themselves, giving the O a partial negative charge and the H on N a partial positive charge — the ingredients of a hydrogen bond (Module 2). Secondary structure is built from these backbone H-bonds.

*Concepts linked:* Electronegativity and H-bonds (Module 2) · Secondary structure

---

**Q327. [True / False · 1점]**

> Module 1 lists acquiring and using energy as a property of every cell, including plant cells.

**Claim:** Even a photoautotroph's cells need ATP, because light cannot directly power processes such as protein synthesis.

**Answer: True**

Light energy is first captured as chemical energy (ATP and sugars). ATP is the currency that actually drives cellular work everywhere in the plant, including root cells that never see light.

*Concepts linked:* ATP as universal currency · Cells acquire and use energy (Module 1)

---

**Q328. [Multiple Choice · 2점]**

The Na+/K+ pump (Module 3) uses about a third of a resting cell's ATP. In Module 6 terms, what is it doing with that energy?

① Coupling ATP hydrolysis to pushing ions up their gradients　 ② Using the kinetic energy of moving ions to make new ATP　 ③ Lowering the activation energy of diffusion, the way an enzyme would　 ④ Turning all of ATP's energy into ion movement, with no heat lost　 ⑤ Running an exergonic process that needs no energy input

**Answer: ① Coupling ATP hydrolysis to pushing ions up their gradients**

Moving ions against their gradients is endergonic (it builds potential energy); ATP hydrolysis is exergonic. The pump couples the two so the net ΔG is negative. Some energy is always lost as heat (second law).

*Concepts linked:* Energetic coupling · Active transport (Module 3)

---

**Q329. [Multiple Choice · 2점]**

In sickle-cell disease, codon 6 of the β-globin gene changes from GAG to GTG on the nontemplate strand. Using the genetic code table, what happens to the protein?

**◇ Diagram — The standard genetic code (mRNA codons, read 5′→3′).** A 4 × 4 table of the 64 mRNA codons: row blocks by 1st base (U, C, A, G, down the left), columns by 2nd base (U, C, A, G, across the top), and the 3rd base (U, C, A, G) listed down the right of every row block; each codon is followed by a three-letter amino-acid abbreviation or Stop, the three Stop entries are tinted, and AUG Met carries a small start tag. Entries by row block (2nd base U, C, A, G in turn): 1st base U: UUU Phe, UUC Phe, UUA Leu, UUG Leu, UCU Ser, UCC Ser, UCA Ser, UCG Ser, UAU Tyr, UAC Tyr, UAA Stop, UAG Stop, UGU Cys, UGC Cys, UGA Stop, UGG Trp; 1st base C: CUU Leu, CUC Leu, CUA Leu, CUG Leu, CCU Pro, CCC Pro, CCA Pro, CCG Pro, CAU His, CAC His, CAA Gln, CAG Gln, CGU Arg, CGC Arg, CGA Arg, CGG Arg; 1st base A: AUU Ile, AUC Ile, AUA Ile, AUG Met, ACU Thr, ACC Thr, ACA Thr, ACG Thr, AAU Asn, AAC Asn, AAA Lys, AAG Lys, AGU Ser, AGC Ser, AGA Arg, AGG Arg; 1st base G: GUU Val, GUC Val, GUA Val, GUG Val, GCU Ala, GCC Ala, GCA Ala, GCG Ala, GAU Asp, GAC Asp, GAA Glu, GAG Glu, GGU Gly, GGC Gly, GGA Gly, GGG Gly.

① mRNA codon CUC becomes CAC, so His replaces Leu　 ② mRNA codon GAG becomes GTG, which ribosomes can't read　 ③ The reading frame shifts, changing every later amino acid　 ④ mRNA codon GAG becomes GUG, so Val replaces Glu　 ⑤ Nothing, since the genetic code is redundant

**Answer: ④ mRNA codon GAG becomes GUG, so Val replaces Glu**

The mRNA matches the nontemplate strand with U for T (Module 4): GAG (Glu) → GUG (Val). A single substitution changes one codon and one amino acid without shifting the frame, and it isn't silent, because GUG codes for a different amino acid.

*Concepts linked:* Nontemplate strand → mRNA (Module 4) · Reading the code · Real-world: sickle-cell disease

---

**Q330. [Multiple Choice · 2점]**

Two patients have deletions inside the coding region of the dystrophin gene. Patient A loses 6 nucleotides and has mild muscle weakness (Becker muscular dystrophy); patient B loses 5 nucleotides and has severe disease (Duchenne). Which explanation fits best?

① A's deletion removes two whole codons, keeping the frame; B's shifts the frame downstream　 ② B's deletion removes more amino acids than A's, so B's protein is shorter but still in frame　 ③ Deleting 6 nucleotides changes the start codon, while deleting 5 nucleotides leaves it intact　 ④ A's deletion is silent because the genetic code is redundant, but B's deletion is not silent　 ⑤ Deleting 5 nucleotides destroys the mRNA's 5' cap, so no ribosome can start translating it at all

**Answer: ① A's deletion removes two whole codons, keeping the frame; B's shifts the frame downstream**

A DNA deletion is copied into the mRNA (Module 4). Six nucleotides are two whole codons, so the frame is kept and the protein just lacks two amino acids. Five is not a multiple of three: every codon after the deletion is misread, and a premature stop codon soon ends the chain, usually leaving a nonfunctional protein.

*Concepts linked:* Reading frame · Nonoverlapping triplets · Central dogma (Module 4) · Real-world: muscular dystrophy

---

**Q331. [Multiple Choice · 2점]**

Why must a cell keep spending ATP to maintain its Na+ gradient, even when nothing else changes?

① The pump uses up Na+ ions, so the cell must keep making new ones to pump　 ② ATP keeps the bilayer fluid, and the gradient depends on fluidity　 ③ The gradient stores no energy, so it has to be paid for again　 ④ Na+ leaks back down its gradient, so the pump must keep restoring it　 ⑤ The pump works only while an enzyme lowers its ΔG below zero

**Answer: ④ Na+ leaks back down its gradient, so the pump must keep restoring it**

A gradient is an ordered, high-energy state, and spontaneous change runs toward lower energy and higher entropy (second law): ions leak back through channels. Keeping the gradient means continually paying with ATP hydrolysis (Module 3).

*Concepts linked:* Second law · Gradients store potential energy (Module 3)
